# Develop on Linux

## Code Quality Tools

The repository uses clang-format and clang-tidy for C/C++, Ruff for Python, and ShellCheck and shfmt for Bash.

On macOS with Homebrew:

```sh
brew install llvm ruff shellcheck shfmt pre-commit
brew install ninja
export PATH="$(brew --prefix llvm)/bin:$PATH"
```

On Ubuntu:

```sh
sudo apt-get install clang-format clang-tidy shellcheck shfmt pre-commit pipx ninja-build
pipx install ruff
```

Run `bash tools/quality.sh --fix` to apply available formatting and lint fixes, then install the pre-commit hook with `pre-commit install` once per clone. Each check is its own hook (clang-format, clang-tidy per architecture, the filesystem core's include check, Ruff, ShellCheck, shfmt) that runs only when the commit touches files it covers, prints its duration, and prints diagnostics on failure. The clang-tidy hooks check only the sources the staged files affect: the staged sources and every source whose last build included a staged header, from Ninja's dependency log (`ninja -t deps`); a staged `.clang-tidy` or CMake file, or a missing log, checks everything. `bash tools/quality.sh check` runs every stage with one timed line each; `bash tools/quality.sh check clang-tidy-x86_64` runs one (`bash tools/quality.sh stages` lists them). Build at least one architecture first so clang-tidy can use `build/<arch>/compile_commands.json`; issues without automatic fixes are reported for manual repair.

The `fs-core` stage keeps `src/fs/core/` ready to leave the repository: every source, CMake file and script there must start with `// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT OR Apache-2.0` (`#` in CMake and scripts), every quoted include must name a file inside the folder (without `..`), and the library's angle-bracket includes must be one of `<cstdint>`, `<cstddef>`, `<array>`, `<span>`, `<string_view>`, `<optional>`, `<expected>`, `<bit>`, `<type_traits>` and `<utility>`. Only `tests/`, `host/` and `tools/`, which run on a host, may include other standard and system headers and Catch2. clang-tidy checks the core's library through the kernel builds, which compile it; its tests, `host/` and `tools/` are not in a kernel's compile database and, like `tests/unit/`, are not tidied.

### clang-tidy checks

`.clang-tidy` turns every warning into an error, so a commit with one fails. It enables `bugprone-*`, `clang-analyzer-*` and `performance-*`, minus the checks that flag what a kernel and its C library do on purpose: integers made into pointers for MMIO and physical memory (`performance-no-int-to-ptr`), enum widths set by hardware and the ABI (`performance-enum-size`), flag enums that combine bits (`EnumCastOutOfRange`), layouts that follow hardware (`Padding`), libc's own `strcpy` and `malloc(0)`, and case-by-case `if` ladders (`bugprone-branch-clone`). Multiplications of constants are not flagged as widening; any other `uint32_t` product that becomes a 64-bit address or offset is computed in 64 bits (`static_cast<uint64_t>(index) * PageSize`), since sector, page and cluster numbers are where 32-bit overflow hides. A finding the analyzer gets wrong takes a `NOLINT(<check>)` with the reason on the line above; `libctest` misuses functions on purpose and turns those checks off for the whole file.

### Lock annotations

clang-tidy runs Clang's thread-safety analysis (`-Wthread-safety`, passed through `ExtraArgsBefore`; GCC builds the code and ignores the annotations). The macros are in `src/user/thread_safety.h`, shared by the kernel and libc:

- `Sync::SpinLock`, `Sync::Mutex` and `Sync::VolumeMutex` in the kernel, and `LibC::Lock`/`RecursiveLock` in libc, are capabilities; their guards (`SpinLockGuard`, `MutexGuard`, `LibC::Guard`) take and release them for a scope.
- Data a lock protects is declared `GUARDED_BY(lock)` (`PT_GUARDED_BY` for what a pointer points at), with the lock declared first. Reading or writing it without the lock is an error.
- A function that expects its caller to hold a lock says `REQUIRES(lock)`; by convention its name ends in `Locked`. `Scheduler::WaitOn` requires the lock it sleeps on.
- `NO_THREAD_SAFETY_ANALYSIS`, with a comment saying why, marks the few places that are right but cannot be expressed: the lock primitives themselves, a panic breaking the serial lock, `Files::Quiesce` holding the namespace lock, every volume lock and the table lock until shutdown, objects set up before any other CPU can see them or freed after none can, a process reading its own group and session, and stdio's `flockfile` family.
- Data that each CPU writes for itself and others read, or that is set once at boot, is read and written with `__atomic` builtins instead of a lock, and says so where it is declared.
- `Sync::VolumeMutex` is a `Mutex` that records its owner (`HeldByCurrentTask`); a task holds at most one volume lock (`Scheduler::CurrentLocal().held_volume`), except the one that quiesces every mount (`LockWithOthers`), and taking a second panics `VFS: two volume locks`. Planned order for the per-volume locking of M30 Part 5 (design `docs/design/M30-P5.md`, D15): the namespace mutex, one volume lock, the descriptor-table lock, `mount_lock`, then leaf spinlocks; nothing takes an earlier one while holding a later one.

New shared state gets a lock and `GUARDED_BY` when it is added.

## Prerequisite for the GCC + Binutils

- sudo apt-get install libgmp3-dev
- sudo apt-get install libmpc-dev

## Prerequisite for Running

On macOS:

```sh
brew install xorriso qemu mtools e2fsprogs
```

Homebrew installs `e2fsprogs` keg-only, outside `PATH`; the core's CMake looks for `mke2fs` and `debugfs` in `/opt/homebrew/opt/e2fsprogs/sbin` and `/usr/local/opt/e2fsprogs/sbin` first, then on `PATH`. The core's tests use them for its ext2 images and skip those tests without them. `tools/create_test_disk.py` needs `mke2fs` for every QEMU run, since the test disk has ext2, ext3 and ext4 partitions; it looks in the same Homebrew directories, then on `PATH`, `/usr/sbin` and `/sbin`, and stops with an install hint when it finds none.

Install Ninja and CMake 3.20 or newer as well; kernel and unit-test CMake builds use the Ninja generator. GNU Make is still used to build Limine. Every target, the kernel, libc, the programs and the host tests, is C++ built as `gnu++23`.

- sudo apt-get install -y xorriso
- sudo apt-get install -y qemu-system-x86-64
- sudo apt-get install -y qemu-system-arm
- sudo apt-get install -y mtools
- sudo apt-get install -y e2fsprogs
- sudo apt-get install -y python3

Swift programs (optional) need a swift.org toolchain, installed with [swiftly](https://www.swift.org/install/). `swiftly install` in the checkout installs the version `.swift-version` pins, and `build.sh` asks swiftly for its location, or uses `SWIFTC=/path/to/swiftc` when set. Xcode's `swiftc` is not used, since it does not reliably target ELF. Without a usable toolchain the build says so and skips the Swift programs, and the shell smoke test skips their checks. With one, `build.sh` also fetches the Swift and libc++ sources that the concurrency runtime is built from, and that build needs `python3` (see "Swift concurrency").

## Boot Menu And USB Stick

Limine's menu is hidden by default (`timeout: 0` in `configs/<arch>/limine.conf`). `LIMINE_TIMEOUT=N ./build.sh --arch x86_64 iso` writes a copy of the config with `timeout: N` into the ISO, so the menu shows for N seconds and `e` edits the kernel command line (`usb=poll`, `usb=off`, `ps2=off`, `usbhid=report`, `root=vdaN`, `storage=ro|rw`); the number must be a non-negative integer.

`bash tools/make_usb_stick.sh [image]` builds `build/x86_64/featheros-usb.img` from the artifacts of `./build.sh --arch x86_64 build`: a GPT disk (protective MBR, both headers with CRCs, written by an inline python3 script) with one FAT32 EFI system partition at 1 MiB, made with mtools (`mformat`, `mmd`, `mcopy`; the partition is sized from the kernel and initramfs, at least 64 MiB, about 100 MiB in a Debug build) holding `EFI/BOOT/BOOTX64.EFI` (Limine's), `limine.conf` (the x86-64 config with `LIMINE_TIMEOUT`, default 5 here, and `KERNEL_CMDLINE` as `cmdline:`), `kernel.elf` and `initramfs.tar`. Write it with `dd` to a stick (`docs/hardware.md`, which also has the laptop checklist and the QEMU command that rehearses the stick under OVMF).

## Architecture Builds

An architecture must be specified for `build`, `iso`, `run`, `test`, `debug`, and `all`; there is no implicit architecture default. Select it with `--arch <x86_64|aarch64>` or the `ARCH` environment variable. The command-line option takes precedence if both are supplied.

```sh
./build.sh --arch x86_64 test
ARCH=x86_64 ./build.sh test
./build.sh test-all
```

`BUILD_TYPE` chooses how the kernel, the C library and the programs are compiled:
- **Debug** (the default) builds at `-O0 -g`, and Swift at `-Onone` with `-g` for the programs. The `FeatherSupport` module is built without `-g`, because swiftc cannot combine `-g` with `-emit-module` for ELF, and the programs compile in its code anyway.
- **Release** builds at `-O2`, and Swift at `-O`. It keeps `assert`, which the tests rely on.

`build/<arch>/programs` keeps each program with its debug information and symbols for gdb. The copies in the initramfs are stripped with `--strip-all`, which keeps the initramfs small: debug information alone is most of a program's file, and Embedded Swift's mangled names take about a quarter of what is left of a Swift program (`hello` at `-Onone` is 1.2 MB with symbols and 0.9 MB without). Each build starts with `cmake --fresh`, so switching types rebuilds everything:

```sh
BUILD_TYPE=Release ./build.sh --arch aarch64 test
```

Optimization brought two fixes:
- GCC turns the loops inside `memset` and `memcpy` into calls to themselves. `src/kernel/string/memory_functions.cpp` and `src/libc/string.cpp` turn that off with `#pragma GCC optimize("no-tree-loop-distribute-patterns")`. It is a pragma rather than a flag, because clang-tidy reads the compile commands and does not know the flag.
- AArch64 shifts `unsigned __int128` values right through libgcc's `__lshrti3`, which the C library provides in `src/libc/compiler_runtime.cpp`.

## Smoke Test

Run `./build.sh --arch x86_64 test` to configure and build the kernel, create the bootable ISO, and boot it in QEMU without a display. The test passes when the kernel emits its ready message over serial without reporting a panic. Debug builds and their compile databases go under `build/x86_64` and `build/aarch64`, Release builds under `build/<arch>-release`, so switching the build type does not rebuild everything. `build` also makes what every image of a build shares (the initramfs, Limine's tool and AArch64's device tree); `iso` then packs the ISO and a fresh disk image into `OUT_DIR` (the build folder by default), and `smoke` runs `iso` and the smoke test without building.

The kernel test hooks (`PANIC_TEST`, `EXCEPTION_TEST`, `INTERRUPT_TEST`, `MEMORY_TEST`, `TIMER_TEST`, `SCHEDULER_TEST`, `USER_PREEMPTION_TEST`, `SMP_TEST`, `STORAGE_TEST`, `NETWORK_TEST`, `INPUT_TEST`, `PCI_TEST`, `USB_STORAGE_TEST`, `NVME_TEST`, `AHCI_TEST`, `STORAGE_POLICY_TEST`) are built only with `KERNEL_TESTS=ON` (CMake `FEATHEROS_KERNEL_TESTS`, off by default), which compiles `src/kernel/kernel_test.cpp` and `kernel_test_mode.cpp` and defines `FEATHEROS_KERNEL_TESTS` for the kernel; everything else the hooks need is inside `#if defined(FEATHEROS_KERNEL_TESTS)` (the memory test's fault recovery in the exception handlers, x86-64's divide-error stack, `Console::WriteSerialLine`, the user-preemption and per-mode calls in `kernel_runtime.cpp`, and the `test=` parsing in `kmain`), so an ordinary kernel carries none of it (about 23 KB of code and, on x86-64, 16 KiB of stack per CPU less) and ignores `test=`. `test`, `smoke`, `test-all`, `tools/check.sh` and any of the toggles below turn it on. In a kernel built with them, the hooks are chosen at boot: `iso` adds `cmdline: test=<mode>` (`panic`, `exception`, `interrupt`, `memory`, `timer`, `scheduler`, `user-preemption`, `smp`, `storage`, `network`, `input` or `pci`) to the image's `limine.conf`, the kernel reads it through Limine's kernel-file request before it sets up exceptions (`KernelTest::ParseCommandLine` in `src/kernel/kernel_test_mode.cpp`, host-tested), and an unknown mode panics; `iso` refuses a `test=` image of a build without the hooks. One build can therefore run any of them, and at most one toggle may be on. `KERNEL_STORAGE=ro|rw` adds `storage=ro` or `storage=rw` to it, in any run (`BootOptions::ParseStorage`, host-tested, the last `storage=` wins and any other value counts as absent; `tools/matrix.py` clears it like the other `KERNEL_*` variables): it is the write policy of NVMe and AHCI disks (M16), decided per controller by `Storage::DecideWrites` (`src/kernel/storage/write_policy.cpp`): `ro` makes every such disk read-only, `rw` makes every one writable, and without it only QEMU's controllers (PCI subsystem 1AF4:1100) are writable and real hardware is read-only. No controller driver exists yet, so the option has no visible effect before step B3. `KERNEL_ROOT=vdaN` adds `root=vdaN` to the same `cmdline:` line, in any run (it is not a test hook and works without `KERNEL_TESTS`), and the kernel parses it before the heap exists (`BootOptions::ParseRoot` in `src/kernel/boot_options.cpp`, host-tested; the kernel-file request is therefore always present, and `KernelTest::ParseCommandLine` shares its word splitting): it names the partition that holds the root, whichever of FAT16, ext2, ext3 and ext4 it has; without it the root is the lowest-numbered partition of those four. The grammar is a disk name and a partition number from 1 to 9999 with no leading zero: `vd<letter>`, `sd<letter>` or `nvme<c>n<ns>p` (`root=vda3`, `root=sda2`, `root=nvme0n1p2`; the last `root=` wins). The root is chosen after `Storage::WaitForControllers`, so `root=sda2` and `root=nvme0n1p2` name a disk that the NVMe and AHCI probes registered (the smoke cases for them are M16 step D3); a name matching no registered disk logs `KERN/W: VFS: root=sda2: no disk sda, using the default root`, and without `root=` the root is on `vda` whatever other disks exist. A `root=` that is malformed or names no usable partition logs `KERN/W: VFS: root=vda9 is not a usable partition, using the default root` (or `root= is not a partition such as vda1 or nvme0n1p2, ...`) and the default rule applies. `ROOT_TEST=ON` (`--expect-root`, `tools/smoke/cases/root.py`) is the smoke mode for it: with `KERNEL_ROOT=vda3` or `vda6` (default `vda3`) it boots the ext3 or ext2 partition of the test disk as the root, unchanged (it has no `BLOCK.TXT`, `/home`, `/bin` or `/sbin`, so the kernel logs `Init: starting /system/init from the initramfs`, reads no file from the root, and the shell starts in `/`), checks `pwd`, writes `/rootwrite.txt` from the shell, runs `shutdown`, and checks a copy with `e2fsck -fn` and `debugfs` and that the journal was closed (ext3) or the volume marked clean (ext2); `KERNEL_ROOT=vda1` first makes the disk copy's root partition a new ext4 volume with `mke2fs` (only `lost+found`, then a garbage `/sbin/init` and two `/etc/init.d` files, one that does not parse and one that runs, written with `debugfs`, so the kernel must fall back to `/system/init` and init must skip the broken file and run the other beside the built-in services) and checks the same; any other name, such as `KERNEL_ROOT=vda9`, expects the warning and the normal boot with the default root.

`./build.sh test-all` runs `tools/matrix.py`: 100 cases across x86-64 and AArch64, covering panic and exception reporting, normal startup, interrupts, memory, timers, cooperative scheduling, user-mode preemption, multi-CPU scheduling, disk-write persistence across two boots on MBR and GPT disks, `shutdown` (QEMU must exit by itself) and `reboot` (the system must boot again; `POWER_TEST=shutdown` or `POWER_TEST=reboot`), an ext3 root, an ext2 root and a freshly made empty ext4 root chosen with `KERNEL_ROOT=vda3`, `vda6` and `vda1`, and a `KERNEL_ROOT=vda9` that falls back (`ROOT_TEST=ON`; the default root is the disk's ext4 `vda1`), `fbdemo`'s pixels and a played `featherfall` checked against QEMU's `screendump` and QMP keys (`GRAPHICS_TEST=ON`), `wickdeep`'s self-test and demo runs (`DUNGEON_TEST=ON`), UTF-8 text on the console checked cell by cell (`FONTS_TEST=ON`), the desktop's test card checked byte for byte against the host renderer (`PIXELS_TEST=ON`), shell sessions on QEMU's `max` CPU with hardware protections enabled, UART input, window-keyboard input, and shell sessions that run user programs, in Debug and once more in Release. Both keyboard cases inject keys through QEMU's QMP interface. The runner builds each architecture and build type the cases need once, then boots the cases three at a time (`-j N` or `MATRIX_JOBS`), the longest first, each with its own ISO, disk image, log (`log.txt`) and session (`smoke-output.txt`) in `build/matrix/<case>/`; `-k <text>` runs only the cases whose name contains it, and `--no-build` reuses the builds. It prints one line per build and case, a failure's `FAIL` lines with its log and session paths, and `OK` or `FAILED` at the end. QEMU is stopped as soon as the expected record appears, with a timeout as a fallback.

`tools/smoke_test.py` is the harness's entry point; the harness itself is the package `tools/smoke/`. `session.py` runs QEMU and types a case's script, a whole line per prompt (`script.py` has the items besides plain lines: `TypeWhen` and `WaitFor` (match the whole session so far; `TypeWhen(..., fresh=True)` waits for a copy printed after the line before it, as `top`'s Ctrl+Z does, since a key typed before `top` is raw would be echoed as `^Z`), `WaitUntil`, and Ctrl+C or Ctrl+Z for a running command, sent 0.5 s after the shell echoed the command's newline), `qemu.py` builds the command line and talks QMP, and `hosts/` has the servers and clients the network case starts on the host. Each mode is a file in `cases/` with a `Case` subclass (`cases/base.py`: its timeout, extra QEMU arguments, script, end markers, a hook on new output, and `Check`, which returns a failure message), and `cases/__init__.py` maps each `--expect-<mode>` switch to its case. The shell mode types the parts in `cases/shell/`, one per feature, in the order `PARTS` lists them; each has its input, the texts its output must contain, an optional check, the program the build may skip it for, and how many `^C` and `^Z` it makes the terminal echo.

## Unit Tests

Run `./build.sh unit-test` for native host tests of USTAR, FAT16 reads and writes, VFS, ELF loading, terminal rendering, syscall dispatch, program-argument parsing, calendar conversion, file-syscall error codes and `Stat`, Spawn environment validation, init configuration (`env` lines, the merge of built-in, `/etc/init.d` and disabled services), `Seek` origins, and the C library's integer parsing, sort, environment table, allocator, `printf` and `scanf` engines, `strftime`, and `posix_spawn` requests. CMake fetches the pinned Catch2 `v3.7.1` release on the first run, then CTest runs the cases from `build/unit-tests-ninja`, one per CPU at a time (`CTEST_PARALLEL_LEVEL` sets another number; the cases keep their scratch files apart, and the long crash tests are one case per image so they run side by side). With a Swift toolchain, `unit-test` also runs the host tests of the Swift programs' parsers (see "Swift programs"). These tests use an in-memory block device; QEMU remains responsible for exercising real PCI/MMIO and boot integration.

`unit-test` also adds the shared filesystem core (`src/fs/core/CMakeLists.txt`), which builds its host library, `host/standard_host.cpp` and its own Catch2 tests from `src/fs/core/tests/` (CTest names start with `fs.core:`), using the Catch2 target `tests/unit` passes in. The FAT16 tests run over volumes built in memory and over an image `src/fs/core/tests/make_fat16_image.sh` makes with mtools at build time; the core reads that image, writes to it, and `mtype` and `mdir` must read the changes back. Without mtools the image is not made and those two tests are skipped. The ext2 tests read images that `src/fs/core/tests/make_ext2_image.sh` makes at build time with `mke2fs -d` and `debugfs` from e2fsprogs (see "Shared filesystem core"); without them those tests are skipped too. The ext2 write tests change copies of those images and require `e2fsck -fn` to exit 0 on each afterwards; CMake finds `e2fsck` beside `mke2fs` (Homebrew's keg, then `PATH`), and without it they run without that check. The second core target `featherdisk_core_tests_page_cache` (CTest `fs.core.page-cache:*`) reruns the ext suites with every mount over a `CachedDevice`, which doubles the run, so `unit-test` skips it unless `FULL_UNIT_TESTS=1` is set; `tools/check.sh --matrix` sets it. Configured on its own, the core fetches the same Catch2 release itself: `cmake -S src/fs/core -B build/fs-core -G Ninja && cmake --build build/fs-core && ctest --test-dir build/fs-core`.

The tests and Catch2 are built with AddressSanitizer and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer (`-fsanitize=address,undefined -fno-sanitize-recover=all`), so an out-of-bounds access, use after free or undefined behaviour fails the test that hits it. `SANITIZE=OFF ./build.sh unit-test` builds them without. `./build.sh unit-test-tsan` builds them again in `build/unit-tsan` with ThreadSanitizer (`FEATHEROS_THREAD_SANITIZER`, which excludes ASan; Homebrew LLVM on macOS) and runs only the `[concurrency]` cases, the ones that start real threads (the `VolumeMutex` and filesystem concurrency tests, and the core's `featherdisk_core_tests` page cache test); the scheduler stubs in `tests/unit/support/` are thread-safe for them. On macOS the sanitized build uses Homebrew LLVM (`brew install llvm`, which `tools/quality.sh` also needs): Apple clang 17's AddressSanitizer runtime deadlocks before `main` on macOS 26. Leak checking is not available on macOS arm64.

## Initramfs

The ISO build packages `initramfs/hello.txt` as `build/<arch>/initramfs.tar` and adds it as a Limine module on both architectures. The kernel mounts the read-only USTAR archive; the shared VFS supports open, read, seek, stat, and close over it. The build also creates `build/<arch>/virtio-disk.img`, a 166 MiB disk with six partitions, four of them primaries (`tools/create_test_disk.py`). The first, `vda1` from sector 2048 to 128 MiB, is the root (127 MiB, which holds every program of a Debug build, about 75 MB): an ext4 volume with mke2fs's default features (`-t ext4 -b 1024 -I 256`: extents, 64bit, flex_bg, metadata_csum and its seed, a 1 MiB journal (`-J size=1`), orphan_file; label `FEATHEROS`) made with `mke2fs -d` from a staging directory holding `BLOCK.TXT` (a copy of `initramfs/block.txt`, which the `STORAGE` test reads), an empty `home` directory (the shell starts in `/home`), `bin` with every program of the build (stripped, mode `0755`, the administrative ones excepted) and `sbin` with `init`, `mount`, `umount`, `sync`, `net` and `dhcp` (`create_test_disk.py --programs build/<arch>/disk-programs`, which `build.sh` fills while it strips the programs into the initramfs's `/system`; the aarch64 device-tree disk gets none), `PATH` being `/bin:/sbin:/system` and `samples/` with `hello.txt`, `extents.bin` (2 MiB of 1 KiB blocks of `ext4 block BBBB line LL extents` lines, every fourth block zeros, which mke2fs leaves out as holes, so its 512 extents need a tree of depth 2), `docs/nested/deep.txt` and `link` -> `docs/nested/deep.txt`; mke2fs adds `lost+found`. Everything on it belongs to user and group 0 (`-E root_owner=0:0`, then `debugfs`); files are `0644`, directories `0755`. A fixed volume UUID, hash seed, `E2FSPROGS_FAKE_TIME` and file times keep the image byte for byte reproducible. The second partition, `vda2` from 128 to 144 MiB, is FAT16 (label `FEATHERFAT`, 2 KiB clusters, which keep mformat from choosing FAT12) with a long-named `Read Me First.txt` and an empty `home`; nothing mounts it at boot, and `mount -t fat16 vda2 /fat` does. The third, `vda3` from 144 to 148 MiB, is ext3 (`mke2fs -t ext3 -b 1024 -I 256`, label `FEATHEREXT3`, a 1 MiB journal) with `hello.txt`, `big.txt` (3000 lines of `ext3 line NNNNN`, 48000 bytes, through an indirect block), `docs/readme.txt` and `link` -> `docs/readme.txt`, all of user and group 0. The fourth MBR slot is an extended container (type 0x05, 148 to 162 MiB, not a partition of its own) with two logical partitions, each 1 MiB after its extended boot record, as Linux's `fdisk` lays them out: `vda5` from 149 to 153 MiB is a second ext4 volume (`mke2fs -t ext4 -b 1024 -I 256 -J size=1`, label `FEATHERDATA`) with `hello.txt` (`Hello from the second ext4 partition.`) and `samples/hello.txt` (`data volume`, the root's path with other contents), all of user and group 0; and `vda6` from 154 to 162 MiB is ext2 (`mke2fs -t ext2 -b 1024 -I 128`, label `FEATHEREXT2`) with `hello.txt`, `big.txt` (5000 lines of `ext2 line NNNNN`, 80000 bytes), `docs/notes/deep.txt`, `fast-link` and `slow-link` (symbolic links whose targets fit in the inode or need a block) and a 73-character name FAT16 cannot hold; everything but `lost+found` belongs to user and group 1000 (`hello.txt` is `0640` in group 100). The last 4 MiB of the disk are free (the disk and the partition offsets are constants in `tools/create_test_disk.py`, which the smoke harness imports). The partitions are MBR types 0x83, 0x06, 0x83, the extended 0x05 holding two 0x83 logicals; `DISK_LAYOUT=gpt` makes a GPT disk with a protective MBR, a backup table (clear of the free tail), and the Linux filesystem and Basic Data type GUIDs instead, with the same volumes at the same sectors and numbers (entry 4 empty, the data volume entry 5, ext2 entry 6); `create_test_disk.py` compares the numbers, starts and sizes with `sfdisk -J` when `sfdisk` is installed; x86-64 attaches the disk through virtio-PCI and AArch64 through virtio-MMIO. Normal startup logs which volume is the root (`VFS: / is vda1 ext4, read-write`), and reads no file from it (the `STORAGE` test checks `/BLOCK.TXT`). Run `./build.sh --arch x86_64 test` or `./build.sh --arch aarch64 test` to check the full boot path.

Both virtio-blk drivers read and write single sectors (`BlockDevice::ReadSector`/`WriteSector`). Each driver serializes requests with a lock, because one request buffer serves every CPU, and writes fail when the device reports itself read-only. A request is polled until the device answers, since a flush waits for QEMU to sync the image file, which under host load can take tens of milliseconds; a device silent for 30 s (AArch64, by the generic timer's counter) or 2^31 `pause` polls (x86-64) is taken as failed and gets no more requests, because the request it still holds would later complete in place of another. `STORAGE_TEST=ON ./build.sh --arch <arch> test` boots the same disk image twice. On each boot the kernel reads a boot counter from sector 2047, the last sector before the root's partition. It then writes the counter plus one and a byte pattern, checks that a write past the end of the disk fails, and reads the sector back. The second boot must report `boot count 2`, which shows that the first boot's write persisted. Each boot then mounts the FAT16 partition at `/fat` (`fat16 vda1` and `ext2 vda2` are `NotFound`) for the FAT16 file checks (below). The root is the test disk's ext4 `vda1`, so `CheckRoot` first reads its `/samples` (`hello.txt`, the depth-2 extent tree of `extents.bin` block by block with the holes as zeros, `link`, `docs/nested`). The first boot builds `/os` on the root (a 24000-byte `data.txt` past the direct blocks, a hard link to it, a symbolic link, a directory with a file renamed into it, a truncated file, and a file and a directory made and removed again) and checks that `EEXIST`, `ENOTEMPTY`, `EPERM`, `EINVAL` and `ENOENT` come back from the core; the second checks all of it, then removes a name of the hard-linked file, the symbolic link, moves the directory to `/moved-sub` and appends to a file. Both sync, which commits the root's journal without closing it. Each boot then mounts `ext2 vda6` read-only at `/ext2` (refusing changes and a link opened) and `ext3 vda3` at `/ext3` and `ext2 vda6` read-write (`CheckExt3AndExt2`; `ext2 vda3`, `ext4 vda3`, `ext3 vda6`, `ext4 vda6`, `ext2 vda1` and `ext3 vda1` are `NotFound`), checks that `/proc/mounts` lists them `rw`, reads `hello.txt`, the subdirectories, the links' targets and `big.txt` line by line, and runs `WriteVolumeFirstBoot` or `ChangeVolumeSecondBoot` on each (the same writes as on the root, under `/ext3/os` and `/ext2/os`) before unmounting both; `CheckSecondExt4` then mounts the data ext4 `vda5` at `/data` beside the root (both `rw`), checks its files against the root's `/samples/hello.txt`, runs the same `/os` writes on it (the second boot first finds the first boot's shell session's `/periodic.txt` replayed from its journal), writes `/data/boot<N>.bin` and the root's `/boot<N>-root.txt`, checks that mounting `vda5` or `vda1` again gives `Busy` and that a rename or link between the volumes gives `EXDEV`, unmounts it and prints `KERN/STORAGE: two ext4 volumes mounted at once; boot N`. A read-only mount of ext3 before the replay is checked on the second boot (`CheckReadOnlyBeforeReplay`); the root cannot be (it is mounted read-write at boot). Last, `CheckRemount` unmounts the root and the mounts inside it, mounts it read-only (`ext4 vda2` and `ext2 vda1` are `NotFound`; `/samples` and the boot's `/os` file read back, changes fail with `EROFS`) and read-write again. `CheckRootRemount` then remounts in place (`VFS::Remount`): the root read-only (the superblock read from the disk has `needs_recovery` clear, `/samples`, the root and its own `/etc` refuse changes, `/tmp` takes one), read-write (a file is written), read-only (read back, journal closed again) and read-write; and `/fat` read-only (creating a file gives `EROFS`) and back, with `PERSIST.TXT` intact. Then `/fat` is unmounted. The first boot's shell session then mounts ext3 and ext2 again and the data ext4 `vda5` at `/data` (a second mount of it at `/d2` gives `Device or resource busy`, and `/proc/mounts` lists both ext4 lines), writes `/periodic.txt` on the root and on all three, sleeps 8 s and is stopped from outside with no `sync` or `umount`: the second boot's root mount replays the root's journal (`VFS: / is vda1 ext4, read-write`, one `ext2: journal recovered; transactions replayed: ...` line; the harness requires two with ext3's), its kernel test finds ext2 clean with the file, mounts ext3 read-only first (no replay, `/periodic.txt` missing), then read-write, which replays its journal, and renames the files (the data volume's replay is `CheckSecondExt4`, above). After each boot the harness copies the ext2 partition out of the image and requires its clean bit, `e2fsck -fn` exit 0 and `debugfs` to read back what that boot left (`tools/smoke/cases/storage.py`; `e2fsck` and `debugfs` come from e2fsprogs, found as `create_test_disk.py` finds `mke2fs`), checks copies of the ext4 root, ext3 and the data ext4 (the same checks, plus the `/boot<N>.bin` and `/boot<N>-root.txt` files; after boot 1: `needs_recovery` set, `/periodic.txt` only in the log, `e2fsck -fy` on the copy then `-fn`; after boot 2: ext3 and the root both clean as they are, `needs_recovery` clear and `e2fsck -fn` passing with no replay on the copy, because the second boot's shell ends with `mount -o remount,ro /`, which closes the root's journal; it first checks that `remount,ro` refuses `write /remount.txt`, that `/proc/mounts` says `vda1 / ext4 ro 0 0`, and that after `remount,rw` its `write /remount.txt remounted` is on the disk), and reads the FAT16 partition with mtools. The shell case's part `remount.py` (after `mounts.py`) remounts `/tmp` read-only and back, checks the errors for `/system` (`EROFS`), `/dev` and a plain directory (`EINVAL`) and a missing path (`ENOENT`), that `mount -o remount` alone prints the usage, and that the root goes read-only and back with `/proc/mounts` following and `/tmp` still writable meanwhile; the case then replays the root's copy and checks ext3 and ext2 are clean after its part `ext4.py`, and the power cases (`shutdown`, `reboot`) that the root's journal was closed (after a shutdown) and ext2 marked clean. The ISO build recreates the disk image, so every test starts from a fresh disk.

The block-device layer (M19 Part 2 step 3a, `src/kernel/storage/disk.{h,cpp}`): a transport implements `Storage::DiskDriver` (`disk_driver.h`: `Info`, `Gone`, `Read`, `Write`, `Flush`, `Stop`, `Release`) and `Storage::Register` turns it into a `Storage::Disk`, a `FeatherDisk::BlockDevice` that the filesystem core uses. The virtio-blk device is one such driver (`storage/virtio_disk.cpp`, `BlockDevice::RegisterVirtioDisk`, registered by `MountAndVerifyDisk` as `vda` with a 64 KiB transfer limit), and `BlockDevice::CoreDevice()` returns that boot `Disk`; the USB mass-storage `Drive` registers itself after its probe. A `Disk` checks bounds (`InvalidArgument` for a transfer past the end or of a partial sector), splits a transfer into chunks of at most `max_transfer_bytes` in order, answers `Io` once it is gone (`Remove` was called) or ejected without calling the driver, answers `ReadOnly` to a write when the driver reports the disk read-only or has refused a write with `ReadOnly` (`DiskInfo::read_only_reason` says why: `Medium` for a write-protected medium, `Policy` for the M16 real-hardware write policy), answers a flush of a read-only disk with success without calling the driver, and counts sectors read and written and flushes (`Disk::Counters()`, below any cache). Names: virtio disks are `vda`, `vdb`, ..., and SCSI disks (USB, and AHCI from M16) `sda`, `sdb`, ..., each at the lowest letter no allocated `Disk` holds, so a letter comes back only after the last reference to its disk is released; `Storage::PartitionName` appends the partition number, with a `p` first after a name ending in a digit (`nvme0n1p1`); an NVMe disk is named `nvme<controller_index>n<namespace_id>` from `DiskInfo` instead of a letter, and a duplicate name is refused (M16 step A1). The registry (`Storage::DiskRegistry`, one global instance behind `Storage::Register`, `Remove`, `Acquire(name)`, `BootDisk()`, `FlushAll()`, `FormatPartitions`, `SetRemovalHandler`) holds at most `MaxDisks` (16) disks and refuses a driver that is already gone (I17) or any driver before `Storage::Initialize`. A `Disk` is counted by the registry until `Remove` (idempotent, calls the removal handler once with no lock held), by each `Acquire` and by each future volume slot; the last `Release` frees it and calls `DiskDriver::Release` once. `FlushAll` (shutdown) flushes every registered disk that is neither gone nor ejected and fails if one flush fails. Volumes still exist only on `vda`; the partition scan, volume slots and `/proc/partitions` for other disks come in step 3b. The host tests are `[disk]` (`tests/unit/kernel/storage/disk_tests.cpp`, each over its own `DiskRegistry` and fake drivers; one `[concurrency]` test also runs under `unit-test-tsan`), and `filesystem_tests.cpp` registers a fake `DiskDriver` as `vda`.

`FeatherDisk::Partition::Find` (`src/fs/core/partition/partition.cpp`) finds partitions. When the MBR holds a protective `0xEE` entry, it reads the GUID partition table and checks the header and entry-array CRC32s; a damaged table yields no partitions rather than falling back to the MBR. Otherwise it uses the MBR's FAT16 (0x06, 0x0E) and Linux (0x83) entries, primaries first, then the logical partitions of the first extended entry (0x05, 0x0F or 0x85; the container itself is never listed). Each `Partition::Range` carries Linux's `number`: an MBR primary's slot (1 to 4, with gaps), the logicals from 5 in the order of the extended boot record chain (every non-empty logical takes a number, listed or not), a GPT entry's index plus one. A chain ends at a record that cannot be read, has no 0x55AA signature, links outside the container or not strictly forward, or after 64 records; a logical must lie after its record and inside the container. It works with any power-of-two sector size from 512 bytes. FAT16 mounts the first partition with a valid boot sector, so a Linux partition listed before it is skipped, and ext2 the first that holds an ext2 volume. The kernel names partitions as Linux names the first virtio disk's: `vda1`, `vda2`, ... by `Partition::Range::number`, the numbering `fscli` uses (partitions numbered above 16 are not mounted) (`Filesystem::Drivers::PartitionSource`; `vda` alone would be a filesystem on the whole disk, which nothing mounts yet). On the test disk ext4, the root, is `vda1`, FAT16 `vda2`, ext3 `vda3`, a second ext4 `vda5` (a logical partition inside the MBR's extended slot 4, or GPT entry 5) and ext2 `vda6`; `vda4` does not exist.


### Filesystem locking

Filesystem operations lock one volume; operations on different volumes run in parallel (M30 Part 5). The locks, in the order they are taken (nothing takes an earlier one while holding a later one):

1. `namespace_lock` (`src/kernel/files.cpp`): `Files::Mount`, `Unmount`, `Remount`, `ChangeDirectory` and `Quiesce`, so the mount tree and the working directories an unmount checks change one at a time.
2. One volume lock, `Driver::VolumeLock()`, a `Sync::VolumeMutex` that records its owner. Each `CoreDriver` has its own; `/tmp` and `/etc` share one (Tmpfs's node table is shared); `/system`, `/dev` and `/proc` have one each. A task holds at most one: taking a second is `Panic("VFS: two volume locks")` in the kernel and a counted violation in host tests. Every `VFS::` call locks its driver unless the task holds that lock already, so `Files` takes it once around a check-then-act sequence (`VolumeHold`, after a mount user that keeps the mount from being unmounted) and the calls inside do not lock again. Path walks (`VFS::ResolvePath`), `ReadDirectory`, the syncs and `/proc/mounts` hold none: each driver call inside takes its own volume's lock for that call. A rename or link between two volumes gives `EXDEV` before any volume lock is taken.
3. `table_lock`: descriptor tables, `OpenFile` references and `disk_files`; never held across a driver call or while waiting for a volume lock. It is taken before `process_lock` (closing a pty master under it hangs up a session, and `/proc`'s process status takes `table_lock` first, then `process_lock`), never after, so no task sleeps on it while holding that spinlock. A descriptor operation pins its `OpenFile` (`references++`) under it, works under the file's volume lock, then unpins; the last unpin closes the file under that lock.
4. `mount_lock` (a spinlock; the mount and type tables only).
5. Leaf spinlocks: the core's per-volume host lock, the page cache's lock (an interrupt-saving spinlock around index, LRU and counter work and copies of one page; no device call, allocation, free or log under it), the virtio device lock and the heap.

`VFS::LastError()` is per task (`Scheduler::CurrentLocal().filesystem_error`); a driver's own `LastError()` is read under its volume lock. `Files::Quiesce` takes every mounted volume's lock with `VolumeMutex::LockWithOthers` and keeps them. In the thread-safety analysis the volume lock is the pseudo-capability `held_volume` (`VolumeHold` acquires it; `OpenFile::handle` is `GUARDED_BY` it). The concurrency host tests (`tests/unit/kernel/filesystem/concurrency_tests.cpp`, tag `[concurrency]`) also run under ThreadSanitizer with `./build.sh unit-test-tsan`.

### Shared filesystem core

`src/fs/core/` holds the code that FeatherOS shares with the future Feather Disk app and `fscli` (M22 in `docs/roadmap.md`): its namespace is `FeatherDisk`, it includes nothing from outside its folder (checked by `tools/quality.sh`'s `fs-core` stage) and it is licensed MIT OR Apache-2.0. Its public headers are in `include/fs/`: `error.h` (`FeatherDisk::Error`, `Result<T>` as `std::expected<T, Error>`, `Status`), `block_device.h` (the abstract `BlockDevice`: sector size and count, multi-sector `Read`/`Write`, `Flush`), `host.h` (`FeatherDisk::Host`: `Allocate`/`Free`, an opaque `Lock` with `CreateLock`, `Acquire`, `Release` and `DestroyLock`, `Log` and `WallClockNanoseconds`, which each host defines), `filesystem.h` (the driver interface: `FilesystemType` probes, mounts and unmounts; a mounted `Filesystem` looks up names, reads directories by cookie, gets and sets attributes, reads, writes and truncates, creates, removes, renames, links and makes directories and symbolic links, all on opaque 64-bit `NodeId`s, and reports its `Capabilities`) and `partition.h`. `common/` has the little-endian load and store templates, `HostBuffer`, `CopyBytes`/`ZeroBytes`, allocation bitmaps (`bitmap.cpp`), `BlockCache`, which writes through, and the page cache (`page_cache.cpp`, M30 Part 6). `BlockDevice::CachesBlocks()` tells a user of a device that a host cache sits below it (`CachedDevice` while attached): ext then gives its `BlockCache` no slots, so the cache passes every `Read` (one read of the whole block), `ReadUncached` and `Write` straight to the device and keeps no copy; plain devices keep the 32 KiB cache. The kernel builds the core as the static library `featherdisk_core` with its own flags and `src/libcxx`; its side of the boundary is `src/kernel/storage/core_block_device.cpp` (`BlockDevice::CoreDevice()`, the virtio disk as a core `BlockDevice`) and `src/kernel/filesystem/core_host.cpp` (`host.h` over the kernel heap, an interrupt-saving spinlock, `KLogger` on the serial port and the wall clock). `include/fs/fat.h` gives the FAT16 driver, `FeatherDisk::Fat::Fat16Type()` (`fat/fat16.cpp`, with long names in `fat/names.cpp`), and `partition.h` also has `Partition::Volume`, one partition of a device as a `BlockDevice`. FAT16 node IDs name a file by its directory entry's location (the directory's first cluster, 0 for the root, and the entry's index) and a directory by its first cluster with bit 63 set, so a directory keeps its ID when renamed and `..` resolves to the same ID as the directory itself. `include/fs/ext2.h` gives `FeatherDisk::Ext::Ext2Type()`, `Ext3Type()` and `Ext4Type()` (`ext2`, `ext3`, `ext4`): one driver under three names, which reads ext2, ext3 and ext4 and writes ext2 (see below). Each type probes only volumes of its own variant (ext4 with any ext4 feature, else ext3 with a journal, else ext2, as `DescribeFeatures` and blkid name them), and each mounts all three, as Linux's ext4 driver does. The kernel reaches core filesystems through `Filesystem::CoreDriver` (`src/kernel/filesystem/core_driver.cpp`), a VFS driver that walks paths with `Lookup`, turns core errors into failures and core attributes into `FileInfo` and `DirectoryEntry`, and serves one partition (`CoreDriver::OpenPartition` takes the first of FAT16, ext2, ext3 and ext4 whose probe and read-only core mount accept it, and its options follow that kind: `periodic_sync` for the ext ones, `close_on_detach` for ext3 and ext4); `Filesystem::Drivers` keeps a static pool of 16 of them, `Volume(n)` being partition number `n`'s for the kernel's whole life (`VolumeOf`, `FindPartitions`, `OpenVolume`, `ForgetVolume`), and `CoreDriver::PartitionNumber()` and `TypeName()` tell what each holds. `VFS::MountBlockDevice` opens every partition `Partition::Find` lists in the core, read-only (FAT16 too; it is reopened read-write when mounted), keeps a volume mounted somewhere other than `/`, forgets a slot whose partition has gone or holds no volume, registers each volume as its kind and its partition's name (`ext4` `vda1`, `fat16` `vda2`, `ext3` `vda3`, `ext4` `vda5` and `ext2` `vda6` on the test disk), and puts one of them at `/`, read-write: the one on partition `root=` names (`MountBlockDevice(requested, &honored)`; the choice itself is `VFS::ChooseRoot`), or when there is no `root=`, or its partition holds no usable volume (then `honored` is false and startup warns), the lowest-numbered (a disk whose FAT16 partition comes first, like the host tests' fixtures, has a FAT16 root). An ext3 or ext4 root is mounted like `mount` would: read-write with its journal replayed if it needs recovery, covered by the 5 s writeback and closed by `VFS::DetachAll` at shutdown. A volume the core refuses to write (not cleanly unmounted, errors recorded, unknown features) becomes a read-only root, and startup logs a warning: `VFS: / is vda1 ext4, read-only: the volume refused writes`. The other volume stays out of the tree until `mount -t fat16 vda2 /fat`; a volume mounted somewhere other than `/` keeps its filesystem when `MountBlockDevice` runs again. Each core mount has its own lock from `Host::CreateLock` and its own block cache, so `core_host.cpp` serves both.

`fscli` (`src/fs/core/tools/fscli/`) reads and writes core filesystems on the host. `./build.sh unit-test` builds it as `build/unit-tests-ninja/fs_core/tools/fscli/fscli`; on its own, `cmake -S src/fs/core -B build/fs-core -G Ninja && cmake --build build/fs-core` gives `build/fs-core/tools/fscli/fscli` (and `ctest --test-dir build/fs-core` runs the core's tests). It opens an image file or a raw disk (`/dev/rdiskN` on macOS, `/dev/sdX` on Linux, which need read permission, and write permission to change them) read-only, or read-write for the commands that change it (`cp-in`, `rm`, `mkdir`, `rmdir`, `mv`, `ln`):

```sh
fscli info build/x86_64/virtio-disk.img        # table, partitions, filesystems, free space
fscli ls build/x86_64/virtio-disk.img /home    # mode as ls -l shows it, size, name
fscli ls disk.img:2 /                          # partition 2, numbered as `info` lists them (`Range::number`)
fscli cat build/x86_64/virtio-disk.img "/Read Me First.txt"
fscli cp build/x86_64/virtio-disk.img /BLOCK.TXT /tmp/   # copy out
fscli cp-in disk.img:2 notes.txt /docs                   # copy in, as /docs/notes.txt
fscli rm disk.img:2 /docs/notes.txt
fscli mkdir disk.img:2 /docs/old                         # mode 0755
fscli mv disk.img:2 /docs/plan.txt /docs/old             # into a directory, as /docs/old/plan.txt
fscli rmdir disk.img:2 /docs/empty
fscli ln disk.img:2 /docs/old/plan.txt /plan.txt         # a second name (hard link)
fscli ln -s disk.img:2 docs/old/plan.txt /plan-link      # a symbolic link holding the text
fscli readlink disk.img:2 /plan-link
```

`cp-in <image>[:N] <host file> <path>` copies a regular host file in: into a directory of the volume under the host file's name, or to the path, replacing a file there. The new file gets the host file's permission bits and user and group 0; if the copy fails (a full volume, say), a file it created is removed again. `rm <image>[:N] <path>` removes a file, symbolic link or special node, not a directory. Both fail with `the volume mounted read-only` when the filesystem will not take writes (an ext2 volume with a journal, errors or an unclean state; the reason is logged as `featherdisk warning: ext2: mounted read-only: ...`), and report a failed final sync. They work on FAT16 and ext2; check an ext2 image with `e2fsck -fn` afterwards if in doubt. `mkdir <image>[:N] <path>` makes a directory with mode 0755 and `rmdir <image>[:N] <path>` removes an empty one. `mv <image>[:N] <from> <to>` renames or moves a file, link or directory as POSIX `rename` does: onto an existing file it replaces it, and an existing directory at `<to>` takes `<from>` inside under its own name, as `mv` does, replacing a file or empty directory of that name there. `ln [-s] <image>[:N] <target> <path>` makes a hard link to the file `<target>` in the volume, or with `-s` a symbolic link holding `<target>` as given (it need not exist); a directory at `<path>` takes the link under `<target>`'s last name. `readlink <image>[:N] <path>` prints a link's target. FAT16 holds no links (`not supported by this filesystem`); a hard link to a directory fails with `operation not permitted`.

Without a partition number it uses the whole disk if it holds a filesystem, otherwise the first partition that does. It probes the types in `Fscli::FilesystemTypes()` (`disk.cpp`: FAT16, then ext2, ext3 and ext4), so `info` names an ext volume's variant (`filesystem: ext4`). `Partition::Find` lists an MBR's FAT16 and Linux entries and every used GPT entry. `info` also prints an ext volume's variant and features (`format: ext4, features: has_journal ...`, in dumpe2fs's order). `ls` prints the mode as `ls -l` does (FAT has no permissions, so its read-only mount shows `-r--r--r--` and `dr-xr-xr-x`; a device, FIFO or socket shows `?`), the size and the name, and a symbolic link as `name -> target`. Links are not followed: `cat` and `cp` refuse them, and devices, FIFOs and sockets. Exit status is 1 when an operation fails and 2 for a usage error; `fscli --help` lists the commands. The `fs.core:fscli` CTest cases (`tools/fscli/tests/fscli_tests.sh`) run it over a bare and an MBR-partitioned FAT16 image that mtools makes at build time, and the `ext2-*` cases over the ext2 images; each is skipped without the tool that makes its images.

ext2 (`src/fs/core/ext/`) reads revisions 0 and 1 with 1, 2 or 4 KiB blocks, inodes of 128 bytes or more (with nanosecond times when they have room), direct and single, double and triple indirect blocks, holes as zeros, directories with and without the filetype feature, and fast (in the inode) and slow symbolic links. ext3 and ext4 volumes whose files use block maps read the same way (M22 Part 5 step 1): ext3's journal is ignored, and ext4's 64bit (64-byte group descriptors), flex_bg, large inodes, huge_file, dir_nlink, extra_isize, unwritten groups (uninit_bg: their inodes read as free) and checksums are understood. CRC16 descriptor checksums (gdt_csum) and CRC32C ones (metadata_csum, seeded from the UUID or `s_checksum_seed`) are verified on read for the superblock, every group descriptor at mount, each inode and each directory block (leaf tails and hash-index blocks); a mismatch is `Error::Corrupt` with a log line. Bitmap checksums are not checked on read, since reading loads no bitmap; the write path checks each bitmap it loads. A journal that needs recovery (`needs_recovery`) is replayed by a mount asked to write (M22 Part 6 step 1, `ext/journal.cpp` and `ext/recovery.cpp`): jbd2 superblocks v1 and v2, 32- and 64-bit tags, revoke records, escaped blocks and checksums v2 and v3 (descriptor and revoke tails, block tags, commit blocks; the compatible v1 checksums are not verified). Only a torn tail ends the log: a transaction whose commit block never comes, or with async_commit fails its checksum. The transactions before it are copied to their places, the journal is emptied (`s_start` 0, `s_sequence` one past the last transaction looked at, as e2fsck leaves it) and `needs_recovery` is cleared, then the mount goes on as for a clean volume. A committed transaction with a descriptor, revoke, block or (without async_commit) commit checksum that fails, and a malformed journal (a block past the volume, a revoke count past its block, a log that goes round the ring, a hole, a superblock that does not hold together), fail the mount with `Error::Corrupt` before anything is written, leaving `needs_recovery` set for e2fsck. A read-only mount never replays: it reads the volume as it is on disk and logs that recent changes are missing; so does a writable mount when the journal is on another device, has unknown features, or the device refuses writes. Extents read since Part 5 step 2, so mke2fs's default ext4 mounts: trees of any depth (`ext/extent.cpp`), with 48-bit block numbers, holes and unwritten extents read as zeros, and each node's header, depth, order, block range and (with metadata_csum) CRC32C tail checked, else `Error::Corrupt`; the extents flag on a volume without the feature is `Corrupt` too. Files, directories and slow links map a run of blocks per tree walk. Any other incompatible feature (meta_bg, inline_data, encrypt, casefold, large_dir, mmp, journal_dev, ...) and bigalloc refuse the mount with `Error::Unsupported` and a log line naming the feature bits; `FeatherDisk::Ext::DescribeFeatures` and `fscli info` name a volume's variant and features either way. Node IDs are inode numbers, the root is inode 2, names match byte for byte, and `ReadDirectory`'s cookie is the byte offset of the next record. Everything read from the disk is checked before it is used: record and name lengths, inode numbers against the inode count, block numbers against the block count and the superblock's block, inode tables, a directory's size against the blocks it holds, and a size past what the block map reaches; a check that fails gives `Error::Corrupt`. Metadata blocks go through `common/block_cache.cpp`, 32 KiB per mount (none over a device whose `CachesBlocks()` is true, where the page cache is the only copy), whose host lock covers only copying; file data is read straight into the caller's buffer. A mount asked for with `read_only = false` also writes files (M22 Part 4 step 3): `Create` (a regular file with the requested permission bits, owner and group 0, the filetype byte when the volume has the feature; a full directory grows by a block), `Write` (holes stay holes, `large_file` is set with the first file past 2 GiB, a full volume gives the bytes written so far), `Truncate` (shrinking frees data and indirect blocks, growing leaves a hole), `Remove` (files, symbolic links and special nodes; the last link frees the blocks, an extended attribute block's reference and the inode) and `SetAttributes` (mode bits, owner, group, access and modification times). Since step 4 it also changes the tree (`ext/namespace.cpp`): `MakeDirectory` (a block holding `.` and `..`, two links, the parent's count up by one, `bg_used_dirs_count` up; a directory in the root goes to the group with the fewest directories among those with at least the average share of free inodes and blocks, any other one near its parent), `RemoveDirectory` (only an empty directory, `NotEmpty` otherwise; `.` and `..` are `InvalidArgument`, the root `Busy`), `Rename` (within and across directories, replacing a file or an empty directory of the same kind, `NotDirectory`/`IsDirectory` for mixed kinds, `InvalidArgument` for a directory moved into itself or below it, which a walk up `..` from the new parent finds, and nothing at all for two names of the same inode; a moved directory's `..` and both parents' link counts follow), `SymbolicLink` (mode `0777`, a target under 60 bytes in `i_block` with no blocks, a longer one in a block of its own, `NameTooLong` from `MaximumPathLength`, 1024, on) and `Link` (files, links and special nodes; `NotPermitted`, a new core error, for a directory, `TooManyLinks` at ext2's 32000, which also limits subdirectories; with dir_nlink 65000, and a directory past that counts 1, as on Linux). Removed entries never shrink a directory, as in Linux's ext2: their records join the one before or are marked unused, and the blocks stay for later entries. Link counts go up before the entry or `..` that adds a link is on the disk and down only after it is gone, so after a crash they are too high, never too low, except for a renamed node, which may briefly have both names (the order and what e2fsck reports at each point are at the top of `ext/namespace.cpp`). Since Part 6 step 2 the same writes run on ext4 layouts without a journal or extents: 64bit, flex_bg, metadata_csum and its seed, gdt_csum (uninit_bg), huge_file, dir_nlink, extra_isize and sparse_super2. Every block written carries its checksums (superblock, descriptors, inodes, bitmaps, directory leaf tails, index blocks, extended attribute blocks), each bitmap's checksum is checked as it loads (a mismatch stops writes on the mount and records the errors state), groups never used (`BLOCK_UNINIT`, `INODE_UNINIT`, `bg_itable_unused`) are set up on first use, and a hash-indexed directory keeps its index while names leave it but drops it before a name is added (the blocks that held the index become ordinary leaves). Since Part 6 step 3 extents are written too (`ext/extent_write.cpp`): on a volume with the feature, new files, directories and slow links get an extent tree in the inode as on Linux (fast links and special nodes none), while files that still have block maps keep them. Writes map holes with runs of new blocks near the ones before, extending the last extent while it continues (up to 32768 blocks), split full nodes and move a full root down a level, turn the unwritten blocks they reach written (data first, zeros around it), and `Truncate` and `Remove` free data and tree blocks, an emptied tree becoming a leaf in the inode again. It mounts read-only instead, with a host log line, for a journal that cannot be written through (see below), another incompatible feature, unknown read-only compatible or compatible features (quota, ...), a volume that was not cleanly unmounted or has errors recorded, inconsistent group descriptors or a device that refuses writes, so `GetCapabilities().writable` says which it got. While mounted for writing the superblock says not clean; `Sync` and `Unmount` write the group descriptors and the superblock's counts and state back, and the next change marks it again. Every other block of a volume without a journal is written through at once, in an order that leaves at worst leaks after a crash (the comment at the top of `ext/write.cpp`). Allocation stays near the file's inode, new inodes near their directory, and the root's reserved blocks are used too, since FeatherOS has no users. Changes and `Sync` must not run alongside any other operation on the mount; reads may. `src/fs/core/tests/make_ext2_image.sh` builds the test images (1 KiB blocks with 128-byte inodes, 4 KiB and 2 KiB blocks with 256-byte inodes, the 2 KiB one without filetype, revision 0, the 1 KiB volume inside an MBR disk, ext3, ext4 with extents as `ext4` and, in 32 MiB with a two-level extent tree, fragmented and unwritten files and a 3000-entry hashed directory, `ext4-extents`, and ext4 without extents as `ext4-blockmap` (metadata_csum, flex_bg, a hash-indexed `many/`), `ext4-64bit` and `ext4-gdtcsum`, and without a journal either, which the core writes, as `ext4-csum`, `ext4-csum64` and, with a 3000-entry hashed `large/`, `ext4-htree` and `ext4-htree-gdtcsum`, and ext4 with extents and no journal as `ext4-nojournal` (`ext4-extents` without the journal) and `ext4-mixed` (4 KiB blocks, extents turned on after the files were written, so they keep block maps)) with `mke2fs -d` and fixes owners, times and link modes with `debugfs`, so `fscli` can be tried on them: `build/fs-core/ext-images/1k.img` after a standalone build. In FeatherOS the test disk's ext2 partition is `vda6`, mounted by `mount -t ext2 vda6 /mnt` (it was the root until M22 Part 6b); `mount -t ext2 <partition> <point>` mounts another one read-write and `mount -r` read-only. An ext2 volume found at boot but not put at `/` stays mounted read-only in the core, so it stays clean, and is reopened for writing when a read-write mount asks (`CoreDriver::OpenForMount`). A volume the core refuses to write mounts read-only anyway, and `/proc/mounts` says `ro`. The test disk's ext3 partition mounts with `mount -t ext3 vda3 <point>`, and any other ext3 or ext4 partition the same way (the test disk's ext4 is the root, and a volume mounts at one point at most), read-write unless `-r` is given or the core refuses writes (since M22 Part 6 step 5): the core logs why (`ext2: mounted read-only: ...`), and changes fail with `EROFS`. The type must name the volume's variant: `mount -t ext2 vda1` (the ext4 root) is `No such file or directory`, unlike Linux, where ext4 mounts all three. Unmounting and shutting down or rebooting (`VFS::Unmount` and `Files::Quiesce` call `Driver::Detach`) leave an ext2, ext3 or ext4 volume clean, `sync` makes ext2 clean and commits ext3 and ext4, and the writeback task does the same every 5 s; a machine stopped within about 5 s of a change leaves ext2 not clean, so the next boot mounts it read-only until `e2fsck -fy`, and ext3 and ext4 with a journal that the host (`e2fsck -fy`) or the next writable mount replays. New files get mode `0666`, directories `0777`, owner and group 0. Symbolic links are followed by the VFS walk. Names of up to 255 bytes list and open whole, since M22 Part 4 widened the VFS to `NAME_MAX` and `PATH_MAX`.

ext3 and ext4 volumes with an internal jbd2 journal are written in the core and by `fscli` since M22 Part 6 step 4 (design in `docs/design/M22-P6-s4.md`; FeatherOS mounts them read-write since step 5, `docs/design/M22-P6-s5.md`). Every metadata block of a change goes through the journal and file contents are written in place before the commit (ordered mode, so an overwrite inside a file is not atomic across a crash). `ext/transaction.cpp` holds the changes of a step, of the open transaction and of the committed ones not yet checkpointed as block copies in memory; a commit writes the log and flushes twice, and happens at `Sync`, at unmount, when the open transaction reaches a quarter of the journal or 5 s after its first change (the host calls `Sync` every few seconds, the core has no timer), and a checkpoint writes the committed copies to their places and empties the log. `needs_recovery` is set while the volume is mounted. A change that does not fit one transaction (a large write, truncation, removal or rename onto a large file) is split, with an orphan file slot or `s_last_orphan` entry between its first transaction and its last, and every entry a crash leaves is finished by the next writable mount (or by `e2fsck`). A volume with a journal on another device, a version 1 journal, one shorter than 1024 blocks or with fast commits or unknown features, a malformed orphan list or orphan file, or orphans without a journal mounts read-only. `ext/ext2_filesystem.h` has `Ext2Filesystem::LimitJournal` and `Activity()` for tests. The crash tests (`tests/ext_transaction_crash_tests.cpp`) stop runs of changes after each write, or each write around a commit, checkpoint or split, recover every copy with a writable mount and with `e2fsck -fy`, and require the same tree and free ranges from both, `e2fsck -fn` exit 0 on each, and the tree of a committed state; `MemoryDevice::TrackUnflushed` and `CopyKeeping` also let them lose a random part of the writes since the last flush. `fscli cp-in`, `rm`, `mkdir`, `rmdir`, `mv` and `ln` work on such volumes (CTest `fs.core:fscli ext-journal`) and leave the journal empty.

Filesystem errors reach programs as Linux's `errno` values. `CoreDriver` maps every core error with `Filesystem::SyscallError` (`NotFound` to `ENOENT`, `Exists` `EEXIST`, `NotDirectory` `ENOTDIR`, `IsDirectory` `EISDIR`, `NotEmpty` `ENOTEMPTY`, `NoSpace` `ENOSPC`, `ReadOnly` `EROFS`, `InvalidArgument` `EINVAL`, `NameTooLong` `ENAMETOOLONG`, `FileTooLarge` `EFBIG`, `TooManyLinks` `EMLINK`, `Busy` `EBUSY`, `NotPermitted` `EPERM`, `OutOfMemory` `ENOMEM`, `Unsupported` `EOPNOTSUPP`, `Io` and `Corrupt` `EIO`) and keeps it for `Driver::LastError`; the VFS records it, or `SyscallErrorReadOnly` for a read-only mount, and `VFS::LastError()` hands it to `files.cpp`, which still answers what it can check itself first, in Linux's order. A write that fills the volume returns the bytes that fit and the next one fails with `ENOSPC`. `.` and `..` never reach a driver, since paths are resolved first, so `files.cpp` gives Linux's answers from the path as written: `rmdir` of a last component `.` is `EINVAL` and `..` `ENOTEMPTY`, and `rename` with either name ending in `.` or `..` `EBUSY`. Drivers that cannot say why (tmpfs, the virtual filesystems) give `SyscallErrorFailed`, which libc reports as `EIO`.

FAT16 supports subdirectories and VFAT long names, such as `/home/Project Files/Meeting Notes.txt`; `.` and `..` work, and `..` at the root stays at the root. Each path component matches an entry's long name or its 8.3 short name, ignoring case. Names are up to 63 printable ASCII characters without `\ / : * ? " < > |` and may not end in a space or dot. A name that fits 8.3 with a single case in each part is stored as a short entry alone, with lower case recorded in the Windows NT case bits so `notes.txt` lists as `notes.txt`. Any other name gets long-name entries, which `src/fs/core/fat/names.cpp` encodes and decodes, followed by a generated, unique `BASIS~N` short alias in consecutive free slots; a subdirectory grows when there is no such run. A long name counts only when its sequence is complete and its checksum matches the short entry after it; otherwise the short name is used, and non-ASCII characters read as `?`. Removing a file also marks its long-name entries deleted. `VFS::Create`, `Write`, `Truncate` (shrink only), `Remove`, `MakeDirectory`, and `RemoveDirectory` work on disk paths, while the initramfs under `/system` stays read-only. A directory entry is located by its directory's first cluster (0 for the fixed-size root) and its index. New directories get `.` and `..` entries in a zeroed cluster, which is written before the parent links it, and a full subdirectory grows by one cluster. Only empty directories can be removed, and `Open` and `Remove` refuse directories. Writes may start anywhere up to the end of the file, so files grow only at their end. New clusters are zeroed before use, every FAT copy is updated, and walking a cluster chain is bounded so a looping chain fails instead of hanging. A write that fills the disk stops with the bytes written so far, and the file size covers exactly those bytes. Updates are ordered so an interrupted operation can only leak clusters: data and FAT entries are written before the directory entry that references them, and a directory entry is cleared before its clusters are freed. A `VFS::FileHandle` caches the file's size, so a second handle to the same file does not see the first one's changes. The driver keeps nothing in memory that changes and takes no lock of its own; the kernel's file lock in `files.cpp` lets one task change the disk at a time. In `STORAGE_TEST`, each boot mounts the FAT16 partition at `/fat`, checks the previous boot's `/fat/PERSIST.TXT`, rewrites it with 200 lines spread over several clusters, and creates and removes `SCRATCH.TMP` to check that its clusters come back. It then unmounts the built-in filesystems, `/fat` and the root (`/` must be busy first while they are mounted, and again while `/BLOCK.TXT` is open), checks that the file is gone, mounts the root read-only and then read-write again with `VFS::Mount("ext2", "vda1", "/")`, mounts the others back and checks `PERSIST.TXT` again. Each `STORAGE_TEST` boot then drives the shell, which mounts the partition with `mount -t fat16 vda2 /fat` and works in `/fat/home`. Boot 1 writes `notes.txt` with `write` and `write -a`, and reads it with `cat` and `ls`. It also reads `Read Me First.txt`, which mtools wrote with a long name, and writes `My Plans.txt`, which needs one. Boot 1 also creates `docs/` with `docs/plan.txt`. Boot 2 must still `cat` all three files after the restart, using a differently cased long name for `My Plans.txt`, checks that `rmdir` refuses the non-empty `docs/`, then removes both files and the directory and checks that `cat`, a second `rm`, and `ls` see them gone. Both run `sync` before the last `ls`. After each boot, the smoke test reads the FAT16 partition with mtools: it must see the same `PERSIST.TXT`, no scratch file, `NOTES.TXT`, `DOCS/PLAN.TXT`, and `My Plans.txt` after boot 1 and none of them after boot 2, and matching FAT copies.

### Page cache memory (M30 Part 6)

The page cache's frames are physical frames from the page allocator, reached through the HHDM, not heap memory, because the heap never unmaps and could not shrink (`FramePages` in `src/kernel/storage/page_cache.cpp`). Only the entry array and the hash buckets (about 36 bytes per page of the limit) come from the heap, once, in `InitializeCache`, which runs first in `MountAndVerifyDisk`, after the heap, whether or not a disk exists (a machine with USB drives only caches their reads too), and before `Storage::Initialize`, the virtio disk and `MountBlockDevice`; the `PageCache` object itself sits in raw storage, since the kernel runs no static destructors. Sizing (`src/kernel/storage/cache_sizing.cpp`, host-tested): the watermark is max(total frames / 32, 1024 frames), the cache's limit is a quarter of the free frames at that moment and never more than what lies above the watermark (about 130,000 pages of QEMU's 2 GiB), and the cache grows only while the free frames stay above the watermark (`FramePages::Headroom`); with no headroom a fill recycles the cache's own least recently used page or reads uncached. Three things shrink it. The writeback task calls `BalanceCache()` every 5 s: once the free frames are at or below the watermark it frees one page at a time until they are half a watermark above it, or the cache is empty. A failed `AllocatePageFrame` calls the reclaimer registered with `SetFrameReclaimer` (the cache's `Shrink(32)`) with no allocator lock held, and tries once more; that covers user page faults, kernel stacks, page tables and heap growth, since they all allocate through it, and runs inside callers that hold the heap or page-table lock, so the reclaimer never allocates, logs or takes a lock but the cache's own leaf lock. `AllocateContiguousPageFrames` does not reclaim. `Storage::BootDisk()->Counters()` (`disk.h`) counts sectors read and written and flushes below any cache, as an oracle for the kernel test. The host unit tests build `page_allocator.cpp` over a static buffer that stands in for physical memory (`tests/unit/support/page_cache_host.cpp`, shared by `tests/unit/kernel/storage/page_cache_tests.cpp`), and a Catch2 listener there runs the kernel's `InitializeCache` before the first test of every process, so the filesystem and concurrency tests mount their volumes through the same cache the kernel uses. A host test that changes the disk below a volume that stays open must call `HostPageCache::InvalidateAll()` first, or the volume reads its cached copy.

Which volumes use the cache (M30 Part 6 step 4): each `CoreDriver` has a `FeatherDisk::CachedDevice` over its `Partition::Volume`, and every probe, mount, remount and journal close-and-reopen runs over it, so a core filesystem never reads or writes the volume by another path (the partition scan and the kernel test's scratch sector read the whole disk and lie outside every volume). `OpenPartition` attaches it (a new volume id each time), `Forget` detaches it, and `Detach` (unmount, shutdown) invalidates it; a remount and a volume `MountBlockDevice` keeps keep their pages. `Drivers::OpenVolume` attaches none when `BlockDevice::CacheReady()` is false, and none when the partition's sectors overlap another open volume's (a warning is logged and that volume keeps the core's 32 KiB metadata cache); a write through an uncached overlapping volume is not seen by pages the other already cached, so overlapping partitions are a misconfiguration this does not make safe.

The QEMU checks (M30 Part 6 step 5) run in `RunStorageTests` on both boots, after the root file check and before the writeback task starts, so nothing else reads the disk and `Storage::BootDisk()->Counters().read_sectors` moves only for the check's own reads. `CheckPageCache` takes the ext4 root's `/samples/extents.bin` (2 MiB, a quarter of it holes), the FAT16 `PERSIST.TXT` and the ext2 `vda6`'s `big.txt` (mounted read-only just for this): after `CoreDriver::InvalidateCache()` the first read must match the disk's pattern and read at least the file's data sectors, the second must match, read no sector and raise the cache's `hits`; it prints `KERN/STORAGE: second read of a large file did no disk reads; boot N`. `CheckCacheReclaim` then allocates every frame with `AllocatePageFrame` (chained through frames of their own, so it needs neither the heap nor a touch of each frame), which must reclaim all of the cache's pages (at least `free + cached - 32` frames taken, the cache empty, the free count back to what it was after the frames are freed), re-reads `extents.bin` correctly and prints `KERN/STORAGE: allocation reclaimed the page cache; boot N`. `tools/smoke/cases/storage.py` expects both lines on each boot.

## User Programs And Syscalls

The CMake build compiles each program listed in `src/user/CMakeLists.txt` (`src/user/<name>.cpp`) with the matching freestanding cross-compiler and architecture-specific user linker script into `build/<arch>/programs/<name>-<arch>.elf`, and ISO generation packages every program there in the initramfs as `/system/<name>`, staged in `build/<arch>/initramfs/system`. The archive's top-level directories are `system/`, mounted read-only, and `etc/`, from `initramfs/etc`, which the kernel copies into the RAM filesystem at `/etc`. Programs may use floating point and SIMD. The kernel never touches those registers: x86-64 builds it with `-mgeneral-regs-only` and AArch64 with `+nofp+nosimd`. So the registers keep the running program's values through interrupts and syscalls, and the scheduler only saves them into the outgoing task and loads the incoming task's on a context switch (`src/kernel/arch/floating_point.h`): FXSAVE/FXRSTOR on x86-64, and q0–q31 with FPCR and FPSR on AArch64. Each CPU enables them at boot: CR0/CR4 on x86-64, CPACR_EL1 on AArch64. A new task starts with every exception masked and rounding to nearest. x86-64 programs enter `_start` with `rsp` 8 bytes past a 16-byte boundary, as if called, which SSE code on the stack relies on. Each ELF has separate RX, RO, and RW load segments. `src/kernel/process.cpp` loads a program into its own address space, maps a user stack, and enters ring 3 on x86-64 or EL0 on AArch64. A CPU exception in user mode terminates only that program; the kernel reports it with the `ExitStatusFault` or `ExitStatusKernelAccessFault` status from `src/user/syscall_abi.h`.

`src/user/syscall_abi.h` defines the syscall ABI shared by the kernel and programs, and `src/user/syscall.h` wraps it. x86-64 enters through `int 0x80` with the number in `rax` and arguments in `rdi`, `rsi`, and `rdx`; AArch64 uses `svc #0` with the number in `x8` and arguments in `x0`-`x2`. The result returns in `rax` or `x0`. Values at or above `SyscallErrorBase` are errors: as on Linux, the top 4095 values are reserved, which no size, address or process ID reaches. Each error names a cause (`SyscallErrorBadDescriptor`, `SyscallErrorBadAddress`, `SyscallErrorReadOnly`, `SyscallErrorExists`, `SyscallErrorNotDirectory`, and so on), which the C library maps to an `errno` value.

| Number | Syscall | Behavior |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | `Exit(status)` | Ends the program with an exit status |
| 1 | `WriteCharacter(character)` | Writes printable ASCII or `\n` to the console |
| 2 | `ReadLine(buffer, capacity, prompt)` | Shows the prompt and blocks until Enter; only the foreground process may read |
| 3 | `Spawn(command_line, flags, environment)` | Starts the program the first word names and returns its process ID: a name without a `/` is a file in `/system`, anything else a path relative to the caller's working directory. Any regular file may be tried: it is checked as an ELF (`SyscallErrorNotExecutable` otherwise, `SyscallErrorIsDirectory`, `SyscallErrorNotFound`) and its segments are read into the new process's pages (`SyscallErrorNoMemory` past the page limit), while the file stays open, so it cannot be written, replaced or removed meanwhile (`SyscallErrorBusy` if it is open for writing); `SpawnBackground` leaves the controlling terminal with the caller, and `SpawnSessionLeader` makes the child lead a new session and group, taking the terminal at its descriptor 0 as its controlling terminal if no session has it. `environment` is the child's environment block, or null for an empty one |
| 4 | `Wait(process_id, status_pointer, flags)` | Blocks until a child exits, stores its status, releases it and returns `WaitResultExited`; with `WaitStopped` or `WaitContinued`, also returns `WaitResultStopped` with the stop signal or `WaitResultContinued` once for each stop or continue not yet reported; with `WaitNoHang`, returns `SyscallErrorWouldBlock` while the child runs |
| 5 | `ReadClock()` | Returns the current UTC time in nanoseconds since 1970-01-01, advanced by 10 ms timer ticks |
| 6 | `ExtendHeap(increment)` | Moves the program break by `increment` bytes, a signed value, and returns the previous break; `0` returns the current one |
| 7 | `Sleep(milliseconds)` | Blocks for at least that long (rounded up to timer ticks, at most one day) without using a CPU; `Sleep(0)` only lets other ready tasks run first |
| 8 | `ReadUptime()` | Returns milliseconds since the kernel's timer started |
| 9 | `CurrentCpu()` | Returns the index of the CPU running the caller; it can change at any preemption |
| 10 | `Open(path, flags)` | Opens a file and returns a descriptor; flags are `OpenRead`, `OpenWrite`, `OpenCreate`, `OpenTruncate`, `OpenAppend`. A symbolic link in the last component is followed (`OpenCreate` makes a dangling link's target), or fails with `SyscallErrorLoop` under `OpenNoFollow` |
| 11 | `Read(descriptor, buffer, length)` | Reads from the descriptor's offset and returns the byte count; `0` at end of file. On descriptors 0–2, returns one console line |
| 12 | `Write(descriptor, buffer, length)` | Writes at the descriptor's offset and returns the byte count, which is short if the disk fills |
| 13 | `Close(descriptor)` | Releases the descriptor |
| 14 | `Seek(descriptor, offset, whence)` | Moves the offset relative to `SeekFromStart`, `SeekFromCurrent` or `SeekFromEnd` (the offset is signed) and returns the new one, which may be at most the file size; `SyscallErrorNotSeekable` on the console |
| 15 | `Remove(path)` | Deletes a file, or a symbolic link itself rather than what it names |
| 16 | `ReadDirectory(path, index, entry)` | Fills a 280-byte `DirectoryEntry` for the index-th entry of a directory (`/` lists `system` first): `node` (the `node` that `Stat` reports, 0 if unknown), `size` (0 for directories), `type` (the mode's type bits shifted down by `EntryTypeShift`, so `d_type`'s values; 0 for an ext2 device node, FIFO or socket) and `name` (up to `MaximumNameLength`, 255 bytes, and a terminator); `SyscallErrorNotFound` past the last entry and for a missing directory (`Stat` tells them apart), `SyscallErrorNotDirectory` for a file |
| 17 | `MakeDirectory(path)` | Creates a directory on the disk |
| 18 | `RemoveDirectory(path)` | Removes an empty directory |
| 19 | `ChangeDirectory(path)` | Sets the caller's working directory |
| 20 | `CurrentDirectory(buffer, capacity)` | Copies the working directory and returns its length |
| 21 | `Shutdown(action)` | Powers off (`ShutdownPowerOff`) or restarts (`ShutdownRestart`); session leaders such as init and the shell only, otherwise `SyscallErrorNotPermitted` |
| 22 | `Stat(path, status)` | Fills a 96-byte `FileStatus`, following a symbolic link in the last component (`LinkStat` describes the link): `device` (a number per mount), `node`, `mode` (type bits `ModeRegular`, `ModeDirectory`, `ModeSymbolicLink`, `ModeCharacterDevice` and the rest, with Linux's values, and permission bits), `link_count`, `owner`, `group`, `size`, `access_time`, `modify_time` and `change_time` (seconds and nanoseconds), `type` (regular, directory, terminal, device, pipe, socket or link, which tells terminals from other character devices) and `read_only` (the filesystem or pipe end cannot be written); `syscall_abi.h` asserts the layout |
| 23 | `StatDescriptor(descriptor, status)` | The same for an open descriptor; pipes, sockets and terminals have device 0, their own node number, and modes `0600`, `0777` and `0620` |
