| 24 | `ProcessId()` | Returns the caller's process ID |
| 25 | `ParentProcessId()` | Returns its parent's process ID, `0` for init |
| 26 | `Duplicate(descriptor, target, flags)` | Makes another descriptor for the same open file: the lowest free one at or above `target`, or exactly `target` with `DuplicateExact` (closing what it named, as `dup2` does); `DuplicateCloseOnExec` marks the copy. A `target` at or above the soft descriptor limit fails with `SyscallErrorBadDescriptor` with `DuplicateExact` and `SyscallErrorInvalidArgument` without it; no free descriptor below the limit is `SyscallErrorTooManyOpenFiles` |
| 27 | `DescriptorFlags(descriptor, set, flags)` | Returns the descriptor's flags (`DescriptorCloseOnExec`), after setting them to `flags` if `set` is nonzero |
| 28 | `Pipe(descriptors, flags)` | Creates a pipe and stores its read end, then its write end, as two `uint64_t`s; `PipeCloseOnExec` marks both, and `PipeNonBlocking` makes both `OpenNonBlocking` |
| 29 | `ProcessGroup(process_id)` | Returns the process group of the process, or of the caller for 0 |
| 30 | `ForegroundGroup(descriptor, set, group)` | Returns the foreground group of the descriptor's terminal, after moving it to `group` if `set` is nonzero. The terminal must be the caller's controlling terminal (`SyscallErrorNotTerminal`), and the group one of its session (`SyscallErrorNotPermitted` otherwise); a caller outside the foreground group gets `SIGTTOU` first, as for `TerminalMode` |
| 31 | `Kill(target, signal)` | Sends `signal` to a process, the caller's group (0), every process but init and the caller (-1), or group `-target`; signal 0 only checks the target exists. init cannot be signaled (`SyscallErrorNotPermitted`) |
| 32 | `SignalAction(signal, new_action, old_action)` | Reads and sets a `SignalAction`: `SignalDefault`, `SignalIgnore`, or a handler with a mask, flags (`SignalResetHandler`, `SignalNoDefer`) and the restorer it returns to |
| 33 | `SignalMask(how, set)` | Blocks, unblocks or sets the blocked signals, and returns the previous mask; `SIGKILL` cannot be blocked |
| 34 | `SignalReturn()` | Made by the restorer: puts back what a handler interrupted |
| 35 | `Pause()` | Waits for a signal, then returns `SyscallErrorInterrupted` |
| 36 | `TerminalSize(descriptor)` | Returns the descriptor's terminal's text rows in bits 32–63 and columns in bits 0–31; for the console the area below the status bar, or 24×80 without a framebuffer. `SyscallErrorNotTerminal` for other descriptors |
| 37 | `TerminalMode(descriptor, new_settings, old_settings)` | Reads and sets the descriptor's terminal's `TerminalSettings`: canonical lines or raw bytes, echo, Ctrl+C and Ctrl+Z as `SIGINT` and `SIGTSTP`, Enter as `\n`, a raw read's minimum and timeout, and whether other output ends a raw read. On the caller's controlling terminal another group's change sends it `SIGTTOU` and fails with `SyscallErrorInterrupted`, starting again once the group continues; one that ignores or blocks `SIGTTOU` goes ahead, and an orphaned group's fails with `SyscallErrorInputOutput`; see Shell And Console Input |
| 38 | `ThreadCreate(entry, first, second)` | Starts a thread at `entry` in the caller's process and returns its ID; its first argument points to `{first, second}` at the top of its new stack (see Threads below) |
| 39 | `ThreadExit(value)` | Ends the calling thread only; `value` is what `ThreadJoin` reports |
| 40 | `ThreadJoin(thread, value)` | Waits for a thread of the process to end, stores its value, and frees its slot; not for the caller itself or the main thread |
| 41 | `ThreadId()` | The calling thread's ID; the main thread is 1 |
| 42 | `FutexWait(address, expected, milliseconds)` | Sleeps while the aligned 32-bit word at `address` holds `expected`, until a `FutexWake` on it, a signal, or the time limit (0 has none); `SyscallErrorWouldBlock` when the word already differs, `SyscallErrorTimedOut` at the limit |
| 43 | `FutexWake(address, count)` | Wakes up to `count` of the word's waiters in the caller's process, oldest first, and returns how many |
| 44 | `ThreadDetach(thread)` | Marks a thread so its slot is freed once it ends, without a join; it can no longer be joined |
| 45 | `Rename(from, to)` | Moves a file, directory or symbolic link (neither name is followed) within the disk or `/tmp`, replacing a file or link at `to`, or an empty directory by a directory, as one filesystem call (atomic on ext2/3/4 and `/tmp`, not on FAT16); a kind mismatch gives `SyscallErrorIsDirectory` or `SyscallErrorNotDirectory`, a non-empty directory `SyscallErrorNotEmpty`; open files (source or target) cannot move (`SyscallErrorBusy`), a directory cannot go inside itself, and another filesystem gives `SyscallErrorCrossDevice` |
| 46 | `Socket(family, type, protocol)` | A socket descriptor: `SocketStream` with protocol 0 or `SocketProtocolTcp` for TCP, or `SocketDatagram` with protocol 0 or `SocketProtocolUdp` for UDP, or `SocketProtocolIcmp` (IPv4) or `SocketProtocolIcmpv6` (IPv6) for ping sockets (Networking below); `SocketCloseOnExec` in `type` marks it close-on-exec, and `SocketNonBlocking` makes it `OpenNonBlocking` |
| 47 | `Bind(descriptor, address)` | Binds a socket to a `SocketAddress`: the unspecified or a local address, and a port, 0 picking a free one |
| 48 | `SendTo(descriptor, message, flags)` | Sends the buffer a `SocketMessage` names to its address, or to the connected peer when the address is 0 (`SyscallErrorDestinationRequired` without one). TCP waits until all of it is buffered and fails with `SyscallErrorBrokenPipe`, raising `SIGPIPE`, once the connection can no longer send; `flags` may hold `SocketMessageNoSignal`, which skips the signal, and `SocketMessageDontWait`, which makes this call non-blocking |
| 49 | `ReceiveFrom(descriptor, message, flags)` | Waits for data and stores the sender at the message's address unless that is 0. A datagram socket copies what fits of one datagram; TCP returns what is buffered, up to 1480 bytes, and 0 at the end of the stream; `SyscallErrorWouldBlock` after the receive timeout; `flags` may only hold `SocketMessageDontWait` |
| 50 | `SetSocketOption(descriptor, option, value)` | `SocketOptionReceiveTimeout` in milliseconds, 0 for none |
| 51 | `NetworkSettings(new_settings, old_settings, changes)` | Stores the interface's `NetworkSettings` in `old_settings` unless it is 0, then applies the parts of `new_settings` that `changes` names: `NetworkChangeIpv4` (address, prefix length, gateway), `NetworkChangeNameServers` (up to 3, port 0 meaning 53) and `NetworkChangeLink` (`link_up` 0 or 1, applied first); any process may change them; `SyscallErrorNetworkUnreachable` without a device, `SyscallErrorNetworkDown` for IPv4 while the link is down, `SyscallErrorFailed` when the device does not start again |
| 52 | `Connect(descriptor, address)` | TCP opens a connection and waits until it is established: `SyscallErrorConnectionRefused` on a reset, `SyscallErrorTimedOut` after 5 unanswered SYN retries (about a minute), `SyscallErrorNetworkUnreachable` without a source address. Non-blocking, it fails with `SyscallErrorInProgress` once the SYN is queued, then `SyscallErrorAlreadyInProgress` until the handshake ends, after which the socket polls writable and `SocketOptionError` holds any error. A datagram socket records the peer, which sends without an address go to and the only one it receives from |
| 53 | `Listen(descriptor, backlog)` | Makes a TCP socket accept connections, binding a free port if none is bound; at most `MaximumListenBacklog` (16) connections wait for `Accept`, and further SYNs are dropped. `SyscallErrorOperationNotSupported` for datagram sockets |
| 54 | `Accept(descriptor, address, flags)` | Waits for an established connection on a listening socket and returns a new descriptor for it, storing the peer's `SocketAddress` unless `address` is 0; `flags` may hold `SocketCloseOnExec` and `SocketNonBlocking`; the receive timeout applies |
| 55 | `ShutdownSocket(descriptor, how)` | `SocketShutdownRead` makes reads return 0, `SocketShutdownWrite` sends FIN once the buffered data is out, `SocketShutdownBoth` does both; `SyscallErrorNotConnected` before a connection or `Connect` |
| 56 | `SocketName(descriptor, address, peer)` | Stores the socket's local address and port, or with `peer` 1 the connected peer's (`SyscallErrorNotConnected` without one) |
| 57 | `Poll(descriptors, count, milliseconds)` | Fills in `returned_events` of `PollDescriptor`s (laid out as `struct pollfd`), at most the soft descriptor limit of them (more fail with `SyscallErrorInvalidArgument`; they go in a heap buffer), and returns how many have events, waiting until one has or the time passes; 0 checks once and `PollForever` has no limit; `SyscallErrorInterrupted` after a signal |
| 58 | `SetClock(nanoseconds)` | Sets the wall clock to a UTC time in nanoseconds since 1970-01-01; `SyscallErrorInvalidArgument` before the timer runs or past 2262 |
| 59 | `FileStatusFlags(descriptor, set, flags)` | Returns the open file's `OpenRead`, `OpenWrite`, `OpenAppend` and `OpenNonBlocking` flags, after setting the last two from `flags` if `set` is nonzero (access flags in `flags` are ignored, others refused). Every descriptor sharing the open file shares them; the console's cannot change. With `OpenNonBlocking`, pipe and socket calls that would wait fail with `SyscallErrorWouldBlock` |
| 60 | `GetSocketOption(descriptor, option, value)` | Stores an option's value as a `uint64_t` at `value`: `SocketOptionReceiveTimeout`, or `SocketOptionError`, a failed TCP connection's error code or a UDP socket's ICMP error, which reading clears; 0 for none |
| 61 | `CreateSession()` | Starts a session and group led by the caller, without a controlling terminal, and returns its ID, the caller's process ID; `SyscallErrorNotPermitted` for a group leader |
| 62 | `SessionId(process_id)` | Returns the session of a process, or of the caller for 0; `SyscallErrorNotFound` if it does not exist |
| 63 | `TerminalControl(descriptor, request, argument)` | On a pseudo-terminal master: `TerminalControlNumber` returns the pair's number N (its slave is `/dev/pts/N`), `TerminalControlLock` locks the slave (argument 1) or unlocks it (0); `SyscallErrorNotTerminal` on other descriptors. `TerminalControlSetSize` also works on any terminal: the argument holds the rows in bits 32-63 and the columns in bits 0-31, each at most 65535, and a change sends `SIGWINCH` to the foreground group |
| 64 | `Mount(source, target, request)` | `request` points to a `MountRequest` (`type`, the name's address, and `flags`, `MountReadOnly`, `MountRemount` or 0; any other bit is `SyscallErrorInvalidArgument`). With `MountRemount` only `target` and `MountReadOnly` count (`source` and `type` may be null): `Files::Remount` changes the mode of the mount at `target`, with `SyscallErrorInvalidArgument` for a path that is not a mount point or `/dev`, `SyscallErrorNotFound`, `SyscallErrorBusy`, `SyscallErrorReadOnly` and `SyscallErrorIo`, as `sys/mount.h` lists. Mounts the filesystem instance named by `type` and `source` (as `/proc/mounts` lists them) at `target`, read-write unless `MountReadOnly` asks or the filesystem refuses writes, which need not exist but must be a directory if it does, in an existing directory. `SyscallErrorUnknownFilesystemType` (`ENODEV`) for an unknown type, `SyscallErrorNotFound` for an unknown source or a missing parent, `SyscallErrorNotDirectory`, `SyscallErrorBusy` if the target is a mount point or the instance is mounted elsewhere, `SyscallErrorNoMemory` when all 16 mounts are in use |
| 65 | `Unmount(target)` | Detaches the filesystem (a journalled one is closed and clean) and removes the mount. `SyscallErrorNotFound` if target does not exist, `SyscallErrorInvalidArgument` if it is not a mount point, `SyscallErrorBusy` while a file on it is open, a process's working directory is inside it or another mount point lies inside it, `SyscallErrorInputOutput` if the sync failed (the mount stays) |
| 66 | `LinkStat(path, status)` | `Stat` without following a symbolic link in the last component, as `lstat`: a link reports `ModeSymbolicLink`, `FileType::SymbolicLink` and its target's length as its size |
| 67 | `ReadLink(path, buffer, capacity)` | Copies a symbolic link's target, cut to `capacity` bytes and without a terminator, and returns how many it copied; `SyscallErrorInvalidArgument` for anything else or a capacity of 0 |
| 68 | `SymbolicLink(target, path)` | Makes `path` (not followed) a link holding `target` as given, which need not exist; in Linux's order `SyscallErrorExists`, `SyscallErrorNotFound` (missing parent or empty target), `SyscallErrorReadOnly`, and `SyscallErrorNotPermitted` where the filesystem holds no links (FAT16, `/system`, `/dev`, `/proc`, as Linux's vfat answers). `/tmp`, `/etc` and ext2 hold them |
| 69 | `Link(from, to)` | Gives the file `from` a second name `to`, following neither: `SyscallErrorCrossDevice` between filesystems, `SyscallErrorNotPermitted` for a directory or where the filesystem holds no hard links (FAT16 and tmpfs, so every writable one until ext2 writes), and otherwise the errors of `SymbolicLink` |
| 70 | `ResolvePath(path, buffer, capacity)` | Copies the physical path of an existing file, with every link, `.`, `..` and repeated `/` resolved, and its terminator, and returns its length (`realpath`); `SyscallErrorRange` if it does not fit |
| 71 | `Sync()` | Syncs every mounted filesystem (`VFS::SyncAll`, one mount at a time under its volume lock), so ext2 writes its counts and marks itself clean and ext3 and ext4 commit their running transaction (no checkpoint); `SyscallErrorInputOutput` if one failed (`sync`) |
| 72 | `ResourceLimit(resource, new_limits, old_limits)` | `prlimit` on the caller for `ResourceOpenFiles` (7, `RLIMIT_NOFILE`); any other resource is `SyscallErrorInvalidArgument`. Stores the old `ResourceLimits {current, maximum}` first (`SyscallErrorBadAddress` for a bad pointer, either may be null), then applies the new ones through `Files::DescriptorLimits`: current above maximum `SyscallErrorInvalidArgument`, maximum above 1024 `SyscallErrorNotPermitted` (`getrlimit`, `setrlimit`, `sysconf(_SC_OPEN_MAX)`, `ulimit`) |
| 73 | `DeviceControl(descriptor, request, argument)` | Requests on `/dev/fb0` (`Kind::Framebuffer`, close-on-exec, never inherited), as libc `ioctl` with `<featheros/framebuffer.h>`: `FramebufferGetInfo` (0x4600, a `FramebufferInfo {width, height, console, dropped_events}`; any time, `console` 1-4 or 0 without a virtual console), `FramebufferAcquire` (0x4601, argument 0; the open file owns the caller's controlling console, which then draws neither text nor status bar until released: `SyscallErrorNotTerminal` for a pty or no terminal, `SyscallErrorBusy`, SIGTTOU for a background caller), `FramebufferPresent` (0x4602, a `FramebufferPresentRequest {pixels, stride, x, y, width, height, flags}` of 0x00RRGGBB pixels, clipped to the screen, row by row through `Console::PresentRow`; `SyscallErrorInvalidArgument` for a non-owner, flags, a size above 16384 or a stride below the width, `SyscallErrorBadAddress` for unreadable pixels, which are all checked before the first row is drawn) and `FramebufferRelease` (0x4603; the last close of the file, exit and kill release too). Another kind of descriptor is `SyscallErrorNotTerminal`, an unknown request `SyscallErrorInvalidArgument`; `write` on the file gives `EINVAL`. `read` on the owner's file returns whole `FramebufferEvent`s (design `docs/design/M32-P2.md`; 8 bytes: `code`, `kind` Release/Press/Repeat/Shown/Hidden, `modifiers`, `time_ms`): up to 64, blocking until one arrives unless the file is `O_NONBLOCK` (`SyscallErrorWouldBlock`), `SyscallErrorInvalidArgument` for a length of 1 to 7 or a file that owns no console, `SyscallErrorInterrupted` for a signal; `poll` reports `PollReadable` while events wait and `PollError` for a non-owner. `FramebufferInfo.dropped_events` counts events the 64-slot queue refused since the acquire. The keys come from `Input::SubmitKey` through `Console::TakeKey` while the shown console is owned (Ctrl+C also sends `SIGINT`; Alt+F1-F4 presses are not queued) |
| 74 | `Eject(disk)` | `disk` is a removable disk's name (`sda`, `/dev/sda` also accepted by the C library; at most 31 characters). `Files::Eject` under the namespace lock, then `VFS::EjectDisk`: the disk is looked up in the registry (`SyscallErrorNotFound` for an unknown or gone one, `SyscallErrorOperationNotSupported` for a non-removable one such as `vda`, 0 for one already ejected); every volume of the disk is marked ejecting (a `mount` of it fails with `SyscallErrorNoDevice`); every mount of those volumes must pass the working-directory check (`AnyWorkingDirectoryWithin`, as `Unmount`) and `MountTable::BeginUnmount`, and one busy mount gives `SyscallErrorBusy` with the begun ones cancelled and nothing unmounted (all or nothing); then each mount is detached (sync plus SYNCHRONIZE CACHE) and removed, a failure giving `SyscallErrorInputOutput` with the finished mounts staying unmounted and the rest cancelled; every volume of the disk is forgotten, `Disk::Flush()` runs once more (a failure is `SyscallErrorInputOutput`: the drive is not stopped and not marked ejected), `Disk::Eject()` sends START STOP UNIT (start 0, LoEj 1 for a removable drive; a refusal is logged as `Storage: sda: the drive refused to stop` and the disk is marked ejected anyway), and `Storage: sda: ejected, safe to unplug` is logged. An ejected disk fails every transfer with `Io`, shows `ejected` in `/proc/partitions`, and `mount` of its volumes gives `SyscallErrorNoDevice` until it is unplugged |

The kernel resolves every user pointer through the caller's page tables before touching it, so unmapped, read-only, or kernel addresses fail with `SyscallErrorBadAddress` instead of faulting. Blocking syscalls re-enable interrupts and yield to other tasks while they wait.

File descriptors live in a per-process table in `src/kernel/files.cpp` (`Files::Table`): a heap array that starts at 32 slots the first time something is put in it and doubles on demand, never past the soft limit in force or 1024, and is freed when the process exits. A new descriptor takes the lowest free number below the soft limit (256 by default, `User::DefaultDescriptorLimit`; the hard limit is 1024, `User::MaximumDescriptorLimit`), and `SyscallErrorTooManyOpenFiles` (`EMFILE`) comes when none is free; `Spawn` hands the child its parent's limits, and `Files::DescriptorLimits` reads and sets them (lowering the soft limit below the open descriptors closes nothing, it only stops new numbers at or above it; syscall 72 and libc's `getrlimit`/`setrlimit` reach it). Disk, `/tmp` and `/etc` files are tracked system-wide in another heap array that starts at 64 entries and doubles to 1024, one entry per distinct file however many descriptors share it; a new distinct file past 1024 fails with `SyscallErrorTooManyFilesInSystem` (`ENFILE`), and a table that cannot grow with `SyscallErrorNoMemory`, in both cases before anything is created. A table whose `Spawn` setup cannot be allocated makes `Spawn` fail with `SpawnStatus::NoResources`. Each names an open file, which keeps the offset and the access mode and closes when its last descriptor does: `Duplicate` and `Spawn` share it, so a copy moves the same offset, as on Unix. A child inherits every descriptor not marked close-on-exec (`OpenCloseOnExec`, `DuplicateCloseOnExec`), so the console reaches it through 0–2 unless the parent put other files there. `OpenAppend` makes every write go to the end, under the filesystem lock. Descriptors 0–2 start as the console, one open file that is readable and writable, like a terminal. A pipe (`src/kernel/pipe.cpp`) is a 4 KiB ring buffer with a read end and a write end. A read waits for data and returns what is there; once every write end is closed, it returns 0. A write waits for room and puts down pieces of up to 512 bytes whole, so writers never interleave within one; with no read end left, it fails with `SyscallErrorBrokenPipe`. Both waits end with `SyscallErrorInterrupted` after Ctrl+C, and neither holds the filesystem lock. An open file that is `OpenNonBlocking` (from `Open`, `PipeNonBlocking`, `SocketNonBlocking`, or later through `FileStatusFlags`) fails with `SyscallErrorWouldBlock` instead of waiting; a non-blocking write puts down the whole pieces that fit. The flag belongs to the open file, so `Duplicate` and `Spawn` share it; on disk files it changes nothing, and the console's cannot be set. Writing prints the bytes as one unit, so another program's output cannot land in the middle; tab prints as a space, bytes above `0x7F` as `?`, and control bytes other than ESC, `\r` and `\b` are dropped. Reading waits for a line, as `ReadLine` does, and only the foreground process may read (`SyscallErrorNotForeground` otherwise). It returns the line with its `\n`, and keeps what does not fit the buffer for the next read. There is no end-of-file key yet. `Seek` on it fails with `SyscallErrorNotSeekable`, and `Duplicate` can put it back on a descriptor that was closed. Paths are at most 1023 characters (`User::MaximumPathLength`, `PATH_MAX`, is 1024 with the terminator), counted as the program passes them, once joined to the working directory and once their symbolic links are expanded, and each component at most 255 (`User::MaximumNameLength`, `NAME_MAX`); longer ones fail with `SyscallErrorNameTooLong`. A path argument is copied into a heap buffer, as are the other path and directory-entry buffers along the syscall, VFS and driver chain, so the 16 KiB kernel stacks hold none of them. The shell's double quotes keep spaces inside one argument (`cat "my notes.txt"`). `/` is the FAT16 disk, and the read-only initramfs's `system/` directory is mounted at `/system`, so `/system/hello.txt` is its `system/hello.txt`. Four virtual filesystems are mounted beside them (see Virtual Files below): `/etc`, a RAM copy of the initramfs's `etc/`, `/dev`, `/proc` and `/tmp`. A disk directory with one of these five names in the root would be hidden by the mount. Every process has a working directory, which starts as its parent's. init starts in `/home`, or in `/` when the disk has none, so the shell does too. Relative paths in file syscalls are joined to it (`Path::Join` in `src/kernel/filesystem/path.cpp`), and the VFS walk then resolves symbolic links, `.`, `..` and repeated slashes (see Path Resolution below), so `/home/..` is `/`. `ChangeDirectory` accepts only existing directories and stores the physical path, without links, but a directory removed afterwards leaves the old path in place, and calls through it then fail. Listing `/` shows `system/`, `etc/`, `dev/`, `proc/` and `tmp/`, followed by the disk's root entries, and `/system` lists its initramfs files. `OpenCreate`, `OpenTruncate`, and `OpenAppend` (start at the end of the file) require `OpenWrite`, and opening an initramfs file for writing fails with `SyscallErrorReadOnly`. A disk, `/tmp` or `/etc` file may have one writer or any number of readers, and an open file cannot be removed; both cases fail with `SyscallErrorBusy`. These rules exist because each descriptor caches the file's size and clusters. When a process exits, including after a fault or Ctrl+C, the kernel closes its descriptors. `Read` and `Write` check the whole user buffer first and then move data through a 512-byte kernel buffer, and a blocking `Sync::Mutex` (`src/kernel/sync/mutex.h`) keeps filesystem calls from different CPUs from interleaving.

`Poll` asks each descriptor for its events: files are always readable and writable as opened; a pipe's read end is readable with data and hangs up without writers, and its write end is writable with 512 bytes free and reports an error without readers; sockets report data, the end of a stream, a waiting connection, buffer space, hang-ups and errors; and the console is writable, and readable for the foreground group when input is waiting (a canonical read may still wait for the rest of the line). Pipes and sockets notify `src/kernel/poll.cpp` whenever their state changes, and a waiting `Poll` checks again after each notification. Console input sends none, so a `Poll` on the console checks again every tick.

Ctrl+C (serial byte `0x03`, or Ctrl+C on the PS/2 or virtio keyboard) interrupts the console's foreground group. Every process belongs to a session and a process group. The kernel starts init in a session of its own without a controlling terminal. A child joins its parent's session, controlling terminal and group unless `Spawn` gets `SpawnSetGroup`, which names another group of the session (the caller's, or one holding one of its children) or, with group 0, starts a new one that the child leads. `SpawnSessionLeader` starts a new session and group led by the child, which takes the terminal at its descriptor 0 as its controlling terminal if no session has it; that is how init's console service, the shell, gets the console. A session leader without a controlling terminal also takes the first terminal it opens, unless it passes `OpenNoControllingTerminal`, and `CreateSession` (`setsid`) moves a process that does not lead a group into a new session without one. `/dev/tty` opens the caller's controlling terminal. When a session leader exits, its terminal no longer belongs to any session. A child's group takes its controlling terminal if the parent's group has it, unless it is spawned with `SpawnBackground`, and on its controlling terminal only the foreground group may read or change the mode; a terminal that is not the caller's controlling terminal has no such check. Another group's read (`Read` or `ReadLine`) sends `SIGTTIN` to its group, and its change of the mode or the foreground group sends `SIGTTOU`; the call fails with `SyscallErrorInterrupted`, which the stop turns into a restart, so the call runs again once the job continues, in the foreground or to stop again. A read that ignores or blocks `SIGTTIN` fails with `SyscallErrorInputOutput` (`EIO`), while a change that ignores or blocks `SIGTTOU` goes ahead, which is how the shell takes the terminal back from a stopped job; an orphaned group gets `SyscallErrorInputOutput` without the signal. `Terminals::DecideBackgroundAccess` makes that choice and `CheckBackgroundAccess` in `process.cpp` applies it. Output from the background is always allowed, as with `stty -tostop`. When the foreground group's last process is reaped, the terminal returns to the group of the process that waited, if it is in the same session. `ForegroundGroup` moves it within the session, as `tcsetpgrp` does. The terminal object (`src/kernel/terminal/terminal.cpp`) keeps the session and foreground group and applies these rules; `process.cpp` keeps each process's session and controlling terminal. Ctrl+C marks every process in the foreground group except session leaders and init, so Ctrl+C at the shell's own prompt only discards the line. The console echoes `^C` and sends `SIGINT` to each process in the group (see Signals below). Ctrl+Z (`0x1A`) does the same with `SIGTSTP` and `^Z`, which stops the group; in raw mode, where `ISIG` is off, it is an ordinary byte, which `RawTerminal` turns into a suspend (see Shell And Console Input). The shell prints nothing for a command `SIGINT` ended. Children never outlive their parent: when a process exits, the kernel sends `SIGKILL` to its remaining descendants and reaps its children before the parent's own exit completes.

### Signals

Each program has pending and blocked signal sets and an action per signal (`src/kernel/signals.cpp`), with Linux's numbers. A signal is sent by `Kill`, by Ctrl+C (`SIGINT`), by a dying parent (`SIGKILL`), by a write to a pipe with no reader (`SIGPIPE`, besides `SyscallErrorBrokenPipe`), by a child's exit (`SIGCHLD` to its parent), and by a terminal size change (`SIGWINCH` to the terminal's foreground group). An ignored signal, or one whose default is to be ignored (`SIGCHLD`, `SIGCONT`, `SIGWINCH` and signal 23), is dropped when sent; the others stay pending until the program returns to user mode, after a syscall or when a timer tick preempts it. Blocking calls (`Sleep`, `Wait`, `Pause`, console and pipe reads, pipe writes) give up with `SyscallErrorInterrupted` once a signal they would take is pending. The program then takes the lowest pending signal it does not block. The default action ends it with exit status `ExitStatusSignalBase + signal`, which `waitpid` reports as `WIFSIGNALED`. A handler runs on the program's own stack: the kernel saves the interrupted registers, its floating-point state (FXSAVE, or q0-q31 with FPCR and FPSR) and the signal mask in a frame below the stack pointer, past a 128-byte red zone, blocks the signal and the action's mask, and enters the handler with the signal number as its argument, returning to the restorer the C library registered. The restorer makes `SignalReturn`, which puts everything back, refusing kernel addresses, flags outside the user-changeable ones, and MXCSR or FPCR/FPSR bits that would fault or are undefined; a frame that cannot be written or read ends the program with `SIGSEGV`. CPU exceptions still end a program as `SIGSEGV` without running a handler. Children start with every handler back at its default action, while ignored signals and the blocked mask carry over, as across exec.

The stop signals `SIGSTOP` (19, which cannot be handled, blocked or ignored), `SIGTSTP`, `SIGTTIN` and `SIGTTOU` (20-22) stop the process by default. The thread that takes one, through `Process::TakeSignal`, stops the process under the process lock that `SIGCONT` also takes, so a `SIGCONT` sent between taking the signal and stopping is not lost. It marks the process and every thread context `stopped`, records the report for the parent and sends it `SIGCHLD`, waking all of its threads. On every return to user mode `Process::HoldWhileStopped` keeps a stopped thread waiting on the process's `resumed` queue, so each thread stops the next time it would run user code; x86-64 restores the TSS kernel stack afterwards. `SIGCONT` continues the process as it is sent, whatever its action, wakes the queue and reports to the parent; `SIGKILL` releases a stopped thread so it can end. Sending a stop signal drops a pending `SIGCONT` and the reverse. `SIGTSTP`, `SIGTTIN` and `SIGTTOU` are dropped when they would stop a process whose group is orphaned: no member has a parent in another group of the same session, as for a session leader. A blocking syscall that failed with `SyscallErrorInterrupted` only because of a stop starts again: the architecture code moves the return address back over `int $0x80` or `svc #0` and puts back the syscall number and first argument, which a call may change, as `Sleep` does to the milliseconds left (`UserExecutionContext::restart_argument`). `Wait` with `WaitStopped` or `WaitContinued` reports each change once; `/proc/<pid>/status` and `ps` show a stopped process as `T (stopped)`.

### Path Resolution

Every path a file syscall takes is walked by `VFS::ResolvePath` (`src/kernel/filesystem/path_walk.cpp`) into its physical form, without symbolic links, `.`, `..` or repeated `/`, which is what the other VFS calls and the drivers take; no driver follows a link itself. `Files` (`src/kernel/files.cpp`) walks under the filesystem lock, so the walk and the operation see the same tree. The syscall layer only joins a relative path to the working directory (`Path::Join`), applying its leading `.` and `..` there: the working directory is physical, so its textual parent is its real one.

- **Links:** a link in the middle of a path is always followed, across mounts: an absolute target restarts at `/`, a relative one goes on from the link's directory. A link in the last component is followed by `Open`, `Stat`, `ChangeDirectory`, `ReadDirectory`, `Spawn`, `Mount`, `Unmount` and `ResolvePath`, and not by `LinkStat`, `ReadLink`, `Remove`, `RemoveDirectory`, `MakeDirectory`, `Rename`, `SymbolicLink` and `Link` (both of its names, as Linux's `link`); a trailing `/` always follows. After 40 links (`User::MaximumSymbolicLinks`, Linux's `MAXSYMLINKS`) the walk fails with `SyscallErrorLoop` (`ELOOP`).
- **`..`:** the walk keeps the physical path resolved so far and drops its last component, so `..` after a link leaves the target's directory, not the link's (POSIX's physical walk; `link/..` is the parent of what `link` names), and leaving a mount's root goes to its mount point's parent. `..` needs an existing directory (`ENOENT` or `ENOTDIR`); other missing components are left for the operation to report.
- **Cost and limits:** the walk keeps its three path buffers in one 3 KiB heap object and counts links instead of recursing, so its stack use is fixed. Components are checked for links together, at the end, before `..` and before an unfollowed last component, by `VFS::FirstLink`, which asks each mount's driver about its part (`Driver::FindLink`); so a path without links costs one extra driver walk per mount it crosses, and drivers that hold no links (`Driver::SupportsLinks`) answer at once. A resolved path, and a link's target together with the rest of the path still to walk, must fit in `PATH_MAX`, or the walk fails with `ENAMETOOLONG`.
- **Drivers:** `Driver::ReadLink`, `SymbolicLink` and `Link` take paths inside the mount and act on the link itself. ext2 (`CoreDriver`, through the core's `ReadLink`) reads fast and slow links; tmpfs stores a link's target as its data, which counts toward its 4 MiB, and holds no hard links, since a node keeps its own name.

### Virtual Files

The VFS (`src/kernel/filesystem/vfs.cpp`) sends each path through a mount table (`VFS::MountTable` in `mount_table.cpp`, 16 entries, no allocation, under a spinlock): the mount with the longest mount point that is a whole-component prefix of the path holds it, so `/tmpx` is on the disk and `/tmp/a` in `/tmp`. Each filesystem is a driver (`Filesystem::Driver` in `filesystem.h`, the built-in ones in `drivers.cpp`) that takes paths relative to its mount point, one instance per mount, and an open file (`VFS::FileHandle`) keeps its driver and a driver-owned node (a 64-bit ID that also keys the open-file rules, plus a payload). At boot `VFS::MountBuiltIn` mounts `/system`, `/etc`, `/dev`, `/proc` and `/tmp`, and `VFS::MountBlockDevice` the FAT16 volume at `/`; "/" exists, listing the other mount points, even before the disk is mounted. A directory lists the mount points directly inside it first, then its own entries. Bare names without a leading `/`, which kernel code uses for programs (`init`), name files in `/system`.

`Stat` and directory entries take what each driver knows (`Filesystem::FileInfo` and `Filesystem::DirectoryEntry` in `filesystem.h`; `StatNode` answers for an open file). Every mount gets a device number of its own when it is added (`MountTable::DeviceOf`, `st_dev`), and a mount point listed in its parent carries its filesystem's root node. `CoreDriver` maps the shared core's attributes: ext2 gives its real mode, owner, group, link count and times, links show as `S_IFLNK` (and fail to open, since nothing follows them yet), and a device node, FIFO or socket has no type bits, since the core calls them all special. FAT16 reports `0644` and `0755` (`0444` and `0555` on a read-only mount), one link, owner 0 and zero times. The virtual filesystems use fixed modes and one link: `/system` and `/proc` `0444` files and `0555` directories, `/tmp` and `/etc` `0644` and `0755`, `/dev` devices `S_IFCHR` `0666`; their node numbers are the archive data's address (`/system`), the tmpfs node's index plus one, a number from the device's node (`/dev`), and fixed numbers per file and process (`/proc`).

Each driver instance is registered under a type and a source name (`VFS::TypeRegistry` in `type_registry.cpp`, filled by `MountBuiltIn` and `MountBlockDevice`): `initramfs` `initramfs` at `/system`, `tmpfs` `etc` at `/etc` (unmounted again once a writable root supplies its own `/etc`), `devfs` `devfs` at `/dev`, `procfs` `procfs` at `/proc`, `tmpfs` `tmp` at `/tmp`, and `ext4` `vda1` at `/`, plus `fat16` `vda2`, `ext3` `vda3`, `ext4` `vda5` and `ext2` `vda6`, registered but not mounted (on a disk whose FAT16 partition comes first, `fat16` is the root and `ext4` the registered one). `vda1` and `vda2` are the names Linux gives the first virtio disk's partitions; FeatherOS has no disk node in `/dev` yet. A driver has one name, so `MountBlockDevice` calls `TypeRegistry::Unregister` and then `Register` for each slot when a disk's partitions change (`TypeRegistry::Capacity` is 32: the five built-ins and sixteen volumes). Each partition has its own driver, which keeps its contents while unmounted, so `VFS::Mount(type, source, point)` mounts that partition's, several volumes of one type can be mounted at once, and a volume is mounted at one point at most (`EBUSY`, the root's partition too; Linux allows more). `VFS::Unmount` refuses (`MountResult::Busy`) while the mount has users or another mount point lies inside it; it then marks the mount, calls the driver's `Detach` (`Sync` by default; FAT16 writes every sector through, so its `Sync` is `BlockDevice::Flush`; an ext3 or ext4 mount closes its journal, as `CoreOptions::close_on_detach` asks, and reopens the core read-only, so the partition is clean) without holding the table's spinlock, and removes the entry, or keeps it if that failed. At shutdown `Files::Quiesce` calls `VFS::DetachAll` for every mount.

The writeback task (`src/kernel/writeback.cpp`, started by `RunConsole`) wakes every 5 s (`Writeback::IntervalSeconds`, fixed) and calls `Files::SyncPeriodic`, which syncs, one at a time under its volume lock, each mount whose driver says `SyncsInBackground()`: ext2, ext3 and ext4 (`CoreOptions::periodic_sync`) while writable in the core. For ext3/ext4 that commits the running transaction, so a change reaches the log within about 5 s; for ext2 it writes the counts and marks the volume clean (the core clears the bit again before the next change). Idle passes write nothing. FAT16, tmpfs and the virtual filesystems are not synced. A stop from outside therefore leaves ext3/ext4 with a journal that needs recovery (`e2fsck -fy` on a copy, or a writable FeatherOS mount, replays it) and an ext2 volume changed less than 5 s earlier not clean. A mount's users are counted in the table: every VFS call holds its mount from lookup until the driver call returns (`MountTable::Acquire`/`Release`), and an open `VFS::FileHandle` holds it until `Close`. A lookup never starts on a mount being unmounted, nor falls through to its parent, so nothing reaches the driver between the busy check and the removal. `Files::Unmount` adds the working-directory rule: under the filesystem lock it asks `process.cpp` whether any process's working directory is the mount point or inside it, and `chdir` (`Files::ChangeDirectory`) checks and stores the new directory under the same lock. Terminal descriptors opened through `/dev` hold no VFS handle, so they do not keep `/dev` busy.

- **`/dev`** (`devices.cpp`) holds `null`, whose reads end at once; `zero`, which reads as zero bytes; and `random`, which reads from `Random::Next`, mixing `RDRAND`/`RNDR` with the cycle counter.
  - **`/dev/mouse`** (`Files::Kind::PointerEvents`, `src/kernel/input/pointer.{h,cpp}`): opening it gives a cursor into `Input::Pointer`'s ring of 256 `User::InputEvent` records (Linux's `struct input_event`, 24 bytes; `<featheros/input.h>`). A producer submits one report at a time, ending in `SYN_REPORT`, which the ring keeps whole: a report that does not fit drops the oldest whole reports first. `Read` copies whole events from the open file's cursor (below 24 bytes is `EINVAL`), waits on a wait queue unless `O_NONBLOCK` (`EAGAIN`) and stops with `EINTR` for a signal; a cursor that fell behind the oldest report gets one `SYN_DROPPED` and moves to that report's start. A new cursor starts at the end. `poll` is readable while the cursor is behind; every `Submit` wakes the readers and calls `Poll::Notify`. Writes are discarded and seeking fails. `pointer_lock` (an interrupt-saving spinlock, a leaf) guards the ring and every cursor. `mousetest [n]` prints n events as `mouse: rel x 10`, `mouse: key 272 1`, `mouse: syn` (`mouse: dropped`), after `mousetest: ready`. All three take and discard writes, report size 0 and file type `Device` (`S_IFCHR`), and accept any seek. `tty` opens the caller's controlling terminal, and `tty1` to `tty4` the four virtual consoles (see the status bar section). `ptmx` and the `pts/` directory are pseudo-terminals (see below). Nothing else can be created in `/dev`.
  - **Pseudo-terminals** (`src/kernel/terminal/pseudo_terminal.cpp`): opening `/dev/ptmx` claims one of 16 pairs and returns its master, a descriptor of its own kind (`Files::Kind::PseudoTerminalMaster`, file type `Device`). The slave, `/dev/pts/N`, is a `Terminals::Terminal` like the console, listed in `/dev/pts` while the pair is in use. It starts locked, and opening it fails with `SyscallErrorInputOutput` (`EIO`) until `TerminalControl` unlocks it, or once the master is closed. Bytes written to the master go through the slave's line discipline as typed input (`Terminal::ReceiveAvailable`: Ctrl+C and Ctrl+Z become `SIGINT` and `SIGTSTP` for the foreground group, and a write waits while the 256-byte input queue is full); program output written to the slave goes into a 4 KiB pipe the master reads, and waits while it is full. Echo that does not fit is dropped. The pair is freed once the master and every slave descriptor (including `/dev/tty` on it) are closed, and its number is then used again. The master may be `O_NONBLOCK`, and `poll` reports it readable while output waits and writable while the input queue has room. `ioctl(TIOCSWINSZ)` on either end (`TerminalControlSetSize`) sets the slave's size, which `TerminalSize` also reads through the master, and a change sends `SIGWINCH` to the slave's foreground group. Closing the master hangs the slave up (`Terminal::HangUp`): queued input is dropped, the terminal is no longer its session's controlling terminal, every process of that session gets `SIGHUP` (`ProcessHooks::hang_up_session`), slave reads return 0 at once, slave writes, `featheros_read_line` and a writer waiting for room fail with `SyscallErrorInputOutput` (`EIO`), and `poll` reports the slave readable, writable and hung up. Each slave descriptor holds a write end of the output pipe (`Pipes::AddWriter`), so once every slave descriptor that was opened is closed, master reads return what is left and then 0, and `poll` adds `PollHangUp`; before the slave first opens, and again after it is reopened, master reads wait. When a process exits, the settings it left on any terminal, a pty's or the console's, are restored.
- **`/tmp`** (`tmpfs.cpp`) is a RAM filesystem with nested directories, symbolic links (`S_IFLNK` `0777`, no hard links) and case-sensitive names, up to 255 bytes. It holds at most 128 files, directories and links and 4 MiB of data and link targets, on the kernel heap, and a write past that is short. Its contents last until restart. With no disk the same RAM filesystem also holds `/` (a third root, `Tmpfs::Root::Ram`, with an empty `/home`; see "Booting without a disk" below) and shares these limits. Open files follow the disk's rules (one writer or many readers, and no removing an open file), and `rmdir /tmp` fails with `SyscallErrorBusy`, like `/`.
- **Booting without a disk** (M14 Part 5 step 10b): when `BlockDevice::Initialize` finds no virtio-blk device (or one under 2 sectors), `MountAndVerifyDisk` (`kernel_runtime.cpp`) logs `Storage: no disk; running from the initramfs` and calls `VFS::MountRamRoot`, which mounts the RAM filesystem's third root at `/` (`/proc/mounts`: `root / tmpfs rw 0 0`) and creates `/home`. Everything else is as on a read-only root: `/etc` is the initramfs's RAM copy filled by `VFS::LoadEtc`, `/sbin/init` does not exist so the kernel starts `/system/init`, and `PATH` (`/bin:/sbin:/system`) finds every program's rescue copy in `/system`. Nothing is persistent, and `/home`, `/tmp` and `/etc` share the tmpfs limits (128 entries, 4 MiB). `mount -t ext4 vda1 /mnt` and the like fail with an unknown source, `sync` has nothing to write, the writeback task idles, and shutdown logs `Shutdown: no disk to flush`. A disk whose partitions hold no FAT16 or ext volume still panics. `NODISK_TEST` checks it.
- **`/etc`** is the root's own directory when the root is writable (M29 Part 2): after the root is mounted, `VFS::UseRootEtc` (called from `MountAndVerifyDisk` in `kernel_runtime.cpp`, logging `VFS: /etc is the root's own directory`) takes the RAM copy's mount away, creates `/etc` on the root if it is missing and copies each file of `initramfs/etc/` that the root lacks, overwriting nothing, so edits to `/etc/hosts` and `/etc/resolv.conf` survive a reboot and a deleted default comes back at the next boot. With a read-only root, or when the copy fails, `/etc` stays the RAM copy: the same tmpfs second root that shares `/tmp`'s limits, filled by `VFS::LoadEtc` from the initramfs at every boot, whose edits are lost at reboot (see [ideas.md](ideas.md), "A persistent `/etc`"). `/proc/mounts` lists `tmpfs etc /etc` only in that case. `STORAGE` checks it in `CheckEtc` (`kernel_test.cpp`): the defaults, an edit appended to both files on boot 1, the edits kept and a deleted `resolv.conf` restored by a second `UseRootEtc` on boot 2, and (M30 Part 4) no `init.conf` by default, with `/etc/init.d/storage` and `/etc/init.disabled` written on boot 1 (init runs the service and skips dhcp at the end of both boots, which the harness checks in the serial output) and unchanged on boot 2; the harness reads them back with `debugfs` (`VerifyRootEtc` in `tools/smoke/cases/storage.py`), and `ROOT_TEST` checks that a root with no `/etc` gets the defaults and no `init.conf`.
- **`/proc`** (`procfs.cpp`) is read-only. It lists `meminfo`, `stat`, `mounts`, `usb`, `kmsg`, and one directory per process, by ascending ID, each holding `status`. A file's text is made when it is opened, so a reader sees one snapshot.
  - `status` gives `Name`, `State` (`R`, `S` or `Z`, as on Linux), `Pid`, `PPid`, `Pgid`, `Pages` mapped, `PageLimit`, `OpenFiles`, `CpuTimeMs` (the timer ticks the process ran for, on any CPU), `LastCpu` and `StartTimeMs` (uptime when it was spawned).
  - `stat` gives `UptimeMs`, `Cpus`, and each CPU's busy time as `Cpu<n>BusyMs`, the ticks it ran a task rather than idling.
  - `meminfo` gives `MemTotal` and `MemFree` from the page allocator, the kernel heap's mapped size (`KernelHeap`) and the memory `/tmp` holds (`Tmp`) and the page cache's size (`Cached`, 4 KiB per cached page, from `BlockDevice::CachedBytes()`), in kB.
  - `mounts` has one line per mount in mount order, as on Linux: `<source> <point> <type> <ro|rw> 0 0`, such as `tmp /tmp tmpfs rw 0 0` (from `VFS::DescribeMount`).
  - `usb` has one line per attached USB device, in bus and device-number order, such as `1-1.2 dev 3 full 0627:0001 class 03/01/01 hid-keyboard`: the path (`<bus>-<root port>` and `.<hub port>` for each hub below it), the device number (the lowest free one from 2 on its controller; the root hub is 1), `low`/`full`/`high`/`super`/`super+`, vendor:product, the class, subclass and protocol of the first interface, and the drivers bound to its interfaces, comma-separated, or `unbound`. It comes from `Usb::FormatDevices` under `topology_lock` and ends at the last whole line that fits the 1 KiB file; empty without a USB controller.
  - `kmsg` is a snapshot of the kernel log: the newest lines of a 64 KiB ring (`src/kernel/log_ring.{h,cpp}`, `KernelLog::Append`/`Snapshot`, a `Sync::SpinLock` taken with interrupts off, so any CPU and interrupt handlers can log), oldest first, every line ending in a newline. Every `KLogger` line (`KERN/<level>: ...`) goes in, as do lines written straight through `Console::WriteLine` with a `KERN/` prefix (`power.cpp`, the network link) and `CPU n online`; the panic and exception reports, which write to the serial port only so as never to wait for the lock, and the test hooks' `KERN/<MODE>:` lines do not. A full ring drops its oldest whole lines to take a new one, and a line longer than the ring is cut to 64 KiB minus 1. Unlike Linux's `/proc/kmsg` it does not consume: every open starts at the oldest line still held, and `stat` reports size 0 (the text is made at open into a 64 KiB + 1 buffer, `Procfs::FileCapacity`).
  - The file system takes its data from `process.cpp` through callbacks (`Procfs::SetSource`), so the host tests can supply their own.
  - `/proc/self` is rewritten to `/proc/<pid>` when a syscall joins a path, before the walk (`Path::ReplaceProcSelf`); it is not a link, so a link pointing to `/proc/self` is not rewritten.

### Device registers

`Memory::MapDevice(physical, size)` (`src/kernel/memory/device_memory.cpp`) maps device registers uncached into the kernel half at `0xFFFFF00000000000 + physical`, so drivers reach them whichever address space is active. `PageFlags::Device` selects Device-nGnRnE memory on AArch64 and `PCD`/`PWT` on x86-64. The AArch64 UART, PL031 RTC, GICv2, and virtio-mmio devices, and the x86-64 local APIC, all use it. The AArch64 UART uses Limine's identity map only until virtual memory is initialized, so the earliest panics still print.

### PCI interrupts and DMA

`Pci::EnableMsiX` and `Pci::EnableMsi` (`src/kernel/pci/msi.{h,cpp}`) mask the function, program the message (the MSI-X entry is written masked, then unmasked, or the MSI address, data and per-vector mask), set INTx disable in the command register and only then set the enable bit; bus mastering stays with `Pci::EnableDevice`. `Arch::Msi::Allocate(handler, context, &message)` (`src/kernel/arch/msi.h`) hands out one message and registers `handler(context)` before it returns; the handler runs in interrupt context on the boot CPU. On x86-64, MSIs use vectors 50-57 (`exceptions.asm` stubs up to 57, `arch/x86_64/msi.cpp`): the address is `0xFEE00000 | (boot CPU APIC ID << 12)`, the data the vector (fixed delivery, edge), and the handler is called after a local APIC EOI, never a PIC EOI. On AArch64 the MSI controller is the GICv2m frame the host bridge's `msi-parent` or `msi-map` names (`arch/aarch64/gicv2m.cpp`; `TYPER` gives the first SPI and the count, at most 16 are used): the address is the frame plus `0x40`, the data the SPI ID, and the SPI is configured edge-triggered (`AArch64::ConfigureEdge`) and routed to the boot CPU before its handler is enabled; `ConfigureLevel` and `Arch::Msi::RegisterIntx(irq, handler, context)` do the same level-triggered for the INTx SPI `Arch::Pci::RouteIntx` finds in the `interrupt-map` (one handler per SPI). Context handlers (`RegisterInterruptHandler(id, handler, context)`) sit beside the plain ones in `interrupts.cpp`. The order of preference for a driver is MSI-X, MSI, INTx on AArch64, polling.

`Memory::Dma::Allocate(bytes, below, &buffer)` (`src/kernel/memory/dma.{h,cpp}`) returns zeroed, physically contiguous pages through the HHDM, entirely below `below` (an exclusive physical address: 4 GiB for a controller without 64-bit addressing, 256 MiB for `edu`), by `AllocateContiguousPageFrames(count, &physical, limit)` (which, like every contiguous allocation, does not ask the page cache to give frames back); `Dma::Free` returns the pages. `Dma::ProducerBarrier()` (`dmb oshst` on AArch64, a compiler barrier on x86-64) goes before a doorbell write or a cycle-bit handover and `Dma::ConsumerBarrier()` (`dmb oshld`) after seeing a descriptor the device handed back. PCI DMA is assumed cache-coherent: AArch64 refuses a host bridge without `dma-coherent`.

### USB locking

The xHCI driver (`src/kernel/usb/`) has three kinds of caller: the `UsbEvents` task (High priority, one per controller; drains the event ring and completes commands and transfers, never blocks on a transfer and never takes `topology_lock`), the `UsbManager` task (Normal; enumerates, configures and detaches, and blocks waiting for commands and transfers) and the interrupt top half (clears `IMAN.IP` and `USBSTS.EINT`, sets a flag and wakes `UsbEvents`; it touches no ring). The locks, in the order they are taken (nothing takes an earlier one while holding a later one):

1. The `/proc` volume lock, held only by readers of `/proc/usb` (step 5).
2. `Usb::topology_lock` (`Sync::Mutex`): the device tree, bindings and numbering; held by the manager during attach and detach and by `/proc/usb`, never by the event task (step 5).
3. `Controller::command_lock_` (`Sync::Mutex`): one command at a time, held from submission to completion or abort; only the manager takes it.
4. `Controller::ring_lock_` (an interrupt-saving spinlock): the command ring's enqueue state, the pending-command table, every endpoint's transfer ring, the pending-transfer table and the doorbell writes; taken by submitters and by the event task when it retires a TRB. No allocation, logging, waiting or `Input` call under it.
5. Spinlocks that only wake or queue: the hardware's completion lock and wait queue (`Hardware::WaitUntil`/`WakeWaiters`), its event lock and wait queue (`SignalEvents`/`WaitForEvents`), `Controller::event_lock_` (held by `ProcessEvents` while it drains; `Restart` takes it once to wait for a drain), the controller's `work_lock_` (the port-work bitmap) and the manager's lock and wait queue in `usb.cpp`. `Usb::Bus::work_lock_` (the hub-work ring the event task fills and the manager empties; the wake hook runs after it is released).
6. Leaves: the HID driver's locks (`registry_lock` over the port table and each port's `lock`, taken after the USB locks and never held across an `Input` call or a control transfer; the per-port lock and the registry's are never nested) and `Input::Pointer`'s `pointer_lock` (a spinlock that saves interrupt state; `Submit` takes it, then wakes readers and calls `Poll::Notify` after releasing it), and the heap.

The device tree (`usb/device.{h,cpp}`) is one `Usb::Bus` per controller, changed only by that controller's manager task under `topology_lock`. Attaching reads the descriptors first, with no lock and nothing visible, then takes the lock to number the device, bind a driver to each interface that one matches (`usb/driver.h`), send `Configure Endpoint` and `SET_CONFIGURATION` and call each driver's `attach`; detaching goes children first, then sets `detaching` (the device refuses new submissions), stops its endpoints so pending transfers complete `Cancelled` (their callbacks still run), waits until the device's `in_flight` count is 0, bumps its `generation`, calls each driver's `detach` and only then disables the slot. A driver submits through `Device::Submit(DeviceTransfer&)`, never `HostController::Submit`; the core counts the transfer and drops its callback if the generation changed, so a driver's state stays valid until detach has waited for the transfers, and a driver must not resubmit after `Cancelled` or `NoDevice`. Logging happens after `topology_lock` is released, and the event task takes no lock of the tree.

A driver's optional `service` (`usb/driver.h`) is how a driver asks the manager task for blocking work: the driver (typically its completion callback or a waiting task) calls `Device::PostWork()`, and `Manager::HandleDeviceWork` later calls `service(device, state)` on the manager task for every interface of that device whose driver has one, after dropping work for a missing, detaching or other-generation device. It runs with **no lock held**, as the hub's work does: only the manager changes `Device::drivers` and `driver_state`, and attach and detach run on that same task, so they cannot change under it. Because it is the manager, it may block in `HostController::CancelEndpoint`, `ClearHalt` and `Control`; it must never take a driver's I/O lock or wait for a task that holds one (the manager has to stay free to recover endpoints). A `true` return asks for a port reset: `Manager::Reenumerate` (still on the manager task, no lock held but the `topology_lock` inside `Bus::Detach`) detaches the device, resets its port (`ResetRootPort` for a root port, a synthetic `RequestReset` event through the hub's `HubPort` state machine for a hub port) and enumerates it again as a new device; a port that asks again within 60 s (`PortResetWindowMs`) is left detached and disabled until its connection changes. `Driver::stalls_are_protocol` makes `Manager::OnRecovery` ignore stalls on that driver's endpoints when it counts recoveries towards unbinding (`UnbindRecoveries` in one second), as a Bulk-Only drive stalls as part of its protocol; other errors still count.

Completion callbacks of transfers (`Transfer::done`) run from `ProcessEvents` after `event_lock_` is released, with no USB lock held, except that a transfer that was cancelled (`StopEndpoints`), failed and recovered (`ServiceEndpoints`) or lost its slot (`DestroyDevice`) completes on the task that did that, the manager, in the last case under `topology_lock`; a driver's callback must therefore tolerate running on either task, and must not call `Input` for `Cancelled` or `NoDevice` (the HID driver releases its keys in `detach`, still an `Input` call under `topology_lock`, which is safe because nothing under `Input` takes it); `finished` is set before the callback, which may `Submit` the same transfer again. A transfer error (stall, babble, transaction error) only flags the endpoint in the event task; the manager task recovers it (`Reset Endpoint`, `Set TR Dequeue Pointer`, `CLEAR_FEATURE(ENDPOINT_HALT)` unless EP0) in `Wait`, `Control` or `Manager::Run`, and a halted endpoint refuses `Submit` until then. Pending commands are keyed by the command TRB's physical address and pending transfers by slot, endpoint and the TD's TRB range; a completion that names none is ignored, and `ProcessEvents` is the only reader of the event ring (the manager reaches it only through `Restart`, which first sets `quiesced_` and waits for a drain).

The HID driver (`src/kernel/usb/hid/driver.cpp`) binds `hid-keyboard` (class 03, subclass 1, protocol 1), `hid-mouse` (protocol 2) and `hid` (every other class-03 interface with an interrupt IN endpoint), in that order. A boot keyboard is switched to the boot protocol and read with the fixed layout; `usbhid=report` (`KERNEL_USBHID=report`) makes it use the report protocol and the parsed descriptor too. A mouse always starts with the report protocol and its parsed descriptor, and both fall back to the boot layout if the descriptor is refused or has no keyboard or mouse fields. Each keyboard gets its own `Input::Keyboard`, so Shift is per keyboard and Caps and Num Lock are global. A report becomes `KeyEvent`s (releases, then presses) in the port's queue; one task at a time drains the queue into `Input::SubmitKey` with no lock held, so the event task, a failure completing on the manager and a repeat never reorder one keyboard's keys. The `Repeater` (500 ms, then 33 ms, in milliseconds of `HostController::NowMilliseconds`) is advanced by `UsbEvents` (`Hid::ServiceRepeat`), whose sleep ends at `Hid::NextRepeat`. A lock change reaches `usb.cpp`'s `OnLockChange` from wherever the key was handled (`Keyboard::Press`, perhaps an interrupt handler), which only wakes the manager; the manager then calls `Hid::SendLeds`. The driver holds at most 32 interface ports; a 33rd fails to attach. A mouse report (`ExtractMouse`) becomes `EV_KEY` events for the buttons 1-8 that changed against the port's previous report (`BTN_LEFT` 0x110 up to `BTN_TASK`), then `REL_X`, `REL_Y`, `REL_WHEEL` and `REL_HWHEEL` when non-zero, then `SYN_REPORT`, submitted to `Input::Pointer` under the port's `mouse_lock` so reports of one mouse never reorder; a report with no change submits nothing, and a failed transfer or a detach submits the release of every held button. A mouse in the boot protocol has no wheel (boot reports are three bytes), which is why mice use the report protocol by default.

Hubs (`src/kernel/usb/hub.{h,cpp}`, `hub_state.{h,cpp}`; the driver is `hub`, class 09 with an interrupt IN endpoint, first in `BuiltInDrivers()`): attaching, on the manager task under `topology_lock` like every driver, reads the hub descriptor (0x29, or 0x2A on a SuperSpeed hub), sends `SET_HUB_DEPTH` with the hub's `Location::depth` (0 for a hub on a root port, which is Linux's level minus 1) to a USB 3 hub, selects alternate setting 1 of a high-speed hub with `bDeviceProtocol` 2 (multi-TT), sends a second `Configure Endpoint` that adds no endpoint but sets the slot context's Hub, port count, TT think time and MTT (the hub class requests are valid only once the device is configured, so these fields cannot ride in the first command, which carries the status-change endpoint), powers the first 15 ports (a route string holds one nibble per tier) and waits `bPwrOn2PwrGood` x 2 ms. The status-change completion runs on the event task and only ORs the bitmap into the hub's `pending` word and posts `{device number, generation}` to `Bus`'s work ring (`Device::PostWork`, `SetWorkWake` makes the kernel wake the manager); it never takes `topology_lock`. The manager (`Manager::HandleDeviceWork`, then `HandleHub`) drops work whose generation is gone, runs `HubPort::Step` per changed port (`GET_STATUS`, clear every change bit the hub defines, `SET_FEATURE(PORT_RESET)` after a 100 ms debounce, poll for `C_PORT_RESET` up to 500 ms, enumerate with the speed from the status bits or SuperSpeed, over-current switches the port off) and only then resubmits the transfer. A child's `Location` comes from `HubPort::ChildLocation`: route = hub's route | port << 4 x hub depth, depth at most 5, ports 1 to 15, the root port inherited, and for a low- or full-speed device the nearest high-speed hub as TT hub (its port when the hub is multi-TT, else 1; a device below a full-speed hub below a high-speed hub keeps the high-speed hub's). Names are the hub's name, a dot and the port (`1-5.2`). Detaching a hub detaches its children first, recursively (`Bus::Detach`, `Bus::DropAll` after a controller restart). Host tests are `tests/unit/kernel/usb/hub_tests.cpp` (tag `[hub]`) over a scripted hub in `fake_device.{h,cpp}` (`ScriptedHub`, USB 2 and USB 3) that the fake controller finds by root port and route string. `USB_HUB_TEST=ON` (`--expect-usb-hub`, `tools/smoke/cases/usb_hub.py`, matrix cases 61 and 62) puts QEMU's `usb-hub` (full-speed, 8 ports) on USB 2 port 1 with the `usb-kbd` and `usb-mouse` on its ports 1 and 2 (`port=1.1`, `port=1.2`; root port 5, so `USB 1-5.1` and `1-5.2`): the typing and `mousetest` steps of `USB_KEYS_TEST` run through the hub, then a keyboard is hot-plugged into hub port 3 with QMP `device_add`, typed on and removed three times, with `lsusb` listing the same three devices before and after.

### Storage locking

The USB mass-storage drive (`src/kernel/usb/storage/drive.{h,cpp}`, `driver.{h,cpp}`) and the storage worker (`src/kernel/storage/worker.{h,cpp}`) add these locks. Nothing takes an earlier lock while holding a later one. `Storage::StartControllers` (`src/kernel/storage/controllers.{h,cpp}`) starts the worker and sets the drive's `Environment`; `MountAndVerifyDisk` calls it after the timer starts and before the root is mounted, so `usb=off` still has the worker; it refuses, with a log line, while `Time::TimerRunning()` is false (the wait that follows ends only by a tick). It also walks PCI once for NVMe (class 01/08/02), AHCI (01/06/01) and RAID-mode SATA controllers (01/04, which only logs a hint); each NVMe and AHCI probe posts its bring-up to the worker and registers it in `Storage::ProbeWait` (`storage/probe_wait.{h,cpp}`), a table of at most 16 pending jobs that the job removes when it ends. `WaitForControllers(timeout_ms)` blocks the boot task through `Environment::WaitUntil` until the table is empty, at most 60 s, and logs `Storage: nvme0 still probing; the root is chosen without it` for the late ones; a late controller still registers its disks, which are scanned as other disks. `Power::Shutdown` calls `Storage::ShutdownControllers(action)` after `FlushAll`, which runs the callbacks registered with `AddShutdown` (16 at most; each NVMe controller and AHCI HBA registers one).

1. `Files::namespace_lock` and `table_lock` (existing); the `/proc` volume lock.
2. Volume locks (`Driver::VolumeLock`): one per task, except `Files::Quiesce`.
3. `Usb::topology_lock`: the manager and `/proc/usb` only. A task holding `Drive::io_lock_` never takes it.
4. `Drive::io_lock_` (`Sync::Mutex`): held across a whole BOT command, its waits and the recovery wait; it serializes the volumes of one drive, the worker's probe and a later eject. `UsbEvents` and `UsbManager` never take it, and it is never nested with `topology_lock`. `Nvme::Controller::io_lock_` is the same kind of lock for an NVMe controller (`src/kernel/storage/nvme/controller.{h,cpp}`): it serializes every command of the controller's namespaces through one bounce buffer (one command in flight) and is held across the command, its wait and the whole recovery ladder (CSTS check, Abort, controller reset, dead), so a thread that times out recovers on its own stack and the others wait behind it for at most the I10 bound (about 54 s); a task holds at most one of the three drive locks. `Ahci::Port::io_lock_` (`src/kernel/storage/ahci/port.{h,cpp}`) is the same kind of lock for one AHCI port: one command in flight (slot 0, one bounce buffer), held across the command, its wait and the whole failure path (`Port::Fail`, the engine stop, COMRESET and restart), so a failing thread recovers on its own stack and the port's other callers wait behind it for the duration of the recovery; the other ports of the HBA are not blocked. It has no admin lock and no completion lock: the wait function reads `PxCI` and `PxIS` itself and `Hba::OnInterrupt` only reads `IS` and calls `Environment::Wake` (I14; the AHCI `[concurrency]` test T23 checks it under `./build.sh unit-test-tsan`). `Port::io_lock_` ranks with `Drive::io_lock_` (4) and is never nested with it. 4a. `Nvme::Controller::admin_lock_` (`Sync::Mutex`), taken after `io_lock_` (the Abort, the reset) or alone (bring-up); `Storage::Remove` runs under `io_lock_` (4 before 5).
5. `Storage::DiskRegistry::lock_` (a spinlock, step 3a), `Drivers::volumes_lock` and the VFS `mount_lock` (spinlocks too; none is held while another is taken, while the removal handler runs or across I/O), taken before a drive's `state_lock_` (`Register` asks the driver `Info` and `Gone` under it). It is never held across I/O, a `Release`, a `delete` or the removal handler.
6. `Drive::state_lock_` (an interrupt-saving spinlock), then the controller's `ring_lock_` (through `Device::Submit`), then the bus's `work_lock_` (through `Device::PostWork`). It guards `device_`, `gone_`, `busy_`/`done_`, the transfer's status and the recovery serials; no waiting, allocation or logging under it.
7. Leaves: the kernel `Environment`'s `wait_lock` (taken by `WaitUntil` before it calls a drive's `ready` hook, which takes `state_lock_`, and by `Wake`, which the callbacks call after releasing `state_lock_`), the worker's `queue_lock`, controller spinlocks, the heap. An NVMe controller's `completion_lock_` (an interrupt-saving spinlock over both queue pairs and the in-flight records) sits at the `state_lock_` level: the `ready` hooks (`ReapPending`, `ReadyRegister`) take it or nothing inside `WaitUntil`, and the MSI handler (`Controller::OnInterrupt`) only calls `Environment::Wake`, never `io_lock_`, `admin_lock_` or `completion_lock_` (I14; the T23 test checks it under `./build.sh unit-test-tsan`).

A drive is one `Usb::Storage::Drive`, counted by the USB binding (dropped by `detach`) and by its registered `Storage::Disk` (which drops it from `Release` once it is freed); a probe job holds a third while it runs, and the last `Release()` frees the two DMA buffers (a 256-byte command page with the CBW at 0 and the CSW at 64, and a 64 KiB bounce buffer every READ and WRITE goes through). `attach` (manager, under `topology_lock`) allocates the drive, sends GET MAX LUN and posts the probe to the storage worker, so attach never waits for a BOT command. The BOT layer's `Pipe` is `Drive::TaskPipe`, for any task but the manager: `Start` submits through `Device::Submit` under `state_lock_` (refused when `device_` is null, a transfer is still busy or recovery failed), the callback `OnTransferDone` records the status, clears `busy_` and wakes through the `Environment`, `Finish` waits for it or `gone_` until the deadline, and `Recover` sets a serial, posts `Device::PostWork` and waits up to 30 s for the manager's reply (or `gone_`), then up to 2 s for the cancelled transfer's own callback; a transfer is never reused, and the buffers never freed, before its callback ran or the drive is gone. The manager's `service` hook (`Drive::Service`) runs the recovery: `CancelEndpoint` IN and OUT, the Bulk-Only Mass Storage Reset (`0x21 0xFF`), `ClearHalt` IN then OUT, then stores the reply and wakes the waiter; it takes neither `io_lock_` nor a volume lock. A failed recovery fails the command, marks the drive broken (no further command is sent, and no second recovery is requested) and `service` returns true, so the manager runs `Reenumerate`: it detaches the device (`Drive::Detached` wakes every waiter with `NoDevice`, still taking no `io_lock_` or volume lock, so a thread that holds a volume lock and waits for recovery always gets an answer), resets the port and enumerates it again, and the drive comes back as a new `Drive` whose probe runs on the worker. A `TransportFailed` command is retried twice by `Drive::Perform` under `io_lock_` (never after a failed recovery); UNIT ATTENTION 28 after the probe marks the drive broken for good and removes its disk from the registry (`Storage::Remove`; the drive stays bound until the unplug). `Drive::Publish` registers the disk after the probe (a drive that is gone is refused, and one that goes in between removes the disk itself); the drive keeps no reference to its `Disk`, only a pointer that `detach` and `MarkLost` take under `state_lock_` and null, so the one that gets it calls `Remove` and no `Disk` is removed twice. `detach` sets `device_` to null and `gone_` under `state_lock_`, wakes every waiter, calls `Storage::Remove` for its disk (no lock held) and drops the binding's reference; the disk's reference goes when the `Disk` is freed. Log lines say `Storage: sda: ...` once registered, and `Storage: USB 1-1: ...` before. `Drive::Move` still bounds-checks and splits above 64 KiB as a guard, although `Disk` does both first. The `Environment` (`storage/environment.h`, namespace `Storage`; `Usb::Storage` aliases it) is the drive's only way to wait: the kernel one (`storage/kernel_environment.cpp`) uses the scheduler's wait queues and the tick clock; host tests use one that pumps the fake controller on one thread and one on real threads.

### Volumes per disk (M19 Part 2, step 3b)

Every disk the registry holds has volumes, and a volume is a `CoreDriver` in one of 32 permanent slots (`src/kernel/filesystem/drivers.{h,cpp}`). Slots 1 to 16 are the boot disk's (`vda`), by partition number, as before (`root=vdaN`, `MountBlockDevice`). Slots 17 to 32 serve every other disk: the storage worker's `OpenDiskVolumes` takes the next free one for each volume it accepts (a filesystem on the whole disk first, named like the disk, else each partition `Partition::Find` lists), holding a counted `Storage::Disk` reference until the slot is released. A disk with more volumes than free slots opens the first ones and logs `Storage: no volume slot left`. The core opens each read-only; `mount` takes the bare name: `mount -t fat16 sda1 /mnt` (a leading `/dev/` is stripped; there are no block nodes in `/dev`). FAT16 needs 512-byte sectors, so a 4096-byte-sector drive logs `no supported filesystem`; MBR type 0x04 counts as FAT16.

`/proc/partitions` lists each disk (`name KiB sector-size virtio|usb|nvme|ahci [removable rw|ro] [ejected]`), then its volumes (`sda1 4096 512 fat16`), in registry order; a partition with no supported filesystem and a gone disk are not listed.

When a disk is removed (unplug, UNIT ATTENTION 28), the removal handler marks its slots dead from any task without a volume lock: the driver's mount mode turns read-only at once (`/proc/mounts` shows `ro`), every `CoreDriver` operation fails with EIO before it reaches the core, `Sync` gives EIO and a remount stays read-only. Mounting a dead volume gives ENODEV. The worker then forgets every unmounted dead volume (`Discard` frees the core's filesystem with no device I/O, the registration goes, the slot and the disk reference are released). A mounted dead volume stays until `umount`: with a file open that is EBUSY until `close`, otherwise `Detach` calls `Discard` and succeeds with no I/O and `Drivers::Unmounted` releases the slot. The disk's name stays taken until its last reference is gone, so a replug meanwhile comes back as the next letter. `FilesystemType::Discard` is the core's call for this; it never touches the device.

### NVMe and AHCI (M16)

`Storage::StartControllers` walks PCI once and gives each NVMe controller (class 01/08/02, `storage/nvme/probe.cpp`) and each AHCI HBA (01/06/01, ABAR in BAR 5, `storage/ahci/probe.cpp`) the next index from 0; the bring-up runs on the storage worker, so the probe returns at once. Logs: `Storage: nvme0: ...` and `Storage: ahci0: ...` for the controller, then `Storage: nvme0n1: ...` or `Storage: sda: ...` per disk. Names: `nvme<controller>n<namespace>` with partitions `nvme0n1p1`, and AHCI disks `sda`, `sdb`, ... by port (USB drives take the letters after them). `/proc/partitions` shows `nvme` or `ahci` in the bus column.

Both drivers move data through one DMA bounce buffer with one command in flight per controller (NVMe, at most 128 KiB per command) or per port (AHCI, slot 0), so there is no NCQ, no second queue, no SGL and no TRIM. NVMe uses an admin and an I/O queue pair on MSI-X; AHCI uses one MSI per HBA (QEMU's `ich9-ahci` has MSI but no MSI-X; with neither, both drivers poll). An NVMe namespace with metadata, an ATAPI device and a SATA port with no link are not registered, each with a log line. Flush is sent when the volume layer syncs and at shutdown; a power-off parks each SATA disk with STANDBY IMMEDIATE (`Storage: sda: parked`).

Recovery: a command that times out (30 s) or fails is recovered on the calling thread. NVMe: CSTS check, Abort, then a controller reset with re-identify; the controller is dead after a failed reset or a second reset within 60 s. AHCI: stop the engine, clear `PxSERR` and `PxIS`, COMRESET only when `BSY` or `DRQ` is set, restart; the port is dead after three recoveries within 60 s, with no link, a changed signature or a PhyRdy change. A failed command returns `EIO` and is not retried; a dead disk is removed from the registry like an unplugged USB drive (its mounted volumes turn read-only, see above). There is no hot-plug: controllers and ports found at boot are never freed, and a SATA hot-unplug is treated as a dead port.

Writes: QEMU's controllers (recognised by their PCI subsystem IDs) are writable. On real hardware the disks are read-only unless the kernel command line has `storage=rw`; `storage=ro` makes every NVMe and AHCI disk read-only, QEMU's too; the last `storage=` wins and any other value counts as absent. The controller line says which rule applied (`QEMU controller, writes allowed`, `real hardware, read-only (boot with storage=rw to write)`, `storage=ro, read-only`, `storage=rw, writes allowed on real hardware`), and `/proc/partitions` shows `ro`. A read-only disk's volumes mount read-only and its flush succeeds without reaching the driver. `build.sh` passes it as `KERNEL_STORAGE=ro|rw`.

Tests: host `[nvme]`, `[ahci]`, `[ata]` over `tests/unit/kernel/storage/fake_nvme.*` and `fake_ahci.*` (register-level fakes with scripted hangs and faults); QEMU `NVME_TEST=ON` (`-device nvme`, one `storage-controller.img` with a FAT16, an ext2, an ext3 and an ext4 partition, built by `tools/create_storage_disks.py`; shell session on each volume, host checks with mtools and `e2fsck -fn`), `NVME_NAMESPACES_TEST=ON` (a 4096-byte, a 512-byte superfloppy and an `ms=8` namespace), `AHCI_TEST=ON` (`ich9-ahci` with an `ide-hd` and an empty `ide-cd`, the same session on `sda1` to `sda4`) and `STORAGE_POLICY_TEST=ON` with `KERNEL_STORAGE=ro` (both controllers, both images' SHA-256 unchanged). The matrix runs them as `nvme`, `nvme_without_usb` (`KERNEL_USB=off`), `nvme_release`, `nvme_namespaces`, `ahci` and `storage_policy`. A mutation that makes a Flush a no-op is caught only by the host tests, because QEMU's disk image keeps its write-back cache.

### Root on any disk (M16)

`root=` takes `vd<letter>N`, `sd<letter>N` or `nvme<c>n<ns>pN` (partition 1 to 9999, no leading zeros; the last `root=` wins; a whole disk or anything else is malformed and the default is used). Without `root=`, the root is the lowest-numbered FAT16, ext2, ext3 or ext4 partition of `vda`. `MountAndVerifyDisk` starts the 100 Hz timer first, registers virtio-blk if there is one, starts the controllers, waits for their bring-up (`Storage::WaitForControllers`, at most 60 s; a controller still probing is logged and its disks are scanned later as ordinary disks), chooses the boot disk by name (`Storage::ChooseBootDisk`) and mounts it; with no boot disk the root is a tmpfs with `/home` (a missing `vda` no longer means that by itself). Other disks are scanned only after the root is mounted (`DeferScans`), so the boot disk takes the 16 boot slots (named `vda1`, `sda1` or `nvme0n1p1` from the disk) and every other disk the shared 16. Removing the boot disk marks all boot slots dead. The root line, such as `VFS: / is nvme0n1p3 ext3, read-write` or `VFS: / is nvme0n1p1 ext4, read-only: the storage policy (storage=rw to write)`, says what was chosen and why it is read-only; a `root=` that names no usable disk or partition logs a warning (`using the default root`) and uses the default rule, or the RAM root.

Tests: `ROOT_DISK_TEST=ON` with `DISK_BUS=virtio|nvme|nvme4k|ahci` (`tools/smoke/cases/root_disk.py`; `nvme4k` boots a root image of 4096-byte sectors from `tools/create_storage_disks.py`, `BuildRootFourK`) and `KERNEL_ROOT=<name>`, checking the root line, init, `/etc`, a write to the root (or its refusal) and on the host `e2fsck` and the journal. Matrix cases `nvme_root`, `ahci_root`, `nvme_4k_root`, `nvme_root_readonly` (`KERNEL_STORAGE=ro`; the image is unchanged), `nvme_no_default_root` (no `vda`: RAM root, then `mount nvme0n1p1`), `missing_root_disk` and `nvme_unusable_root`.

### Position-independent programs

User programs are built as static position-independent executables (`-fpie -Wl,-pie -Wl,--no-dynamic-linker -Wl,-z,text`), linked at 0 by `src/user/linker_<arch>.ld`. The ELF loader accepts `ET_DYN` images, adds the load bias to every segment, and applies the `RELATIVE` relocations listed under `PT_DYNAMIC` while the segments are still writable, then sets their final permissions. It skips `NONE` entries, which GNU ld can leave behind, and rejects relocations that need symbols, a PLT, or a write into a non-writable segment. Spawned programs get a random image base; the kernel's own test programs load at `0x400000` (x86-64) or `0x0000400000000000` (AArch64).

**Dynamic-linking policy:** FeatherOS runs only static PIEs. Every program carries its own copy of the C library, and the kernel never resolves symbols.
