Meanwhile a client in the harness reaches the server through a second forwarded port (the guest's port 80) and checks pipelined GET and HEAD on one connection, `..` and `%2e%2e` targets that must stay inside the directory (404), a POST (501), HTTP/1.0, a missing `Host` (400), HTTP/2.0 (505), the directory redirect's `Location`, the absolute form, and `/system/vi` byte for byte against the build's copy; the shell waits for it, then stops `httpd` with `kill %1` and prints the log, which must list the requests from `127.0.0.1`, `::1` and, for forwarded ones, `10.0.2.2`. The harness checks that TCP connections and UDP datagrams reached the echo server over both versions, that the HTTP requests carried the right `Host` headers, and that the guest's servers answered. Finally an NTP server the harness starts on the loopback addresses, whose clock starts at 2031-01-01 00:00:00 UTC, answers `ntp -n` over IPv4 with an offset of years, then `ntp -6` by name, which sets the clock; `date` must print 2031, and a second `ntp -n` must measure an offset under a second. The server's second port refuses with a `RATE` kiss, which `ntp` must report, and the harness checks that requests arrived over both versions. Last comes the kill switch: with a connection held open through the lossy relay, `net down` must end it with `Network is down`, `net` must show `link down` without addresses, `ping` over both versions, `nc` to the echo server over both, `nc -u` and `fetch` must fail with `Network is down`, and `ping 127.0.0.1` and a pair of `nc` over `127.0.0.1` must still work. The harness checks that none of its servers saw a query, connection, datagram or request between `net down` and `net up`. The DHCP client must report its address gone while the link is down and, after `net up`, lease it again by itself, `nc` must reach the echo server over both versions, and `net` must show both IP versions again. Every boot logs `Network: virtio-net <mac>`, `Network: IPv6 <link-local> and <global>/64 via <router>` and, from init's DHCP client, `Network: IPv4 10.0.2.15/24 via 10.0.2.2`, which the smoke test checks. The shell test pings the gateway, our own address and `127.0.0.1`, then `fec0::2`, `::1` and our global IPv6 address. Host tests cover address formatting, Ethernet frames, ARP packets, the neighbor cache, the Internet and pseudo-header checksums, IPv4, IPv6, ICMP and ICMPv6 formats (echo, neighbor and router messages, port unreachable errors and their size limits), the ICMP error rate limit, IPv6 address building, the router advertisement's DNS option, UDP headers, ICMP and UDP sockets of both versions (identifiers, ports, delivery by port and address, the queue limit, timeouts, connected UDP, port unreachable errors), TCP segments and options, the TCP state machine against scripted segments (both opens, refusal, retransmission and backoff, MSS and window segmentation, fast retransmit, NewReno's inflation, partial acknowledgments and the timeout rule, out-of-order, overlapping and repeated segments, the range limit, segments cut at the window, a FIN ahead of missing data, window probes and updates that grow by segments, window scaling (both opens with and without the peer's option, scaled windows each way, rounding that keeps the right edge, rings that grow), every close sequence, resets, challenge ACKs and drops without a segment), TCP sockets (connect, listen and accept, resets for unknown segments, backlog, lingering closes, shutdown, binding rules, non-blocking connect, accept, send and receive with the error option, connections a link going down ends or keeps, connects while it is down), the name server list, socket descriptors, DHCP messages (renewals, T1 and T2), lease timing (defaults, times out of order, infinite leases, phases, retries, discovery backoff), DNS queries and responses (PTR names under `in-addr.arpa` and `ip6.arpa`, PTR answers with compression, CNAMEs, loops and unsafe characters, truncated responses, answers longer than a datagram), `/etc/hosts` lines (comments, malformed lines, the name limit, matching), `/etc/resolv.conf` (name servers with ports, comments, malformed lines, the server limit, option limits), NTP timestamps (era wrap, rounding), requests, replies (wrong origin, kisses, unsynchronized servers) and offsets, and HTTP URLs (parsing, relative references, dot segments), requests and responses (lengths, chunks, bodies until close, 1xx, 204, long headers, malformed and truncated responses), and for the server request heads (pipelining, keep-alive rules, bodies, every refusal status), request paths (decoding, dot segments, the absolute form), encoded links and content types.

## Wall Clock

The kernel reads UTC wall-clock time from the platform real-time clock with one-second resolution: the CMOS RTC on x86-64 (`src/kernel/arch/x86_64/rtc.cpp`, BCD or binary and 12- or 24-hour formats, century register `0x32`, with reads serialized by a lock because the index/data port pair is shared by all CPUs) and the PL031 at its fixed QEMU `virt` address on AArch64 (`src/kernel/arch/aarch64/rtc.cpp`). QEMU initializes both from the host clock in UTC. Startup logs the time as `Wall clock: YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM:SS UTC`. Once the timer runs, the kernel's wall clock (`src/kernel/time/wall_clock.cpp`) reads the RTC once and advances by timer ticks in nanoseconds, so it has 10 ms resolution; programs read it with the `ReadClock` syscall and set it with `SetClock`, which `ntp` uses. A set time lasts until the next boot: the RTC itself is never written. `src/user/calendar.h` converts between UNIX seconds and UTC dates for both the kernel and user programs; there is no time-zone support.

## Shell And Console Input

After startup, the kernel starts `/sbin/init` from the root when it can run it, else `/system/init` from the initramfs (the rescue system, also used when the root is a disk without `/sbin/init`, such as `root=vda3` or `vda6`, or when the file does not load), logging `Init: starting /sbin/init from the root` or `Init: starting /system/init from the initramfs`, and starts it again if it ever exits. `init` (`src/user/init.cpp`) is the same program in both places. It builds its services from three sources, merged as launchd merges its directories: the built-in `/system/init.conf` (the initramfs's copy, installed by `build.sh` from `initramfs/init.conf`), one file per service group in `/etc/init.d/` (the administrator's, on the root's own persistent `/etc`; create the directory with `mkdir`), minus the built-in services named in `/etc/init.disabled`. `/etc/init.conf` is no longer a default or read: a root left with the file from M29 gets `init: /etc/init.conf is not read any more; put services in /etc/init.d/` at each boot. The built-ins come first in file order, then each `/etc/init.d/` file in name order (files starting with `.` and subdirectories are ignored; at most 16 files of names under 64 characters and 2047 bytes each, at most 16 services in all, the rest reported as `init: <file>: ...` and skipped). Every file has one service per line as `<policy> <command line>` and `#` comments. A service's name is its program: the first word of its command without its directory, or for a `console` service its terminal. A service in `/etc/init.d/` replaces the built-in services of the same name (`respawn /sbin/dhcp -v` replaces `respawn dhcp`, `console /dev/tty2 vi` the shell on `/dev/tty2`) and takes its own place in the order; two `/etc/init.d/` services of one program both run, but a second console on a terminal another `/etc/init.d/` file holds skips that file. `/etc/init.disabled` has one name per line (`dhcp` or `shell` for a program, `/dev/tty3` for a terminal; `#` comments) and removes the built-in services of that name, printing `init: /etc/init.disabled: not starting <command>` for each; it never removes an `/etc/init.d/` service. Env lines merge the same way, a later `env NAME=value` replacing an earlier one of the same name. A line that does not parse is reported and skipped in the built-in file (`init: /system/init.conf: ignoring line N`); an `/etc/init.d/` file with any such line is skipped whole (`init: /etc/init.d/x: ignoring line N`, then `init: /etc/init.d/x: does not parse; skipped`), as is one that does not fit, and the rest of the set is kept. `once` services run to completion in order before the others start. `respawn` services run in the background. A `console` service names its terminal before its command (`console /dev/tty2 shell`), at most one per terminal, and holds that terminal as a session leader. init starts them with `posix_spawn`: a console service with `POSIX_SPAWN_SETSID` and its terminal opened on descriptors 0 to 2, so the child takes it as its controlling terminal, and respawned services with `POSIX_SPAWN_SETPGROUP`, which FeatherOS treats as starting in the background. init polls its services with `waitpid(..., WNOHANG)`, reports each exit as `init: <command> exited with status N` (or `ended by signal N` after a fault or Ctrl+C), and restarts the service; one that exits five times within ten seconds is disabled instead. An `env NAME=value` line adds a variable to the environment every service starts with; the built-in file sets `PATH=/bin:/sbin:/system`, init itself sets `HOME` as `login` does (`/home` when it is a directory, else `/`; a `HOME` line in any file wins), and the shell passes its environment on to every program it runs. When `/system/init.conf` cannot be used, or the merged set has no `console` service (every built-in console disabled, none in `/etc/init.d/`), init prints `init: no console service; starting a shell on /dev/tty1` and starts `console /dev/tty1 shell` (`EnsureConsole`). The merge logic is `src/user/init_config.{h,cpp}` (`AddBuiltIn`, `DisableBuiltIns`, `AddServiceFile`), host-tested in `tests/unit/kernel/process/init_config_tests.cpp`. The default configuration prints a startup line and runs a shell as the console service of each of `/dev/tty1` to `/dev/tty4`, so the shell's `exit` built-in returns to a fresh shell on the same console.

`shutdown` and `reboot` are shell built-ins that call `reboot(RB_POWER_OFF)` and `reboot(RB_AUTOBOOT)`, over the `Shutdown` syscall (`src/kernel/power.cpp`). In order, the kernel:

1. Marks every process except the caller and its ancestors for termination, and waits up to two seconds for them to exit.
2. Takes the namespace lock, every mounted volume's lock (`VFS::DetachAll`, detaching each mount as it goes) and the table lock for good, so no file operation is half done.
3. Sends a virtio-blk flush when the device supports it.
4. Parks the other CPUs with an IPI.
5. Asks the firmware to act.

AArch64 uses PSCI `SYSTEM_OFF` or `SYSTEM_RESET`, through `hvc` or `smc` as the device tree's `/psci` node says (`src/kernel/arch/aarch64/power.cpp`). x86-64 reads ACPI from Limine's RSDP through the device-memory window, checking table checksums. It powers off with the FADT's PM1 control ports and the `\_S5` sleep types, found by scanning the DSDT's AML instead of interpreting it, and enables ACPI mode first if needed. It restarts through the FADT reset register when present, then the keyboard controller, then a triple fault (`src/kernel/arch/x86_64/power.cpp`). If the firmware does nothing, the kernel panics.

The shell shows a prompt with its working directory, such as `/home> `, and edits the line itself in raw mode (`src/user/line_editor.h`, over `RawTerminal` in `src/user/raw_terminal.h`), falling back to `featheros_read_line` when standard input is not the console. The keys are listed in the [readme](../readme.md#keyboard-shortcuts) and in the shell's `help`. History keeps the last 32 lines, skipping blank lines and repeats, and Down returns to the line being typed. The editor redraws a line that wraps with relative cursor moves, using the terminal width from `ioctl(TIOCGWINSZ)`, so a serial terminal narrower than the screen can show long lines oddly. On `SIGWINCH` (`src/user/window_size.h`, shared with `vi` and `top`) it reads the width again and redraws the line from the prompt's first row; `vi` redraws the whole screen and `top` its last table at once. Its reads set `FEATHEROS_WAKE_ON_OUTPUT` (`TerminalWakeOnOutput`): when a service, a background job or the kernel log prints while the shell waits at its prompt, the read returns empty once the output paused for a tenth of a second (console writes wake it, kernel log lines are noticed within a second), the kernel moves the cursor to a new row if needed, and the shell draws the prompt and line again there, as after the DHCP client's lease message at boot. The console returns to canonical input before each command runs. It splits each line into words by the kernel's rules (`SplitCommandLine` in `src/user/command_line.h`, where double quotes group words), runs the first word with `posix_spawnp`, which looks a name up in the directories of `PATH` (`/bin:/sbin:/system`) and runs a word with a `/` as that file, waits for it with `waitpid`, and prints `exit status N` for nonzero statuses. A missing name reports `Unknown command: <name>`, and other failures `shell: <word>: <error>`, such as `Exec format error` for a file that is not a program. `PATH` lists the root's `/bin` and `/sbin` before `/system`, so a program on the root runs ahead of the initramfs's copy, and deleting it falls back to the built-in one (`/sbin` holds `init`, `mount`, `umount`, `sync`, `net` and `dhcp`, the administrative programs (`shutdown` and `reboot` are shell built-ins, not programs), and `/bin` the rest; `/system` keeps all of them as the rescue copies); a root without `/bin` and `/sbin` (an ext3 or ext2 one, or a new ext4 volume) runs everything from `/system`. Programs copied to the disk run like those in `/system`: `cp /system/echo /bin/say` makes `say` a command. A process's name, in `ps` and `/proc`, is the last part of its path. A command's input and output can be redirected with `< file`, `> file`, `>> file`, `2> file` and `2>&1`, as its own word or joined to the file name (`>out.txt`); the shell opens the files and passes them as `posix_spawn` file actions, and a quoted `">"` is an ordinary word. Built-ins cannot be redirected. `a | b` runs up to four commands with each one's output feeding the next one's input, as in other shells, and redirections still apply to each command; the last command's status is reported. Each command line, pipeline or not, runs as one process group led by its first command, so any of them may read the console and Ctrl+C ends them all. A trailing `&` (`sleep 9 &`, or `sleep 9&`) starts it in the background instead: the shell prints `[n] pid` and returns to the prompt, and the job cannot read the console. The shell waits for a foreground job with `WUNTRACED`; when Ctrl+Z stops it, the shell takes the terminal back with `tcsetpgrp`, saves the job's terminal settings and puts back its own (read at startup), and prints `[n] Stopped  <command>`. Before each prompt the shell reaps finished jobs and reports `[n] Done`, `[n] Exit N` or `[n] Signal N` with the command, and `[n] Stopped` for a background job something stopped. `jobs` lists each job as `Running` or `Stopped`; `fg [n]` gives job `n` (or the job used last) its saved settings and the console, sends `SIGCONT` if it is stopped, and waits for it; `bg [n]` continues a stopped job in the background; and `wait` waits for every running job. On `exit`, stopped jobs get `SIGHUP` and `SIGCONT`, since nobody could continue them afterwards. A background job that reads the console stops with `SIGTTIN`, and one that changes its mode with `SIGTTOU`, until `fg` gives it the console. `kill %n` with `SIGTERM` or `SIGHUP` also sends `SIGCONT`, so a stopped job ends too. At most 8 jobs exist at once; a job that stops when all 8 are taken is continued. The shell does not ignore the stop signals itself, because ignored signals carry over to the programs it starts: as a session leader it never gets Ctrl+Z's `SIGTSTP`, and its group is orphaned, so the terminal stop signals do not stop it. It blocks `SIGTTOU` only while it takes the terminal back. `RawTerminal` reads one byte at a time, as readline does, so what is typed after a line's Enter stays queued for the next reader. Its raw mode turns `ISIG` off, so Ctrl+Z arrives as a key, and while it is active it catches `SIGTSTP` and hands that over as the same key. `vi`, `top` and the shell's line editor answer it with `RawTerminal::Suspend`: they leave the alternate screen (the line editor prints `^Z`), the terminal gets the settings it had before raw mode, `SIGTSTP` goes back to its default action and `kill(0, SIGTSTP)` stops the group; once continued, raw mode returns and the program draws its whole screen, or the prompt and line, again. The shell ignores Ctrl+Z at its prompt when it leads its session, since nothing could stop or continue it; a shell started from another one stops. `help` lists the bundled programs:

- `calc [expression]` evaluates an expression such as `4 + 66 - (3 / 4)` with `+ - * /`, parentheses, unary signs, and decimals, using exact fractions (`src/user/calculator.h`) and printing up to six decimal places. Without an expression it reads one per line at a `calc> ` prompt until `exit` or `quit`.
- `date` prints the current UTC time, such as `2026-10-19 03:07:39 UTC`.
- `echo <words...>` prints its arguments.
- `fanout <n>` starts `n` (1–16) `echo` children before waiting for any of them.
- `primes <workers> [limit]` demonstrates multicore scheduling. It counts the primes below `limit` (default 10,000,000) with 1–16 worker processes, each testing every n-th odd number by trial division. Each worker reports its count and the CPUs it ran on in one line through a pipe of its own, and the parent prints one line per worker plus the total time. Compare `primes 1` with `primes 4` and watch the status bar's busy-CPU count.
- `threads <threads> [limit]` counts primes like `primes`, but with 1–15 threads of one process (`pthread_create`, `src/user/prime_count.cpp` shared with `primes`), and shows the CPUs each thread ran on. The shell test runs `threads 4 100000` and `threads 15 20000`, which must find the same counts as `primes`.
- `sleep <seconds>` sleeps, with fractions such as `0.5`, and checks that the wall clock advanced by the whole seconds requested.
- `fptest [workers]` starts 8 workers by default, more than there are CPUs. Each fills every vector register with its own pattern, spins while the others run, and checks the pattern is intact; it fails if context switches lose floating-point state.
- `hardening` runs 69 checks, including `Shutdown` from an ordinary program: syscalls with kernel or invalid pointers (including a `Spawn` environment) and oversized arguments, a kernel-half ELF (`badelf`, generated by `tools/create_bad_elf.py`) from `/system` and from a copy in `/tmp`, `Spawn` of a missing file, a directory, a device, a text file, a truncated program, a program whose segment needs more pages than the limit, a program open for writing, a path too long once resolved, and a program file padded past 1 MiB, which must run, children that execute the heap, touch a heap page after shrinking the heap below it (also from a thread spinning on another CPU, which fails without a TLB shootdown), write their code, read kernel memory, or use an invalid syscall (each must be terminated), a thread starting at a kernel address or joining into kernel memory, the per-process child limit, and zero-length reads, writes, `poll` and datagrams from address 0, which must succeed.
- `aslr` prints where its image, stack, arguments page (`featheros_arguments_page`), and heap (`sbrk(0)`) landed; the shell test runs it twice and requires different addresses.
- `memtest [overflow|exhaust]` grows its stack and heap and checks them, recurses into the stack guard page to show that the kernel terminates it, or grows its heap until the page limit refuses more, shrinks it back to its start, and checks that it grows as far again (the shell test runs it twice and requires the same count).
- `upper` reads one line as the foreground process and prints it in upper case; when its input is a file, it converts all of it.
- `ulimit -n [limit]` and `ulimit -Hn [limit]` are shell built-ins (the limit must change the shell's own, which its commands inherit): they print or set the soft and hard `RLIMIT_NOFILE` limits (`-n N` above the hard limit asks for the hard limit to rise with it), print `ulimit: <reason>` when the kernel refuses (`Operation not permitted` above 1024, `Invalid argument` for a soft limit above the hard) and the usage line for anything else.
- `cd [dir]` and `pwd` are shell built-ins; `cd` alone goes to `$HOME` (`cd: HOME not set` when it is unset), and the prompt shows the working directory, such as `/home/docs> `.
- `env` prints the environment, one `NAME=value` per line.
- `ls [-l] [directory]` lists a directory (the working directory by default) with file sizes, marking subdirectories with `/` from the entry's `d_type`, without a `stat`; other entries are `lstat`'ed, so a symbolic link shows its own size. `-l` prints `ls -l`'s columns instead: the type and permissions (`drwxr-xr-x`, with `s`/`S` and `t`/`T` for set-ID and sticky bits, `?` for an ext2 special node), the link count, owner and group as numbers, the size, and the modification time in UTC (`2026-01-01 00:00`) before the name, and after a link's name its target, as `fast-link -> hello.txt`; FAT16 shows `0644`/`0755`, owner 0 and 1970, since its times are not read yet. `mkdir <dir>` and `rmdir <dir>` create and remove (empty) directories.
- `ln [-s] <target> <link>` makes `link` a hard link to `target`, or with `-s` a symbolic link holding `target` as given, resolved from the link's directory; a `link` that names a directory gets the target's last name inside it, and failures print `ln: <link>: <error>` (`Operation not permitted` where the filesystem holds no such links: the disk holds neither kind, `/tmp` and `/etc` only symbolic ones). `readlink [-f] <path>` prints a link's target, or `readlink: <path>: not a symbolic link`; with `-f` it prints the path with every link, `.` and `..` resolved (`realpath`).
- `mount` prints `/proc/mounts`; `mount -t <type> <source> <target>` mounts a filesystem instance, such as `mount -t tmpfs tmp /tmp` or the test disk's FAT16 partition with `mount -t fat16 vda2 /fat`, read-write unless `-r` or `-o ro` comes first (`-o rw` is the default; `-o` takes a comma list of `ro`, `rw` and `remount`, the last of `ro` and `rw` wins). `mount -o remount,ro <target>` and `mount -o remount,rw <target>` change the mode of a mounted filesystem in place, the root included (an ext3 or ext4 mount closes its journal on the way to read-only, so the partition is clean while it stays mounted; a read-write remount follows a fresh read-write mount's rules and is refused with `Read-only file system` where a mount would fall back to read-only); `remount` without `ro` or `rw` prints the usage, and reports an unknown type as `mount: <type>: unknown filesystem type`. `umount <target>` unmounts one after syncing it, and says `target is busy` while a file on it is open, a working directory or another mount point lies inside it, and `not mounted` for a directory that is not a mount point. `eject <disk>` (`/sbin/eject`, an administrative program like `mount` and `umount`; `-h` for the usage) unmounts every mount of a removable disk such as `sda` or `/dev/sda`, flushes and stops the drive, so it can be unplugged, and prints nothing; the errors are `eject: sda: Device or resource busy` (nothing was unmounted), `eject: sdz: no such disk`, `eject: vda: Operation not supported` (not removable), `eject: sda: Input/output error` and the usage line, all with exit status 1; ejecting an ejected disk does nothing and exits 0.
- `sync` syncs every mounted filesystem (POSIX `sync`, syscall 71); the writeback task does the same for ext2, ext3 and ext4 every 5 s, but run it before stopping QEMU from outside, or an ext2 root changed in the last 5 s is left unclean and mounts read-only at the next boot (on ext3 and ext4 it commits to the log without checkpointing, so the host needs `e2fsck -fy` after a power cut).
- `lsusb` prints `/proc/usb`: one line per USB device with its path (`1-5.2` behind a hub), number, speed, vendor:product, class and bound drivers (`hub`, `hid-keyboard`, `hid-mouse`, `hid`, or `unbound`); nothing without a controller.
- `dmesg` prints `/proc/kmsg`: the kernel log, oldest line first, as far back as the 64 KiB ring reaches. Page it with `dmesg | head -n 60` and `dmesg | tail -n 60`; a laptop without a serial port is read this way (`docs/hardware.md`).
- `cat [file]` prints a file, or its input without one, and says why it could not open one (`cat: cannot open <file>: <error>`); the console shows tabs as spaces and drops other control characters. `write [-a] <file> <text...>` writes the words and a newline to a file, replacing it or, with `-a`, appending. `rm <file>` deletes a file. Names are relative to the working directory, and `cat` also tries `/system` for a missing relative name, so `cat hello.txt` works anywhere.
- Text tools, which read their files or, without any, their input, so they work in pipelines (shared logic in `src/user/text_tools.cpp`):
  - `cp <source> <target>` copies a file, into a directory under the source's name; `touch <file>...` creates empty files (there are no file times to change).
  - `mv <source> <target>` renames or moves a file or directory the same way, with the `Rename` syscall. On the disk this rewrites only directory entries (a moved directory's `..` follows it), and a new case for the same name works, and an existing target is replaced as `rename` does (one call, so an ext3/ext4 crash leaves the old file or the new one). Between the disk and `/tmp` it copies a file and removes the original; directories cannot move between them.
  - `head [-n lines] [file...]` and `tail [-n lines] [file]` print the first or last 10 (or `-n`) lines.
  - `wc [-lwc] [file...]` counts lines, words and bytes, with a total for several files.
  - `grep [-EFivnco] <pattern> [file...]` prints the lines that match a POSIX basic regular expression (the C library's `regex.h`), an extended one with `-E`, or plain text with `-F` (escaped into a basic expression by `FixedTextPattern`): `-i` ignores case, `-v` inverts, `-n` numbers, `-c` counts, and `-o` prints each non-empty match on a line of its own instead of the line, as GNU grep does. A pattern that does not compile prints `regerror`'s message, such as `grep: Unmatched ( or \(`. It exits with 0 on a match, 1 without, 2 on an error.
  - `sort [-rnu] [file...]` sorts lines by bytes, or with `-n` by their leading numbers; `-r` reverses and `-u` drops repeats.
- `filetest` runs 33 checks of the file syscalls: creation, multi-cluster writes, reads, seeks, appends, the one-writer and no-removing-open-files rules, bad pointers and flags, the read-only initramfs, the descriptor limit (253 opens past the console), and a child that exits with a file still open.
- `fbtest` checks `/dev/fb0` on the console it runs on (the screen is blank meanwhile) and prints `fbtest: ok` or its first failing check: close-on-exec and `FB_GET_INFO`, the errors before an acquire, `ENOTTY` for a session leader without a terminal, `FB_ACQUIRE` twice and `EBUSY` from a second open file and another process, `FB_PRESENT` errors and clipping (`EFAULT`, `EINVAL`, off-screen and full-screen rectangles), release on the last close, on kill, on exit and `FB_RELEASE`, and a background caller stopped by `SIGTTOU` or let through when it ignores it. It also checks reading events: `poll` before and after acquiring (`POLLERR`, then `POLLIN` for the `Shown` event and none after it), reads of 4 and 0 bytes, `O_NONBLOCK` from `F_SETFL` and from `open` (`EAGAIN`), `dropped_events` 0, a thread blocked in `read` that `FB_RELEASE` wakes with `EINVAL`, a child whose blocked `read` fails with `EINTR` on `SIGUSR1`, and a child that opens with `O_NONBLOCK` and reads `Shown` then `EAGAIN`. `fbtest pty` (run under `script -c`) checks that a pseudo-terminal's acquire fails with `ENOTTY`. It runs in the SHELL session (`tools/smoke/cases/shell/framebuffer.py`).
- `fbdemo [--keys] [seconds]` takes the screen of its console through `/dev/fb0` (`<featheros/framebuffer.h>`, `docs/design/M32-P1.md`) with the graphics library (`Graphics::Display`, see "Graphics library") and presents a test pattern in one full-screen `FB_PRESENT`; the two rectangles at the corners are drawn into the same surface and clipped by `Surface`: eight 128-pixel color bars over the top half, a gradient below (red from x/4, green from the row), `654321` at the top-left corner (a 20x20 rectangle at -10,-10) and `123456` at the bottom-right (100x100 at 1000,700). It prints `fbdemo: tty1 1024x768` and `fbdemo: presented`, then waits for input: `r` and Enter presents everything again, `q` and Enter quits, and with no input for `seconds` (default 5) it releases the screen, the console repaints whole, and it prints `fbdemo: done`. It needs a virtual console (a pseudo-terminal gives `FB_ACQUIRE: Not a terminal`). `fbdemo --keys [seconds]` also opens the descriptor with `O_NONBLOCK` and prints each key event as `fbdemo: event <press|repeat|release|shown|hidden> <code> <modifiers>` (`code` is the Linux `KEY_*` number, `modifiers` the `FB_MOD_*` bits after the event), presents again and prints `fbdemo: presented` on `shown`, prints `fbdemo: time went backwards` if `time_ms` decreases, stops reading events for 3 s on `p` and Enter (`fbdemo: paused`, `fbdemo: resumed`), and on Ctrl+C reads events until 200 ms pass quietly, prints `fbdemo: interrupted` and exits 130; it prints `fbdemo: dropped N` (`fb_info.dropped_events`) before it releases the screen. `fbdemo -h` and the pty refusal run in the SHELL session; the pixels are checked by `GRAPHICS_TEST`.
- `fontview [name] | --card | --check PATH` (`src/user/fontview.cpp`) works on the `FFNT` fonts (see "Graphics library"). `fontview [name]` (default `sans-24`) takes the console's screen and pages through `/system/fonts/<name>.ffnt`'s glyph table, 16 columns with record 0 dim as the missing box: PgUp and PgDn change the page, Esc or Ctrl+C quits, and switching back to the console redraws. `--check PATH` prints `fontview: font ok glyphs G line L`, or `fontview: font rejected <reason>` (the `FontStatus` names, exit 1), or `fontview: font unreadable <error>` when the file cannot be read. `--card` draws the fixed test card (the ten sizes' line `Hamburgefonstiv 0123 é` at x 16, 4 pixels apart, light on dark in the top half and dark on light from y 384, each half ending with `Missing: U+0378` in `sans-24`), prints `fontview: card drawn` and waits for a key; `FONTS` compares it with a rendering from `tools/fonts/ffnt.py`. It needs a virtual console (a pty gives `FB_ACQUIRE: Not a terminal`).
- `uicard [--page N] [--theme id] [--accent id]` (`src/user/uicard.cpp`, C++) takes the console's screen and draws one of four test-card pages of the desktop's artwork with `UI::DrawCard` (see "Desktop pixels and artwork"): 1 colours, 2 shapes, type roles and ellipsis, 3 the 45 glyphs, app tiles and pointers, 4 the wallpaper with the eight shadows. The default is page 1 in `dusk` with the default accent. It prints `uicard: page N drawn in T ms` and waits: Left and Right change the page, Up and Down the theme, Esc, `q` or Ctrl+C quit. It needs the fonts and `/system/gui/outlines.fvo`; without the art file the card shows empty boxes.
- `gfxcheck` (Embedded Swift, `src/user/gfxcheck.swift`, needs `deps/swift`) builds a 64x56 `Surface` through `import FeatherGraphics`, fills a rectangle, draws an outline clipped at the corner, text in `sans-16` and `mono-32` and a keyed blit, and prints `gfxcheck: surface 64x56, sprite 8x8` and `gfxcheck: checksum <n>, match true`, where `match` compares `Surface::Checksum` with `GfxCheck::ExpectedChecksum` (`src/user/gfx_check.cpp`), which draws the same picture with plain loops over the font files' bytes; it then loads a two-tile `TileSheet` (the rows and palette come from `gfx_check.cpp`), draws both shades keyed, one tile unkeyed and clipped, and prints `gfxcheck: tiles 2 of 8x8, loaded true` and `gfxcheck: tile checksum <n>, match true` against `GfxCheck::ExpectedTileChecksum` (1753845943). It then loads `sans-16` by name, draws a 120x48 picture with it and the console font (`Font::Console()`; accents, Greek, Cyrillic, box drawing, a code point the font lacks, a control, an invalid byte, clipping at the right and bottom edges) and prints `gfxcheck: font checksum <n>, match true` against `GfxCheck::ExpectedFontChecksum` (1648750494), which reads the file and draws with plain loops, and `gfxcheck: font sans 16, line 16, text ends at 157, console 8x16`. It exits 1 and prints `gfxcheck: expected <n>` on a mismatch. It needs no display; the SHELL session runs it on both architectures (`tools/smoke/cases/shell/swift_cxx.py`), and the host test `the picture gfxcheck draws matches its independent checksum` checks the same value (1833110056).
- `featherfall [--seed N] | --selftest | --demo [--seed N]` (Embedded Swift with C++ interop, `src/user/featherfall/`, needs `deps/swift`) is an original falling-block puzzle for a virtual console. With no arguments it takes the screen through `/dev/fb0` and plays (`--seed N` fixes the order of pieces; a pseudo-terminal or a screen under 800x768 is refused with a message): a 10x20 well with a ghost outline of where the piece lands, panels with the held piece, score, level, lines and the next three pieces, and 1 to 4 lines scoring 100/300/500/800 times the level plus 1 and 2 points per soft- and hard-dropped row. Keys: Left and Right move (the game's own auto-repeat, 170 ms then 50 ms), Down soft-drops, Space hard-drops, Up or X turns clockwise, Z anticlockwise, C or Shift holds, P pauses, Esc quits, Ctrl+C quits too; switching to another console pauses the game and coming back redraws it (P resumes). At game over a score above 0 that makes the top 10 asks for three initials (letters, Backspace, Enter saves, Esc skips); the table of ten shows the new entry highlighted, Enter or Space starts a new game. The table is `$HOME/.featherfall` (`/tmp/.featherfall` without `HOME`), lines such as `ABC 1234` (three capital letters, a space, at most nine digits); a file that does not parse is ignored and replaced by the next save, and a failed save is reported on standard error once the screen is released. The game is a pure state machine (`game.swift`: `Game.step(Inputs, milliseconds:)` over a 10x22 well with two hidden rows, a seeded SplitMix64 bag of seven, hold, ghost, kicks in place/left/right/up, a 500 ms lock delay with at most 15 resets), `render.swift` draws with the graphics library and presents only the changed rectangles, `play.swift` is the 10 ms loop (at most 50 presents a second) and `scores.swift` the table. `--selftest` prints `featherfall: selftest ok` or `featherfall: selftest failed: <group>: <detail>` (exit 1; groups include the high score parser, ordering, truncation and a file round trip in `/tmp/.featherfall-selftest`); `--demo` plays a fixed script without a display (a greedy placement search plus scripted holds, a pause, soft drops and waits for gravity, 300 pieces) and prints `featherfall: demo seed N score S lines L level V hash H`, where the hash is a 64-bit FNV-1a of the well; the values are the same on both architectures (seed 1: score 82553, lines 104, level 11, hash 6636180809909333056; seed 2: 84561, 106, 11, 385706885443829982). `GRAPHICS_TEST` runs `--selftest` and the two demos before `fbdemo` and checks the exact lines, and plays the game last (below); the SHELL part `swift_cxx.py` runs `featherfall -h` and a bad seed (and `wickdeep -h` and a bad seed).
- `wickdeep [--seed N] | --selftest | --check-save PATH | --demo [--seed N] [--save-at TURN PATH]` (Embedded Swift with C++ interop, `src/user/wickdeep/`, needs `deps/swift`; design `docs/design/M33.md`) is an original turn-based dungeon crawler of eight floors lit by a lamp that runs down (M33). With no arguments it takes the console's screen (at least 1024x768) and plays; `--seed N` fixes a new run's seed (else `CLOCK_REALTIME`), and a save on disk is resumed instead. Keys: arrows or keypad 8 4 6 2 move or attack, keypad 7 9 1 3 move diagonally, Space or keypad 5 waits, Enter takes the stairs, `i` opens the pack (Up and Down choose, Enter uses or equips, `d` drops, Esc or `i` closes), `h` shows help, Esc outside an overlay or Ctrl+C (the key or `SIGINT`) saves and quits; a press or a kernel repeat is one command and releases are ignored. The screen shows the whole floor of 60x40 cells of 16 pixels (unseen cells black, remembered ones dim and without monsters or items, lit ones in colour) above a panel with hit points, oil, floor, level, embers, attack, defense, score, the last three messages and a key hint, and draws only what changed; the pack, help and the death or victory screen with the hall of runs are boxes over the middle. The art is 28 tiles of 16x16 pixels drawn as palette-indexed text in `art.cpp` (`Graphics::TileSheet`, module `WickdeepArt`). Switching consoles away and back redraws everything. A new start resumes the save, and a save that fails its checks is renamed `.wickdeep-save.bad` and a new run starts with a message saying why; death or victory deletes the save and ranks the run in the hall. The rules (`rules.swift`) are a pure state machine, `Game.step(Command) -> [Event]`, over integers and arrays only (no `Dictionary`, `Set` or floating point), so every architecture computes the same game: moves in eight directions (a diagonal may not squeeze past a wall), waiting and taking the stairs; the lamp holds 0 to 1000 oil, burns 1 per turn and shows a radius of 6, 5, 3, 2 or 1 cells (500, 200 and 50 oil, above 0, out); turns follow the energy order of `Turns` (speeds 6, 12, 18; the player and the monsters of the floor). Floor *n* of seed *s* (`map.swift`) is built from SplitMix64 (`featherfall/random.swift`, shared) seeded with `s ^ (n * 0x9E3779B97F4A7C15)`: 4x3 sectors with a room or a one-cell junction each, joined by a random spanning tree and two extra links with L-shaped corridors, rubble that never cuts a path, the stairs (floor 8: the plinth) in the room farthest from the start. The field of view (`sight.swift`) is integer recursive shadowcasting with the radius test `dx*dx + dy*dy <= r*r + r`; seen cells are remembered. Monsters and items (`actors.swift`, `items.swift`): 3 + depth monsters (at most 16) and 4 + depth / 2 items per floor in the rooms but the start room, eight monsters (dustmite, gnawling in pairs, oilmoth that drinks 25 oil instead of biting, sootcrawler, shardback, rootgrip that never moves, bellwraith, and the Hearthwarden of floor 8 that sleeps until hit), all asleep until they stand in the light (1 in 3 per turn) or are hit, chasing along one breadth-first map per turn within 24 steps; hit chance 70% plus 5 per point of attack over defense, between 20% and 95%, damage attack - defense / 2 plus -1 to 1, at least 1; kills give embers, levels at 10, 25, 50, 90, 140, 200 and 270 embers (+4 hit points, +1 attack); ten items (oil flask, salve, flare stone, blink feather, cartographer's moth, three weapons, two coats) in a pack of 10, picked up by stepping, `use`, `equip` and `drop` commands. Taking the Heartwick from the plinth wins (1000 points), death ends the run. `--selftest` prints `wickdeep: selftest ok` or `wickdeep: selftest failed: <group>: <detail>` (exit 1). `--demo` plays a bot that explores over what it has seen, then takes the stairs, with no display and prints `wickdeep: demo turns T depth D level L score S outcome O hash H` (the hash is a 64-bit FNV-1a of the encoded save; outcome `victory`, `dead` or `limit` after 20,000 turns; the bot fights, heals, refills the lamp, equips and uses a flare stone as the design says and walks to items in view; seed 1: `turns 2690 depth 8 level 7 score 1945 outcome victory hash 10821229194343717296`, seed 2: `turns 703 depth 3 level 2 score 223 outcome dead hash 18362298982438112458`, the same on both architectures). Saves (`save.swift`): `Game.encode()` is every stored field of the game in a little-endian file (header `WDSV`, `u16` version 1, `u16` 0, `u32` payload length, `u64` FNV-1a of the payload; about 4.9 KB) and `Game.decode` refuses a damaged one with `size`, `short`, `magic`, `version`, `reserved`, `length` (also trailing bytes), `checksum`, `range`, `kind`, `count` or `position`; any rule change that alters the payload bumps the version and an older save is refused with `version`. `wickdeep --check-save PATH` prints `wickdeep: save ok depth D turn T` or `wickdeep: save rejected <reason>` (exit 1), and `--demo ... --save-at TURN PATH` writes the encoded state when the bot reaches that turn and then goes on. The game's files (`runs.swift`) are the save `$HOME/.wickdeep-save` (`/tmp/.wickdeep-save` without `HOME`), written to `.tmp` and renamed into place, renamed to `.wickdeep-save.bad` when refused, and the hall of runs `$HOME/.wickdeep-runs` (the 10 best, `score depth turns outcome` per line with outcome `dead` or `victory`; a file that does not parse is ignored and replaced).
- `cxxtest` checks the C++ standard library's headers as `std` (see "C++ Standard Library") and prints `cxxtest: <n> checks passed`, or each failed check.
- `uptime` prints the time since boot as `H:MM:SS`.
- `ping [-6] [-c count] <host>` sends ICMP echo requests to an IPv4 or IPv6 address or a name (see Networking).
- `dhcp` leases an IPv4 address and holds it (`dhcp -r` renews now), `net` shows or changes the network settings and takes the link down or up, and `host [-4|-6] <name>` or `host <address>` looks up a name or an address (see Networking).
- `kill [-signal] <pid>...` sends `SIGTERM`, or the named or numbered signal, to each process, or to a group as `-group`, which `SIGTERM` and `SIGHUP` follow with `SIGCONT` so a stopped job ends; `kill -l` lists the signals. In the shell, `kill %n` names job `n`.
- `keys` switches the console to raw input and prints the name of each key pressed (`up`, `ctrl-a`, `page-down`, `escape`, …), Ctrl+C included, until `q`. It decodes keys with `src/user/key_decoder.h`, which also reads the SS3 and `ESC [ 1 ~` forms some terminals send, and waits a tenth of a second to tell a lone Escape from the start of a sequence.
- `vi [file]` is a small vi (`src/user/vi_editor.cpp`). It draws on the alternate screen, so the shell's screen comes back unchanged when it exits; its keys are in the [readme](../readme.md#vi). It keeps the whole file in memory, scrolls long lines sideways instead of wrapping them, shows tabs as 8 columns and other control bytes as `?`, and writes the file with a final newline. Undo keeps up to 100 whole-buffer snapshots or 512 KiB of them, dropping the oldest. Ctrl+Z, in any mode, leaves the alternate screen and stops it; `fg` brings the screen back. Not supported: visual mode, named registers, `.`, macros, `f`/`t`, operators with motions (`dw`, `cw`), regular expressions in searches, and more than one file.
- `script [-a] [-q] [-c command] [file]` runs a shell (`$SHELL`, or `shell`), or `shell -c command`, on a new pseudo-terminal and saves everything the session prints to `file` (`typescript` by default; `-a` appends), between `Script started on <time>` and `Script done on <time>` lines; `-q` leaves out the start and end messages on the screen. It switches its own terminal to raw input, so Ctrl+C and every other key reach the session's line discipline as bytes, and relays keys to the master and output back over `poll`, with the master non-blocking so a session busy writing cannot stall its input. It copies its terminal's size to the pty at the start and again on each `SIGWINCH`, so a `script` inside `script` follows the outer one's size. The session ends at end of file on the master or, since nothing announces a child's exit, once `waitpid` finds the shell gone and the master is drained; `script` then closes the master, which hangs up anything still on the pty, and exits with the shell's status.
- `stty [-F device] [size] [rows n] [cols n]` shows a terminal's size and its `icanon`, `echo`, `isig` and `icrnl` modes, or with `size` only `rows columns`; `rows` and `cols` set the size, which sends `SIGWINCH` to that terminal's foreground group when it changes. `-F` opens another terminal, such as `/dev/pts/0`, instead of standard input.
- `shell -c command` runs one command line (pipelines and redirections included) and exits with its status, or 128 plus the signal that ended it.
- `clear` clears the screen below the status bar with `ESC [ H ESC [ 2 J`, as Ctrl+L does at the prompt.
- `ps` lists every process from `/proc`: ID, parent, group, state, the CPU it last ran on, its CPU time, and its name.
- `top [-n count] [-d milliseconds]` samples `/proc` every second (or `-d`, at least 100 ms) and prints the processes busiest first, with `%CPU` of one CPU and the whole system's use, On the console, without `-n`, it takes the alternate screen and redraws the table in place, cut to the screen's height, until `q`, Escape or Ctrl+C (raw input, waiting for keys between refreshes), and Ctrl+Z stops it until `fg`; with `-n`, or when its output is not the console, it prints `count` tables one after another.

Every program except the boot self-test prints its usage and purpose for `<name> -h` or `<name> --help` (`HelpRequested` in `src/user/command_line.h`), and the shell's `help` points to this. To add a program, create `src/user/<name>.cpp` with a `main(argc, argv)` and add `<name>:<name>` to `FEATHEROS_PROGRAMS` in `src/user/CMakeLists.txt`. `FEATHEROS_START_PROGRAMS` is for programs that define `_start(const ProgramArguments*)` themselves and skip the C library's startup, which only `test_program` does. `filetest` and `hardening` still call `src/user/syscall.h` directly where they test the kernel's exact errors.

### Swift programs

Programs written in Embedded Swift are listed in `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_PROGRAMS` as `<name>:<source>`, with the source in `src/user/<source>.swift`, or in the directory `src/user/<source>/` for a program of several files, whose top-level code is in `main.swift`. Each one is a single module whose top-level code becomes `main`, compiled whole (`-wmo`, which Embedded Swift requires) at the build type's optimization for `x86_64-unknown-none-elf` or `aarch64-none-none-elf` into one object. That object holds the parts of the Swift standard library and runtime the program uses, and links with the C library's startup into the same static-PIE program as a C++ one. `import FeatherLibC` brings in every C library header but `assert.h`, through the module map in `src/libc/swift/module.modulemap` (Clang reads the headers with `-ffreestanding`; its module cache is under `build/<arch>/src/user/swift/`). Top-level code does not see `main`'s parameters and Embedded Swift has no `CommandLine`, so `FeatherSupport` provides one over `featheros_argument_count()` and `featheros_argument_values()`. Swift does not import variadic C functions such as `printf`; `print`, `puts` and `write` work, and `FeatherSupport` gives `open`, `openat`, `fcntl`, `ioctl` and `String(format:)` fixed parameters. The runtime calls `posix_memalign` and `free` for objects and boxes, `putchar` for `print`, `arc4random_buf` to seed hashing and for `random`, and `memset`, `strlen` and `ceil`. Hashing a `String` (as a `Set` element or `Dictionary` key) and working with `Character`s (`count`, iteration) use Unicode normalization and grapheme tables even for ASCII text, so programs link the toolchain's `libswiftUnicodeDataTables.a`. The linker takes only the parts a program uses: about 40 KB for `hello`, and nothing for a program that sticks to bytes (`utf8`, `[UInt8]`). Swift code may use the x86-64 red zone, which the kernel leaves free when it builds a signal frame. `hello` prints its arguments through `CommandLine` and exercises a protocol with generics, a class with reference counting, a `Set` of strings, a `Character` count, a random number, `String(format:)` (with an `Int.min` and an argument of the wrong type), C calls including `open`, `openat`, `fcntl` and `ioctl`, and what FeatherOS's standard library adds (`print` to a stream, `AnyHashable`, `withVaList`, and a JSON round trip of `Codable` types the compiler synthesizes, with a decoding error), which the shell smoke test checks.

`import FeatherSupport` brings in the Swift programs' shared code, the module in `src/user/swift_support/`, built once per architecture into `build/<arch>/src/user/swift/support/`. Embedded Swift serializes every function body into the `.swiftmodule`, so a program compiles in the parts it uses; the `featheros_swift_support` archive it also links is only a fallback. The module has `CommandLine` (`argc`, `unsafeArgv` and `arguments`, as in the standard library outside Embedded Swift), `Arguments` (`operands` without the program, `programName` as the last part of the path, `helpRequested` for a lone `-h` or `--help`), `InputFile(path:)` and `InputFile.standard` (`readAll()` and `readLine()` return bytes, a line without its newline and `nil` at the end), `Output.standard` and `Output.error` (`write` of a `String` or bytes through `fwrite`, so the text keeps its order with `print`, and `flush`), `print` of several `CustomStringConvertible` items with a `separator`, through `Output.standard`, `SystemError` (a code with `strerror` text as its `description`; `SystemError.current` reads `errno`, which the module provides as a settable property since Swift does not import the C macro), and `ExitCode` with `exit(.success)`/`exit(.failure)`, `warn(_:)` (the program's name, then the message, on standard error) and `fail(_:)` (`warn`, then exit 1), `json`'s `JSONValue`, `JSONParser` and printers, `JSONEncoder` and `JSONDecoder` (see "Codable"), and `RegularExpression` over the C library's `regex.h`: `RegularExpression(pattern, options:)` compiles an extended expression by default (`.extended` left out of the options gives a basic one; `.ignoreCase`, `.newlineSensitive`) or throws a `RegularExpressionError` with `regerror`'s text, and `matches(_:)`, `firstMatch(in:)` and `allMatches(in:)` (each after the end of the one before, an empty one moving on a byte) search a `String`. A `Match` has the matched `range`, the `groups` (`nil` for a subexpression that took no part) and `match[n]` as a `Substring`. It matches bytes, so a range may split a character of several UTF-8 bytes. Functions that can fail use typed throws, `throws(SystemError)`, and are called inside `do throws(SystemError)`; Untyped `throws` would work too, but its callers would get an `any Error` to cast back to `SystemError`. C's `open`, `openat`, `fcntl` and `ioctl` are variadic, so the module defines them with the fixed parameters of Glibc's Swift overlay (`open(path, flags)`, `open(path, flags, mode)`, the same for `openat` after a directory, `fcntl(descriptor, command)` with an optional `Int32` or pointer, `ioctl(descriptor, request)` with an optional `Int32` or pointer), over `featheros_open` and its siblings in the C library; overload resolution picks them over the unavailable C declarations. `String(format:_:)` formats like `printf` through `featheros_format`, which shares the engine in `src/libc/format.h`: its arguments conform to `FormatArgument` (every integer type, `Double`, `Float`, `String`, `Substring` and pointers), whose `formatValue` becomes a tagged C argument. Integers keep all 64 bits unless `hh` or `h` asks for fewer, and a conversion without a suitable argument is written as it appears instead of reading the wrong type. `regex [-Bia] <pattern> [file...]` prints the first match of an extended expression on each line (every one with `-a`, a basic expression with `-B`, ignoring case with `-i`) after the line's number, with each subexpression in brackets or `-` if it took no part: `regex -a "([a-z]+)=([0-9]+)( )?"` prints `1: id=7 [id] [7] -`. `rev [-n] [file...]` prints each line reversed by `Character`, so an accented letter of several code points stays together, and `-n` numbers the lines with `String(format:)`; it warns about a file it cannot open, goes on with the rest and exits 1.

`json [-c] [-r] [-t type] [path [file...]]` (`src/user/json/`) is a small jq: it reads the JSON values in the files, or in its input without any (several values separated by white space, as jq reads them), and prints what the path selects from each, pretty with two spaces by default, one line each with `-c`, and strings without quotes or escapes with `-r`. A path is `.` for the whole value, or steps of `.name` or `."name"` (with JSON escapes) for a member, `[n]` for an element (negative from the end), `["name"]`, and `[]` for every element or member value; the dot before a bracket is optional. As in jq, a missing member or element, or any step into `null`, gives `null`, and a repeated key keeps its first place and its last value; indexing another kind of value is an error that skips that value. The parser (`json_parser.swift`, in `FeatherSupport` since `JSONDecoder` reads through it) follows RFC 8259 over bytes into an `indirect enum JSONValue`: strings and keys are UTF-8 bytes with escapes and surrogate pairs decoded and raw UTF-8 validated, numbers keep their text, so they print as written, and nesting stops at 256 levels for the 252 KiB stack. A parse error names the line and column (in characters) and what was found, for example `json: standard input: line 1, column 4: expected a value, found ','`; values before it are still printed. The printers (`json_printer.swift`) share one walk in an extension of the `JSONPrinter` protocol, which `CompactPrinter` and `PrettyPrinter` implement with their separators and line breaks; output escapes `"`, `\`, control characters and DEL, and leaves other UTF-8 as it is. Working on bytes rather than `String` keeps the Unicode data tables out of the binary. `-t type` decodes each result with `JSONDecoder` into the Swift types a type expression names and prints it encoded again with `JSONEncoder`, pretty or with `-c` on one line (and a string raw with `-r`): `bool`, `string`, `double`, `float`, `int`, `int8` to `int64`, `uint`, `uint8` to `uint64`, `[T]` for an array, `{T}` for an object, and `T?` for an optional (`typed.swift`). The type is known only while the program runs, so containers decode their elements through `superDecoder`, and the root value finds the expression in `userInfo`. A result that does not decode prints the `DecodingError` and is skipped, as `json -c -t "{int?}" .milestones /system/sample.json` shows: `json: /system/sample.json: DecodingError.typeMismatch. Path: next. Debug description: Expected a number but found a string instead.` `json -h` prints the path and type syntax. `unit-test` also builds `src/user/json` without `main.swift`, with `FeatherSupport`'s value, parser and printers, and `tests/unit/swift/json_tests.swift` for the host with the toolchain's ordinary standard library (`-sdk` from `xcrun` on macOS) and runs it as the CTest case `swift.json`; without a Swift toolchain the case is skipped. The shell smoke test reads `/system/sample.json` (from `initramfs/sample.json`) and piped input, and `NETWORK_TEST` pipes `fetch` of the harness's `/status.json` into it.

`life [-p glider|blinker|random] [-n generations] [-s columnsxrows]` (`src/user/life/`) plays Conway's Game of Life on a grid whose edges wrap, starting from a glider or blinker in the middle, or a quarter of the cells at random (the default). Without `-n` it takes the alternate screen and draws a generation every tenth of a second, with the generation and population on the last row, until `q` or Ctrl+C; standard input must be a terminal, and the grid must fit it. Raw mode with `VMIN` 0 and `VTIME` 1 makes each read wait at most a tenth of a second, which paces the generations, and only rows that changed are drawn again. With `-n` it prints the grid after that many generations, `#` for a live cell and `.` for a dead one, to any output. The grid is the terminal's size above its last row by default (from `tcgetwinsize`), or 80×23 without a terminal; `-s` sets it, from 3x3 to 1000x1000. `grid.swift` and `pattern.swift` import nothing, and `unit-test` builds them with `tests/unit/swift/life_tests.swift` as the CTest case `swift.life`, with a seeded generator for the random start. The shell smoke test checks a glider after four generations, a blinker, the size without a terminal and the errors, and animates a glider until `q`.

### Swift concurrency

Programs in `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_CONCURRENCY_PROGRAMS` use `async`/`await`, tasks and actors. Their top-level code may `await`, and runs on `MainActor`. They link `featheros_swift_concurrency`, Swift's concurrency runtime built from source for both targets by `src/user/swift_concurrency/CMakeLists.txt`. The toolchain's own runtime exists only for x86-64, was compiled against Darwin's headers and has no threads.

`build.sh` fetches what the runtime build reads when it finds a Swift toolchain. These are shallow, sparse clones, about 50 MB together:
- `deps/swift`: the `swift-<.swift-version>-RELEASE` tag, with the standard library (see "Swift's standard library"), Concurrency, threading, runtime and shim sources and headers, and the `Cxx` overlay (see "Swift and C++"). `src/user/swift_concurrency/swift.patch` is applied to it, and applied again from a clean tree whenever the patch changes.

`fullclean` removes them. Without them, CMake says so and skips every Swift program, since they all use the standard library built from these sources.

The runtime build follows upstream's Embedded Swift build, with these differences:
- **C++ half:** compiled with the toolchain's `clang++` at `-O2` as C++20 (upstream uses C++17; our headers need C++20), over FeatherOS's own C++ headers (`src/libcxx/include`, see "C++ Standard Library") and C headers; `featheros_cxx` comes after it in each program's link. It uses `<atomic>` (16-byte compare-exchanges through `cmpxchg16b`), `<optional>`, `<chrono>`, `<bitset>` (`to_string` in a debug path), `<queue>` over `deque` for a task group's ready queue, `<set>`, `<unordered_set>`, `<string>` and `aligned_storage`. `swift.patch` adds the `#include <stdio.h>` that `TaskAlloc.cpp` relied on libc++ to bring in.
- **Threading:** Swift's pthreads threading package instead of none, so the runtime's mutexes and once-flags are the C library's. Its per-thread values are C++ `thread_local`s in each thread's TLS block.
- **The patch:** it adds `__featheros__` cases to the runtime's clocks (`CLOCK_MONOTONIC` for both the continuous and the suspending clock, as on WASI), to its sleep (`nanosleep`) and to the thread stack bounds (unknown). It also drops an unused `<sys/syscall.h>`. Its `_Deque`, which buffers `AsyncStream` values, takes the capacity it asked for under Embedded Swift, as upstream does on OpenBSD. Otherwise it reads `ManagedBuffer.capacity`, which on a bare-metal target comes from a `_swift_stdlib_malloc_size` shim that returns 0, so the capacity was negative and a second buffered value failed an assertion. It makes the clocks, `Task.sleep`, `Task.checkCancellation` and `MainActor` available under Embedded Swift (see "What Embedded Swift leaves out"). `MainActor.swift` is compiled when the module is built with `-D FEATHEROS`, as it is on WASI, and `swift_task_enqueue` sends the main executor's jobs to `swift_task_enqueueMainExecutor`, where upstream's Embedded build has no executor but the generic one and actors. Upstream's sleep fallback cast a generic clock to `ContinuousClock` or `SuspendingClock`, which Embedded Swift rejects, so those two clocks conform to an internal `_TimestampingClock` protocol and convert their own instants. For custom executors, `swift_task_enqueue` hands jobs for any other serial executor, and for a task's preferred task executor, to `executors.swift` (below) where upstream's Embedded build stops with `swift_unreachable`, and task creation accepts an owned task executor, as `addTask(executorPreference:)` passes. When the current executor is not the expected one, `swift_task_isCurrentExecutor` asks the expected executor's `isIsolatingCurrentContext`, then its `checkIsolated`, with Swift 6's crashing checks, as upstream does outside Embedded Swift. It skips complex equality, whose `isSameExclusiveExecutionContext(other: Self)` needs the executor's type at run time.
- **Executor:** `src/user/swift_concurrency/global_executor.cpp` instead of upstream's `CooperativeGlobalExecutor.cpp` (see below).
- **Swift half:** only the `_Concurrency` module, compiled with every availability macro set to `*`. Its three `.gyb` sources are expanded with Swift's `utils/gyb.py`, which needs `python3`. Embedded Swift compiles the parts a program uses into the program, and programs find this module first through `-I`. The exception is `src/user/swift_concurrency/executors.swift`, compiled once into the runtime's archive: the functions the runtime calls to reach an executor written in Swift (enqueue a job on a serial or task executor, `checkIsolated`, `isIsolatingCurrentContext`, and the reference an executor gives for itself). Upstream writes them as generic functions over the executor's type, which Embedded Swift emits only into programs that call them, and the runtime passes them an executor reference's object, type and witness table. These rebuild an `any SerialExecutor` or `any TaskExecutor` from the object and witness table, which is how Embedded Swift lays out an existential of a class-bound protocol.

`src/user/swift_concurrency/platform.cpp`, compiled with the runtime, provides what neither the C library nor Embedded Swift's standard library has:
- `swift_getObjectType` (an Embedded Swift object starts with its class metadata), `swift_reportError` (to standard error) and `swift_getMainExecutor`, which asks the global executor for the main executor.
- `swift::getResilientMetadataBounds`, which only distributed actors reach. It prints that they are not supported, and aborts.

The global executor (`global_executor.cpp`, which full Swift's `_Concurrency` uses too, through its `swift_dispatch*` functions) keeps one queue of jobs, ordered by priority and then by arrival, behind a pthread mutex and condition variable:
- **Threads:** the first job starts a worker thread for every online CPU (`sysconf(_SC_NPROCESSORS_ONLN)`). With 4 CPUs a program has 5 threads, with the main thread, of the 16 a process may have.
- **Delayed jobs:** they wait in a list sorted by deadline on `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, one for each queue (full Swift's main executor can delay jobs too), and a thread with nothing ready sleeps in `pthread_cond_timedwait` until the first one is due. `Task.sleep` and a clock's `sleep` queue one each: a job that resumes the sleeping task. Cancelling the task resumes it at once with `CancellationError`, and the job later finds the sleep over and only frees its state. The kernel's monotonic clock advances by 10 ms ticks, so a sleep lasts at least its time, rounded up to a tick.
- **Main actor:** `MainActor`'s jobs wait in a second queue, which only the main thread runs. A program's top-level code (or an `@main` type's async `main`) runs its first part at once on the main thread, then ends in `swift_task_asyncMainDrainQueue`, which runs the main queue until the main task exits the program. The main executor is an ordinary executor to the runtime, whose identity and witness table are two static blocks the runtime only compares. `checkIsolated` and `isIsolatingCurrentContext` answer for it by asking whether the caller is on the main thread (`swift::Thread::onMainThread`, the thread that ran the runtime's constructors), so `MainActor.assumeIsolated` works from synchronous code there.

Jobs on different actors, and tasks that are not on an actor, now run at the same time, so their output interleaves differently from run to run.

`tasks` (`src/user/tasks.swift`) prints a task's value, two tasks taking turns through `Task.yield` (each task's entries reach the shared actor in order), 100 child tasks of a task group incrementing an actor, and a task group's sum. It also prints `async let`, a checked continuation resumed by another task, an `AsyncStream` fed by another task, one whose 100 values are all buffered before the first is read, a cancelled task, an error thrown by a task and caught by its pattern, and a `@TaskLocal` value seen by a child task but not a detached one. Then it sleeps through `Task.sleep(nanoseconds:)`, `Task.sleep(for:)` and `Task.sleep(until:clock: .suspending)` and checks the time against `ContinuousClock`, cancels a task sleeping for 60 s, which must end in under a second with `CancellationError`, and catches `CancellationError` from `Task.checkCancellation()` in a cancelled task. Last, it checks where code ran, comparing `pthread_self` with the main thread's: top-level code and `MainActor.assumeIsolated` on the main thread; from a nonisolated async function on a worker, a `@MainActor` function, a `MainActor.run` closure (which calls `assumeIsolated`) and a `@MainActor` task on the main thread, and a detached task off it; and top-level code still on the main thread after all of that.

`taskprimes [tasks] [limit]` (`src/user/taskprimes.swift`) counts the primes below the limit (1 000 000 by default) in a task group of 16 tasks by default, each taking every sixteenth odd number. It prints the count and time, then the number of threads the tasks ran on (`pthread_self`) and the CPUs they ran on (`sched_getcpu` at each task's start and end). Compare it with `primes`, which uses processes.

`executors` (`src/user/executors.swift`) has two custom executors over a queue that its own threads run: `ActorThread`, a `SerialExecutor` with one thread, and `Pool`, a `TaskExecutor` with two. The queue also takes plain closures, which run outside any job. It checks, comparing `pthread_self` with the executors' threads:
- an actor whose `unownedExecutor` is an `ActorThread` runs 100 calls from a task group on that thread, and resumes there after `Task.sleep`;
- `assumeIsolated` (after `preconditionIsolated` and `assertIsolated`) from a closure on that thread, outside any job, passes through the executor's `isIsolatingCurrentContext`, and for an `ActorThread` that answers `nil` there, through its `checkIsolated`;
- 16 tasks added with `addTask(executorPreference:)` run on the pool;
- under `withTaskExecutorPreference`, a nonisolated async function, `async let` and a default actor run on the pool; the actor with its own executor still runs on its thread, an unstructured `Task` does not inherit the preference, and under `withTaskExecutorPreference(globalConcurrentExecutor)`, which means no preference, a child task runs on the global executor;
- `Task(executorPreference:)` runs on the pool.

The shell smoke test checks every line of `tasks` and `executors` on both targets, and runs `taskprimes 16 100000`. It checks the count, and that the tasks ran on at least two threads and two CPUs; on QEMU's 4 CPUs they run on all four.

### Swift and C++

Programs in `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_CXX_PROGRAMS` import C++ headers: they are compiled with `-cxx-interoperability-mode=default`, C++23, and neither exceptions nor RTTI, as the C++ programs are. The headers they may import are modules in `src/user/swift_cxx/module.modulemap`: `HttpMessage` (`http_message.h`), `CxxInterop` (`cxx_interop.h`), `FeatherGraphics` (the graphics library, see "Graphics library") and `GfxCheck` (`gfx_check.h`). GCC builds the C++ they call like other user code, into `featheros_user_support` or, through `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_CXX_SOURCES_<name>`, into the program alone; Swift reaches it through the Itanium C++ ABI that both compilers follow. Full-Swift programs can import the same modules (see "Full Swift"). Clang inside swiftc reads FeatherOS's C++ library (`-nostdinc++`, `src/libcxx/include` as a system directory), the same headers GCC reads, so standard library types pass between GCC-built C++ and Swift; `src/libcxx/swift/module.modulemap` makes them the Clang module `std`, which Swift imports as `CxxStdlib`. These programs also `import Cxx`, Swift's overlay that makes C++ types into Swift collections and optionals. Upstream builds it only outside Embedded Swift; `src/user/swift_cxx/CMakeLists.txt` builds it from `deps/swift/stdlib/public/Cxx` as a module only, with the runtime's availability macros, and `swift.patch` adds the `import Builtin` its inlinable code needs under Embedded Swift. Next to it, the same file builds upstream's `CxxStdlib` overlay (`stdlib/public/Cxx/std`: `std.swift`, `String.swift`, `Chrono.swift`), unchanged, over module `std` and the toolchain's own `CxxStdlibShim` module (whose header includes `<chrono>`, which is why the durations came ahead of M26 Part 4). Like the concurrency programs, they are skipped when `deps/` lacks the sources.

What works, checked by `cxxinterop` (`src/user/cxxinterop.swift`) on both targets:
- **Declarations:** namespaces (as nested Swift names, `FeatherOS.User.Interop`), free and static member functions, `constexpr` constants, `enum class` (a Swift enum), structs with public fields (with a memberwise initializer), member functions (non-`const` ones are `mutating`), operators such as `+` and `==`, and class templates through a type alias (`using IntBox = Box<int>`).
- **Values:** C++ classes are Swift value types whose constructors, copy constructors and destructors run when Swift creates, copies and drops them. A class whose copy constructor is deleted is noncopyable (`~Copyable`), moved with `consuming` and its move constructor. A virtual method called on the object itself works.
- **Pointers and text:** a Swift `String` passes as `const char*`, a returned `const char*` becomes a `String` through `String(cString:)`, `const T&` parameters take values, `T*` out parameters take `&value`, and a returned pointer is an optional. A `char` array field is a tuple, read through `withUnsafeBytes(of:)`.
- **Shared references:** a class annotated as a shared reference (`import_reference` with `retain:` and `release:` functions, as `<swift/bridging>`'s `SWIFT_SHARED_REFERENCE` writes it; `cxx_interop.h` spells the attributes itself, since GCC has no such header) is a Swift class whose reference count is the C++ object's own. A factory marked `returns_retained` hands over its reference, and Swift releases it after the last use.
- **The standard library:** `std::optional` (`.value` from `Cxx`), `std::vector` (iterated with `for`, copied with `Array(_:)`) and `std::span` made from an `UnsafeBufferPointer`, all returned or taken by GCC-built functions, and `std::string_view` from a string literal. Through `CxxStdlib`: `std::string` from a string literal or a `String`, `String(std.string)`, `+=`, `==` and hashing, and `std.chrono` durations to and from `Duration`. Constructors Swift calls must exist without their default arguments, which Swift does not import; `basic_string` declares its `(pointer, count)`, `(pointer)` and `(count, character)` forms separately, as libc++ does.

What does not:
- A derived class where its base class is expected: Swift's value types have no inheritance, so `Square` does not pass as a `const Shape&`.
- Retain and release functions in a namespace or as static members: Swift looks them up only at global scope (`RetainInteropNode`/`ReleaseInteropNode`).

`http url <url> [reference...]`, `http request [file]` and `http response [file]` (`src/user/http.swift`) parse HTTP with `http_message.cpp`, the C++ of `fetch` and `httpd`. `url` prints a URL's host, port and path, then each reference resolved against it (`../d resolves to http://example.com:8080/a/d`). `request` feeds its input to `RequestParser`, one head at a time, and prints each head's method and target, the file path the target names with its content type, the version and whether the connection stays open; it stops at a refused head (`refused with 505 HTTP Version Not Supported`) or one with a body. `response` feeds `ResponseParser` and prints the final status and any `Location`, then the body without its framing; it warns and exits 1 when the response is malformed or cut short. Both read the file, or their input without one, with `read` in chunks of `BUFSIZ`. The shell smoke test runs `cxxinterop`, resolves URLs, and parses `/system/http-request.txt` (two heads and an HTTP/2.0 one) and `/system/http-response.txt` (a `100 Continue`, then a chunked body), from `initramfs/`.

### Graphics library

`src/user/graphics/` (namespace `FeatherOS::Graphics`, in `featheros_user_support`; design `docs/design/M32.md` D13) is what programs use instead of the raw `/dev/fb0` calls. Colors are `0x00RRGGBB` (the top byte is dropped), and every drawing call is clipped to its surface, whatever the coordinates.
- `Surface` (`surface.h`) owns a `width * height` array of pixels (zeroed; at most 16384 in each direction, else `Valid()` is false), copies deeply and moves. `Fill`, `FillRectangle`, `DrawRectangle` (a one-pixel outline), `Blit` and `BlitKeyed` (pixels equal to the transparent color stay as they were, `source` a pointer so that Swift can pass one) and `BlitPart(source, sx, sy, width, height, x, y, keyed, transparent)` (a rectangle of the source, cut to it before the clipping), `DrawText` and `DrawTextOn` (with a background color) draw UTF-8 text in a `Font` (see Fonts below); `PixelAt` (0 outside) and `Checksum` (FNV-1a over the pixels) read back.
- `TileSheet` (`tile_sheet.h`, design `docs/design/M33.md` D8) turns tiles written as text into two atlases. `Load(rows, tile_count, tile_size, palette, palette_size)` takes `tile_count * tile_size` rows of exactly `tile_size` characters, `.` for a transparent pixel and `a` to `p` for the colour at that index of a palette of 1 to 16, and returns a `TileStatus` (`BadArgument`, `BadLength`, `BadCharacter`, `BadIndex`, `BadPalette`, `OutOfMemory`); a refused load leaves the sheet as it was. The *lit* atlas has the palette, the *remembered* one each channel times 3/8 plus 0x10 blue (`TileSheet::Remember`). Transparent pixels are `TileSheet::TransparentColor` (0xFF00FF) in both, so a palette containing it is refused. `Draw(target, tile, x, y, Shade::Lit|Remembered, keyed)` blits one tile clipped to the target (`keyed` skips transparent pixels, unkeyed shows the key colour); a bad tile or a null target draws nothing. The sheet is move-only like `Display`. Host tests in `tests/unit/user/tile_sheet_tests.cpp` (`[tiles]`); `gfxcheck` loads a two-tile sheet and compares the checksum of the picture it draws with `gfx_check.cpp`'s.
- `DirtyRegion` (`dirty_region.h`) keeps up to 8 rectangles clipped to given bounds, merges those that overlap or touch into their bounding box, and becomes the whole bounds past 8 rectangles or once they cover half of it.
- `Display` (`display.h`) opens `/dev/fb0` with `O_NONBLOCK`, reads `FB_GET_INFO`, takes the screen with `FB_ACQUIRE` and owns a `Surface` of the screen's size (`Screen()`). `Invalidate`/`InvalidateAll` mark what changed and `Present` sends the merged rectangles with `FB_PRESENT` from their place in the surface (nothing for a clean surface, kept dirty after a failure). `WaitEvent(timeout_ms, &event)` decodes `fb_event` into `Event {kind, modifiers, code, time_ms}` (`EventKind::Press`, `Repeat`, `Release`, `Shown`, `Hidden`, `Unknown`), reading up to 16 at once and polling only when none are left; it returns `Status::Timeout` or `Status::Interrupted` (`EINTR`) rather than failing. `DroppedEvents` reads `fb_info.dropped_events`, `Descriptor()` lets a program poll it with other descriptors, `Error()` is the `errno` of the last failure, and `Close` (also the destructor) releases the screen. The kernel calls go through the `System` table of function pointers (`DefaultSystem()` is libc), which the host tests replace.
- Nothing in the headers is a template, and the calls take plain integers, pointers and an `enum class` status, so `FeatherGraphics` (`swift_cxx/module.modulemap`) imports as it is: `Surface` is a Swift value type, and `Display::Screen()` returns a pointer, since a returned reference would be a copy. Swift imports a C++ type only if it can be moved, so `Display` has a move constructor (the moved-from one is closed) and becomes a noncopyable Swift struct (`var display = Display()`, released when it goes out of scope); `Screen()` carries `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_INTERIOR_POINTER` (Clang's `swift_attr("import_unsafe")`) because Swift refuses a pointer into the object otherwise. `featherfall` is the example (`src/user/featherfall/play.swift`; `src/user/gfxcheck.swift` is the smaller one).
- Host tests: `tests/unit/user/graphics_tests.cpp` (`[graphics]`) covers clipping, blits and part blits with and without transparency, dirty-rectangle merging and the threshold, and `Display` with stubbed kernel calls (open, acquire and present failures, partial and failing reads, event decoding, release on destruction).

### Fonts

Text uses pre-rendered `FFNT` files in `/system/fonts/` (`mono-16`, `20`, `24`, `32` and `sans-16`, `20`, `24`, `32`, `48`, `64`, named by line height in pixels; JetBrains Mono and Inter under the SIL Open Font License 1.1, whose files and `README.txt` sit beside them), committed in `initramfs/fonts/` and installed by `build.sh`. The format (design `docs/design/M34.md` D7) is a 64-byte header with an FNV-1a checksum, 20-byte glyph records sorted by code point, 4-bit coverage bitmaps, a missing-glyph record 0 and a text section with the name and the licence notice; `src/user/text/font_data.{h,cpp}` parses it (`FontView::Parse` returns a `FontStatus` named `size`, `short`, `magic`, `version`, `header`, `reserved`, `length`, `checksum`, `layout`, `order`, `range`, `missing` or `text`) and `utf8.{h,cpp}` decodes UTF-8. `Graphics::Font` (`graphics/font.{h,cpp}`, move-only, behind a pointer in Swift) is a parsed file: `Load(path)`, `LoadNamed("sans-24")`, `LoadMemory`, `Font::Console()` (the compiled-in `mono-16`; a program that calls it links `console_font.c`'s bytes, others do not), `LineHeight`, `Ascent`, `Descent`, `Advance`, `HasGlyph` and `MeasureText`. `Surface::DrawText(font, x, y, utf8, color)` returns the pen's end and `DrawTextOn(font, ..., background)` fills the measured box first; `y` is the top of the line box, coverage is blended over the pixels there (`Text::Blend`, integers, the same on both architectures and in `ffnt.py`), clipping is in 64 bits, C0 controls and DEL draw nothing and do not advance, a code point the font lacks draws record 0 and invalid UTF-8 draws U+FFFD (a diamond with a question mark in the sans fonts, synthesised by the generator). A normal build only reads the committed files. To change them, edit `tools/fonts/generate.py` and run it in a virtual environment (`python3 -m venv tools/fonts/.venv && tools/fonts/.venv/bin/pip install -r tools/fonts/requirements.txt`, then `python3 tools/fonts/generate.py`, which finds the environment itself and prints each file's size, the code points a family lacks and the glyphs the console cell clips); commit the files it wrote. The unit tests run `tools/fonts/check.py` (`fonts.files`) and `generate.py --check` (`fonts.reproducible`, skipped without freetype-py), which regenerates into a temporary folder and requires identical bytes.

### Desktop pixels and artwork
