M37 (design `docs/design/M37.md`, user space and host tools only, no floating point in `src/user/graphics/` or `src/user/ui/`, so the host and both guests draw the same bytes). `src/user/graphics/` holds the pixel library: `Region`, premultiplied-ARGB `Surface` formats, `Mask`, `Canvas` (clip stack, rounded rectangles, outlines, 9-slice shadows, scaling, text) and the outline loader, renderer and cache. `src/user/ui/` holds the desktop's look: `tokens.{h,cpp}` (themes, accents, metrics, shadow roles, type roles), `art.{h,cpp}` (glyphs, the feather, app tiles, pointers, the wallpaper, `ShadowFor`) and `card.{h,cpp}` (the test card).

- **Tokens.** `tools/gui/tokens.json` is the one source, taken from `docs/design/gui/prototype.html`. `python3 tools/gui/tokens.py` writes `src/user/ui/tokens.{h,cpp}`; `--check` compares without writing. `tools/gui/check.py` (CTest `gui.tokens`) requires the generated files to be current, the prototype to equal the JSON and every theme and accent to pass the contrast pairs (4.5:1). A failing pair means the design is wrong; never change a colour to pass.
- **Artwork.** Nothing is pre-rendered: the system ships one file of outlines, `initramfs/gui/outlines.fvo` (format `FVOL`, installed by `build.sh` as `/system/gui/outlines.fvo`, with `initramfs/gui/SHA256SUMS`), and draws glyphs, app tiles, pointers, the wallpaper and shadows at any size. Sources are `tools/gui/art/icons.json` (45 glyphs), `feather.json`, `wallpaper.json` and the pointer SVGs in `docs/design/gui/cursors/` with `cursors.json`. `python3 tools/gui/outlines.py` flattens them (only `+ - * /` and `sqrt`) and writes the file, `SHA256SUMS` and the generated `src/user/ui/glyph_ids.h` and `pointer_ids.h`; `--check` compares without writing. `tools/gui/fvol.py` reads and writes the format with the loader's refusals. After editing a source or the design's pointers, run `outlines.py` and commit what it wrote. Limits: 65536 points per file, 8192 per drawing (status `Points`), the outline cache holds 2 MiB (per thread; a larger result is not cached and `DrawOutline` renders it straight), a destination over 65536 pixels draws nothing from the cache.
- **Host renderer.** `guirender` is built with the unit tests (`./build.sh unit-test`, `build/unit-tests-ninja/guirender`) from `tests/unit/tools/guirender.cpp` and links the same library. `guirender pointer --name N --size S --accent RRGGBB [--frame F] --system DIR --out FILE` writes a premultiplied PAM; `guirender card --size WxH --page N --theme id --accent id --system DIR --out FILE` writes the PPM that QEMU's `screendump` writes. `DIR` is `initramfs`.
- **Checks.** CTests `gui.tokens`, `gui.files` (`check_art.py files`: the file, its checksums and every drawing and hotspot present), `gui.reproducible` (`outlines.py --check`), `gui.prototype` (`check_art.py prototype`: the art equals the prototype's paths) and `gui.pointers` (`pointers_check.py`: the 13 pointers at 24, 32 and 48 px within a measured distance of the design's PNGs); unit tests `[tokens]`, `[region]`, `[blend]`, `[canvas]`, `[shape]`, `[outlines]`, `[outline_renderer]`, `[nine_slice]`, `[art]`, `[card]`.
- **`PIXELS_TEST=ON`** (`tools/smoke/cases/pixels.py`, matrix cases 100 and 101, `PIXELS_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` 300) runs `uicard` pages 1 to 4 in `dusk` and `hcl`, takes a QMP `screendump` of each after `drawn in` and requires it to equal `guirender card` byte for byte at 1024x768. It needs `./build.sh unit-test` first (it builds `guirender`) and skips otherwise. The drawing times are in design section 7 (Debug page 2 about 1.5 s on x86-64, inside the 2 s budget). The 1920x1080 pair waits for the screen mode switch of M35.

### Swift's standard library

Every Swift module and program is compiled against a `Swift` module built from `deps/swift/stdlib/public/core` by `featheros_add_swift_stdlib` in `src/user/swift_stdlib/swift_stdlib.cmake`, not against the toolchain's prebuilt one. The host tests build the same module for the host. It is built as upstream builds the Embedded standard library: the files that upstream's `CMakeLists.txt` marks `EMBEDDED` (its `.gyb` ones expanded with `utils/gyb.py`, which imports `SwiftIntTypes.py` and `SwiftFloatingPointTypes.py`), the same compiler flags, every availability macro set to `*`, and only a module, since Embedded Swift compiles the parts a program uses into the program. Programs find it first through `-I`, ahead of the toolchain's. The configure-time probe still uses the toolchain's module, and the Unicode data tables are still the toolchain's `libswiftUnicodeDataTables.a`. Building it takes about 30 seconds per architecture. Without `swift.patch`, a program compiles to the same machine code as with the toolchain's module. With the patch as Part 6 left it, `swiftbench` compiled to the same code on both targets. The benchmark below was run again in M25 Part 7, after `json`'s parser moved into `FeatherSupport`, which made `swiftbench` 2 KB smaller; the ratios moved within the usual 10 %.

`swift.patch` adds back these parts of the standard library outside Embedded Swift:
- **`print(_:separator:terminator:to:)`**: upstream's takes `Any...` and finds each item's text through protocol casts. FeatherOS's takes `any CustomStringConvertible...`, which needs no casts. Printing several items to standard output, `print("a", 1, separator: ", ")`, is in `FeatherSupport` (`output.swift`), not in the patch. In the patch, the standard library's own optimization of that non-generic function changed how its code inlined into every program, which made `swiftbench` 3 KB larger.
- **`AnyHashable`**: upstream's box protocol has generic requirements (`_unbox<T>()`, `_downCastConditional<T>(into:)`), which Embedded Swift cannot call through an existential. Under Embedded Swift, two boxes are equal when one casts to the other's concrete box type and their values are equal. `AnyHashable` works as a `Set` element and `Dictionary` key, with implicit conversion (`let key: AnyHashable = 1`) and `base`. An `AnyHashable` passed to `AnyHashable(_:)` is not boxed again. Without the runtime, there is no custom representation (`_HasCustomAnyHashableRepresentation`) and no upcast of a class to its `Hashable` superclass. A downcast (`key as? Int`) also needs the runtime, so the way out is `key.base as? Int`. `description` stays unavailable.
- **`withVaList` and `CVarArg`**: `VarArgs.swift`, which upstream leaves out of Embedded Swift, builds a `va_list` in the x86-64 System V or AArch64 layout for C's `v` functions, such as `vsnprintf`. The builder asked `arg is _CVarArgPassedAsDouble`, a protocol cast, so under Embedded Swift `CVarArg` has a `_cVarArgPassedAsDouble` requirement that defaults to `false` and is `true` for `Float` and `Double`. The `_CVarArgObject` path, which only Foundation's types use, is left out. Converting a whole `[Int]` to `[CVarArg]` is a dynamic cast, so each element is converted on its own (`$0 as CVarArg`).

`hello` checks each one: `print` with several items into a `String`, three distinct values in a `Set<AnyHashable>` of five, a `Dictionary` lookup by an `AnyHashable` key, and `vsnprintf` with eight integers and nine doubles, which is more than the registers hold on either target.

Two more were checked:
- **`String(describing:)`** already works in Embedded Swift for `CustomStringConvertible` and `TextOutputStreamable` values, through upstream's constrained overloads. The unconstrained one needs protocol casts, so it stays unavailable, as do the descriptions of `Array`, `Dictionary`, `Optional` and the other generic types.
- **`Codable`** works, but not in upstream's form; see "Codable" below.

### Codable

`Codable` is in `src/user/swift_stdlib/codable.swift`, compiled into the `Swift` module in place of the unavailable stubs in upstream's `EmbeddedStubs.swift`, which `swift.patch` removes (it also lists the file in `GroupInfo.json`, which the build requires of every file). Upstream's design cannot work under Embedded Swift: `Encoder`'s `container(keyedBy:)` is a generic requirement, and each container wraps a format's container protocol whose `encode<T>` requirements are generic, all called through existentials. The compiler synthesizes conformances as long as it finds `Encodable`, `Decodable`, `CodingKey`, `Encoder` and `Decoder` in the standard library as protocols, and the members its bodies call by name (`container(keyedBy:)`, `encode(_:forKey:)`, `encodeIfPresent`, `nestedContainer`, `superEncoder`, `allKeys`, `decodeIfPresent`, `DecodingError.Context(codingPath:debugDescription:)` and the rest). So:
- `Encoder` and `Decoder` are protocols without requirements, bound to the classes `EncoderBase` and `DecoderBase`, which conform to them; every encoder or decoder is a subclass, and `struct MyEncoder: Encoder` does not compile. `container(keyedBy:)`, `unkeyedContainer()` and `singleValueContainer()` are final methods of the class, and `KeyedEncodingContainer<Key>`, `UnkeyedEncodingContainer`, `SingleValueEncodingContainer` and the decoding three are structs over it with upstream's members (`encode`, `encodeNil`, `encodeIfPresent`, `encodeConditional`, `encode(contentsOf:)`, `nestedContainer`, `nestedUnkeyedContainer`, `superEncoder`, `codingPath`, `count`; `allKeys`, `contains`, `decode`, `decodeNil`, `decodeIfPresent`, `isAtEnd`, `currentIndex`, `superDecoder`), so code that uses containers reads as it does upstream. Their generic methods are specialized where they are called. They are structs rather than protocols, so `any UnkeyedEncodingContainer` is not a type.
- A format overrides methods that take only concrete types, with a `CodingSlot` saying where the value goes: `.value` in a single-value container, `.key(any CodingKey)` in a keyed one, `.element` for the next element of an unkeyed one. An encoder has `beginKeyedContainer()`, `beginUnkeyedContainer()`, `elementCount`, `encodeNil(at:)`, `encode(_:at:)` for `Bool`, `String`, `Double`, `Int64` and `UInt64` (`Float` defaults to `Double`; `Int128` and `UInt128` to 64 bits, with `invalidValue` beyond), and `nestedEncoder(at:)` for any other value and for `superEncoder`. A decoder has the same, `decode(_:at:)` for each primitive type, `allKeys` as strings, `contains`, `elementCount`, `currentIndex`, `isAtEnd` and `moveToNextElement()`: `.element` is the current element, and `DecoderBase` moves on only once it was decoded, so an element that failed to decode can be tried as something else, as with Foundation's decoder. `codingPath` and `userInfo` are properties to override. Smaller integers travel as `Int64` or `UInt64`, and one that does not fit is `dataCorrupted` (`Parsed number <300> does not fit in Int8.`). Primitives and `Optional` write themselves into the slot through `Encodable`'s `_encode(into:at:)` and `Decodable`'s `_decode(from:at:)`, whose defaults go through a nested encoder or decoder.
- The conformances are upstream's: `Bool`, `String`, `Double`, `Float`, `Float16` (not on macOS x86-64, as upstream), every integer type with `Int128` and `UInt128`, raw-value enums of each of those types, `Optional`, `Array`, `ContiguousArray`, `Set`, `Dictionary`, `Range`, `ClosedRange`, `PartialRangeFrom`, `PartialRangeUpTo`, `PartialRangeThrough`, `Duration`, `Never` and `CollectionDifference` (whose upstream conformances `swift.patch` makes available), and `SIMD2` to `SIMD64` when their scalar is `Codable` (upstream makes `Codable` a requirement of `SIMD` itself, outside Embedded Swift). `Dictionary` uses a keyed container when its key is `CodingKeyRepresentable` (`String`, `Int`, and enums that adopt it) and otherwise alternates keys and values in an unkeyed container, as upstream. Upstream finds out through protocol casts; here `Encodable` and `Decodable` have requirements with defaults that `CodingKeyRepresentable` types override, as `CVarArg` has `_cVarArgPassedAsDouble`. An `Int`-keyed dictionary with a key that is not a number is `dataCorrupted` rather than upstream's `typeMismatch`.
- `EncodingError` and `DecodingError` have upstream's cases, `Context` and `dataCorruptedError` helpers, and upstream's descriptions without the value or type, which Embedded Swift cannot print, such as `DecodingError.keyNotFound: Key 'name' not found in keyed decoding container. Path: inner. Debug description: No value associated with key "name".`; both are also `CustomStringConvertible`, since Embedded Swift interpolates only such values. `CodingKey`'s description starts `CodingKey(` instead of the key's type name, a raw value's error names its type from its extension (`Cannot initialize from invalid String value late`), and a range's leaves out its bounds.
- Every synthesized conformance that uses a keyed container warns once: `<unknown>:0: warning: cannot use generic instance method 'container(keyedBy:)' on a value of type 'any Encoder' in Embedded Swift [#EmbeddedRestrictions]`. Embedded Swift warns about a generic method called through an existential even when it can call it, as it can a final method of the class an `any Encoder` is bound to. It cannot be avoided in the library: the compiler looks the member up by its full name, so it cannot be a property with `callAsFunction`, and it opens the existential for any member. Hand-written code silences it with `@diagnose(EmbeddedRestrictions, as: ignored)` on the function (Swift 6.4's source-level warning control), as `json`'s `typed.swift` does; synthesized code has no source location for the attribute to cover, and swiftc can only raise a warning group (`-Wwarning`, `-Werror`), not ignore it. `hello` builds with four such warnings.

`FeatherSupport` has `JSONEncoder` and `JSONDecoder` (`json_encoder.swift`, `json_decoder.swift`). `JSONEncoder().encode(value)` returns bytes, since there is no `Data`, and `encodeTree(value)` the `JSONValue`; `outputFormatting` takes `.prettyPrinted` (the `json` program's layout) and `.sortedKeys`. Values are encoded into a tree whose primitives are stored in place and whose nested containers are nodes, since they are written to after they are handed out; a key encoded twice keeps its first place and its last value. Whole numbers print without Swift's `.0` and a non-finite number throws `invalidValue`, as Foundation's encoder does by default; slashes are not escaped. `JSONDecoder().decode(T.self, from:)` takes bytes holding exactly one value, or a parsed `JSONValue`. An integer may be written with a fraction or exponent if it is whole (`1e3`), and a number beyond a type is `dataCorrupted` (`Parsed JSON number <1e999> does not fit in Double.`). Foundation's key, date, data and non-conforming float strategies are left out.

`unit-test` fetches and patches `deps/swift` too, builds the `Swift` module for the host with `featheros_add_swift_stdlib` (the host's triple from `swiftc -print-target-info`), then `tests/unit/swift/codable_tests.swift` with `FeatherSupport`'s `json_*.swift` in Embedded Swift for the host, at `-Onone` and at `-O`, linked with the toolchain's host `libswiftUnicodeDataTables.a`. They run as the CTest cases `swift.codable.Onone` and `swift.codable.O`, and are skipped without `deps/swift` or Embedded Swift libraries for the host. They round-trip synthesized structs and enums with every primitive type, optionals, nested types, sets, three kinds of dictionary key, ranges, `Duration`, a SIMD vector and raw values of four types, renamed keys, and a class through `superEncoder` and `superDecoder`, check hand-written nested containers with `userInfo` and their coding paths, and compare the JSON text and the descriptions of encoding and decoding errors. On FeatherOS, `hello` and `json -t` exercise them on both targets.

In full Swift (see "Full Swift"), `Encoder`, `Decoder` and the containers are upstream's protocols, so `FeatherSupport`'s `codable_containers.swift` (compiled only without Embedded Swift) defines `EncoderBase`, `DecoderBase` and `CodingSlot` itself with the API above, conforming to `Encoder` and `Decoder` through private containers that implement every requirement in terms of the slot methods. The JSON coders therefore compile unchanged in both modes. A generic `encode<T>` or `decode<T>` finds a primitive with a cast to the internal protocol `SlotPrimitive` (`Bool`, `String`, the floating-point and integer types) and gives it to the format directly; anything else gets a nested encoder or decoder. A `typeMismatch` or `valueNotFound` for a small integer names the type asked for (`Int`), not the `Int64` it travels as. Descriptions are upstream's, which name types and values: `DecodingError.typeMismatch: Expected value of type Int. Path: next. Debug description: ...`. `swift.codable.full` builds `codable_tests.swift` with the JSON coders and `codable_containers.swift` against the host's full standard library. On macOS the program runs on the system's Swift runtime, whose release words the descriptions in its own way, so in full Swift the test compares the parts of each error (case, path, the format's debug description) in Embedded Swift's layout, and only checks that the three errors whose message the library itself writes (a raw value, a range, an `Int` key) fail.

### What Embedded Swift leaves out

Embedded Swift specializes every generic for the types it is used with and keeps no type metadata at run time. So anything that inspects a type while the program runs is gone. The list below was checked against the pinned toolchain (6.4.2): small programs compiled for both FeatherOS targets at `-Onone` and `-O`, with their undefined symbols inspected, and the ones that compiled also ran under the toolchain's Embedded Swift for macOS.
- **Reflection:** no `Mirror`, `dump`, `_typeName`, or `String(describing:)` and `String(reflecting:)` of an arbitrary value. Interpolating a value of a type that does not conform to `CustomStringConvertible` compiles, but prints `(cannot print value in embedded Swift)`, and so does interpolating a type (`\(T.self)`). Upstream's `print` takes a single `String`, `StaticString`, integer, `Bool` or floating-point value, with no `separator`; FeatherOS adds `print(_:separator:terminator:to:)` and, in `FeatherSupport`, `print` of several items, for `CustomStringConvertible` values (see "Swift's standard library").
- **Libraries:** no Foundation, `Regex` or Objective-C interop. Upstream's `Regex` (swift-experimental-string-processing) builds a match's output tuple type while the program runs (`swift_getTupleTypeMetadata`) from captures held as `Any`, then casts it to `Output`, and reads named captures through key paths; full-Swift programs have it (see "Full Swift"). `FeatherSupport`'s `RegularExpression` matches POSIX expressions through the C library's `regex.h` instead (see "Swift programs"). Upstream leaves `Codable` out, since the compiler cannot synthesize conformances over its protocols. FeatherOS's standard library adds `AnyHashable`, `withVaList` and `Codable` back, the last in a form of its own (see "Codable").
- **Concurrency:** `async`/`await`, actors and `Task` need `import _Concurrency` and its runtime, which the toolchain has only for x86-64, without threads; FeatherOS builds it from source with a thread per CPU instead (see "Swift concurrency"). Upstream leaves `MainActor` out of Embedded Swift outside WASI, and marks the clocks (`ContinuousClock`, `SuspendingClock`), `Task.sleep` and `Task.checkCancellation` unavailable; FeatherOS's runtime makes them available. It also implements custom executors (an actor's `unownedExecutor`, `TaskExecutor` preferences), which upstream's Embedded runtime stops at, and makes `withTaskExecutorPreference`, `addTask(executorPreference:)`, `globalConcurrentExecutor`, running a job on a task executor, and an actor's or executor's `preconditionIsolated`, `assertIsolated` and `assumeIsolated` available; their failure messages do not name the actor or executor. Complex equality (`UnownedSerialExecutor(complexEquality:)`) still compiles, but the runtime never calls `isSameExclusiveExecutionContext`. Top-level `await` crashes the 6.4.2 compiler without `MainActor`, since top-level code runs on it; with FeatherOS's runtime it compiles.
- **References:** no `weak` or `unowned` references, so a cycle of strong references must be broken by hand. `unowned(unsafe)` and `Unmanaged` remain.
- **Classes:** a generic method of a class must be `final`.
- **Dynamic casts:** casting a value to a protocol type (`as? any P`, `is any P`) is a compile error. A cast to a class-bound protocol compiles, but it needs the runtime's `swift_conformsToProtocol`, which Embedded Swift does not have, so it does not link. Casts from `Any` or another existential to a concrete type (`as? Int`) and between classes work. Converting an array to an array of existentials (`[Int]` to `[any P]`) is also a dynamic cast.
- **Existentials and generics:** a generic method, or a protocol extension's method, cannot be called through an existential whose concrete type the compiler cannot see. Parameter packs (`repeat each T`) cannot be specialized, so they do not compile either.
- **Key paths:** a literal key path used in place is resolved at compile time. One that reaches a subscript as an unknown value needs `swift_getAtKeyPath`, which does not link.

`Double(String)` calls `fma` on x86-64, where the CPU model may have no fused multiply-add instruction, so the C library provides it; `hello` parses a `Double` and a `Float` to check. Existentials do work: an `any P` of any protocol, `Any` itself, and untyped `throws`, whose error arrives as an `any Error` and is matched with `catch let error as E`. Embedded Swift also has no `CommandLine`, and Swift imports neither variadic C functions (`printf`, `open`, `fcntl`, `ioctl`) nor the `errno` macro; `FeatherSupport` provides `CommandLine`, `errno`, fixed-parameter `open`, `openat`, `fcntl` and `ioctl`, and `String(format:)` (see "Swift programs"). The `v` forms, such as `vsnprintf`, are imported, and take a `va_list` from `withVaList`.

### Full Swift

Programs in full Swift, with the runtime type information Embedded Swift leaves out, are listed in `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_FULL_PROGRAMS` (`src/user/CMakeLists.txt`) like Embedded ones; so far there are `fullhello`, `reflect`, `fulltasks`, `fullexecutors`, `fullregex`, `fullcodable`, `fulljson`, `fulllife`, `fullswiftbench` and, in `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_FULL_CXX_PROGRAMS`, `fullcxxinterop` and `fullhttp`. A full program compiles in files from other programs' directories through `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_SHARED_SOURCES_<name>`, as Embedded ones do. They are compiled without `-enable-experimental-feature Embedded`, against a standard library and runtime built from the `deps/swift` sources by `src/user/swift_runtime/`:
- **The `Swift` module** comes from upstream's whole file list in `stdlib/public/core/CMakeLists.txt` (every `EMBEDDED` and `NORMAL` entry, the SIMD types, but not `ObjectIdentifier+DebugDescription.swift`, which needs the compiler's macro plugins), at `-O`, with `SWIFT_ENABLE_REFLECTION`, `SWIFT_STDLIB_HAS_STDIN` and `SWIFT_STDLIB_HAS_COMMANDLINE`. It takes about 70 seconds per architecture. Unlike Embedded Swift's module it is compiled to an object, `Swift.o`, that every program links whole. Debug builds also build `SwiftOnoneSupport`, the prespecializations that `-Onone` programs import.
- **The runtime** is upstream's C++ (`stdlib/public/runtime`, with `SwiftObject.mm` and `ErrorObject.mm` compiled as C++, the stubs, the command line support, `LLVMSupport`, the demangler from `lib/Demangling`, and the pthreads threading package), compiled by the toolchain's `clang++` at `-O2` with the settings of upstream's freestanding preset (`mixin_stdlib_minimal`): no Objective-C, `dladdr`, `dlsym` or backtraces. Reflection, type printing, standard input, the environment and Unicode data are on. It is compiled as C++20 over FeatherOS's own C++ headers, as the concurrency runtime is, and links `featheros_cxx` for `basic_string<char>`'s members, `to_string` and `thread::hardware_concurrency`. `swift.patch` drops `MetadataLookup.cpp`'s unused `#include <list>`; `Exception.cpp` includes `<exception>`, which it uses only with exceptions on.
- **Section registration:** a constructor in `SwiftRT-ELF-WASM.cpp`, linked into each program as an object, hands the runtime the program's `swift5_*` sections (protocol conformances, type metadata records, field descriptors and the rest) through the linker's `__start_` and `__stop_` symbols, so the static PIE needs no loader for them.
- **swift.patch** gives `os(none)` on x86-64 a `CLongDouble` of `Float80` (otherwise no `long double` shim imports and `Float80` does not compile), takes `arc4random_buf` for random numbers and the hashing seed where the runtime would open `/dev/urandom`, uses `malloc_usable_size` for `_swift_stdlib_malloc_size`, reports no stack bounds from `Pthreads.cpp`, and adds `__featheros__` cases to `CommandLine.cpp`: `CommandLine.arguments` comes from `featheros_argument_values()`, and `CommandLine.executablePath` is `argv[0]`, under `/system/` when it is a bare name.

- **`_Concurrency`** is upstream's full-Swift build: the runtime's C++ with `ExecutorImpl.cpp`, and the module with `DispatchExecutor.swift` and `ExecutorImpl.swift`. Full Swift reaches its executors through an `ExecutorFactory`. `swift_runtime/PlatformExecutorFeatherOS.swift` names upstream's `DispatchMainExecutor` and `DispatchGlobalTaskExecutor`, as Linux does; they call five `swift_dispatch*` functions, which `global_executor.cpp` provides over the same thread pool as Embedded Swift's (see "Swift concurrency"). Programs import the module implicitly, as on other platforms, and link it from an archive, so only programs that use concurrency carry it.
- **`Cxx`**, the C++ overlay, is upstream's build too, unpatched, compiled into the archive `featheros_swift_full_cxx`. Programs in `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_FULL_CXX_PROGRAMS` import C++ headers as `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_CXX_PROGRAMS` do (see "Swift and C++"): the same flags and modules, FeatherOS's C++ headers, and C++ from GCC in `featheros_user_support` or `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_CXX_SOURCES_<name>`, which `ld.lld` links with the rest. `CxxStdlib` is built the same way into `featheros_swift_full_cxx_stdlib`; as the toolchain names `CxxStdlibShim` only for Embedded Swift and its own platforms, full Swift loads that module map with `-fmodule-map-file`. They pass `-disable-autolink-library swiftCxx` and `swiftCxxStdlib`: with C++ interoperability on, the compiler asks to link them, which a Release object, being LLVM bitcode, turns into an ELF dependent library that `ld.lld` must find, while the link names the archive itself.
- **`_RegexParser`, `_StringProcessing` and `RegexBuilder`**, Swift's regular expressions, come from swift-experimental-string-processing at the same tag, which `build.sh` fetches into `deps/swift-experimental-string-processing` (only `Sources/`). They are built as upstream's `stdlib/public/{RegexParser,StringProcessing,RegexBuilder}` build them, with no patches and the flags of the toolchain's own module interfaces, into one archive, `featheros_swift_full_string_processing`, with `_CUnicode`'s two C files (the script tables behind `\p{Script=...}`), which Clang compiles as C. Programs import `_StringProcessing` implicitly, as on other platforms, and `RegexBuilder` explicitly; the modules built in `swift_runtime/` pass `-disable-implicit-string-processing-module-import`. Programs compile with `-enable-bare-slash-regex`, since they are in Swift 5 mode, where `/.../` literals are otherwise off.

Programs, the modules and the runtime are compiled with `-D__featheros__` for Clang, since the shims' inline functions, `_swift_stdlib_malloc_size` among them, are compiled into each program. swiftc imports `SwiftShims` from its resource directory, where the toolchain's unpatched headers would shadow `swift.patch`'s, so full-Swift builds pass `-resource-dir` for `build/<arch>/src/user/swift_runtime/resource`: symbolic links to the toolchain's `lib/swift`, with `shims` from `deps/swift`. Without it, `_swift_stdlib_malloc_size` returns 0 and `ManagedBuffer.capacity` is negative. From the C library the runtime and module use stdio, the `malloc` family with `malloc_usable_size`, pthread mutexes, condition variables and keys, `getline`, `strtold`, `getenv`, `sysconf`, `__cxa_guard_*`, `remainder`, `rint`, the x87 `long double` functions on x86-64 and the 128-bit divisions (`docs/posix.md`).

The toolchain's `ld.lld` links each program (`--gc-sections`): the program's object, the C library's startup, the registration object, `Swift.o` (and `SwiftOnoneSupport.o`), then the runtime and the C library. GCC cannot drive `ld.lld` here, since the cross compilers ignore `-fuse-ld=lld`. Release builds compile the module and programs with `-lto=llvm-full -experimental-hermetic-seal-at-link`: each object is LLVM bitcode, and at link time the compiler sees the program and the whole standard library as one module, keeping only what is used, type records included. Without the seal, the `swift5_*` records reference every type's metadata and witness tables, so `--gc-sections` alone removes almost nothing. The sealed link turns off LLVM's whole-program devirtualization (`-mllvm -wholeprogramdevirt-skip=*`). Its virtual constant propagation replaces a call to an overridden method that returns a constant with a load beside the class's vtable in the metadata global, which is wrong for a generic class: its metadata is copied to the heap while the program runs. `KeyPath.kind` was one such method, so appending key paths failed. The seal's virtual function and witness method elimination also run only at the link: modules and programs compile with `-Xllvm -enable-vfe=false`. swiftc marks a `private` type's vtable and witness tables as used only within its own file, so LLVM's `GlobalDCE`, which runs in each compile too, dropped every entry that file never called. A conformance escapes its file as an existential, however. Every synthesized `Codable` type has `private` `CodingKeys`, whose `stringValue` `FeatherSupport`'s encoder calls through `any CodingKey`, and `fulljson`'s `any SingleValueDecodingContainer` is a `private` struct of `FeatherSupport`'s; both called a null pointer in Release. At the link the whole program is one module, so the same pass removes what nothing calls with every caller in view, and programs are the same size as with it on at compile time. To check a Release-only crash for this, look for null entries in the program's witness tables (`<type><protocol>AAWP` symbols, filled by `R_*_RELATIVE` relocations). `fullhello` stripped, x86-64 / AArch64: 5.4 / 5.0 MB in Debug, 2.7 / 2.4 MB in Release, against 0.9 / 0.8 MB for Embedded `hello` at `-Onone`; with debug information a Debug build is about 20 MB in `build/<arch>/programs`. Such a program uses 600 to 1,300 of a process's 16,384 pages.

A full-Swift program cannot import an Embedded Swift module, so `FeatherSupport` is built a second time, from the same sources, in full mode (`featheros_add_full_swift_support` in `src/user/CMakeLists.txt`, into `build/<arch>/src/user/swift_full/support` and the archive `featheros_swift_full_support`, which every full program's link group has). Two of its files are Embedded only (`#if $Embedded`): `CommandLine`, which full Swift's library has, and the `print` of several `CustomStringConvertible` items, where full Swift's `print` takes `Any`. One is full only: `codable_containers.swift` (see "Codable"). `fulljson`, `fulllife`, `fullswiftbench` and `fullhttp` are `json`, `life`, `swiftbench` and `http` built unchanged in full Swift; their output is the Embedded programs', except that `json -t`'s errors use full Swift's descriptions, which name the type expected. `fulltasks`, `fullexecutors` and `fullcxxinterop` are `tasks.swift`, `executors.swift` (see "Swift concurrency") and `cxxinterop.swift` (see "Swift and C++") built in full Swift: the same checks, with lines starting `fulltasks:`, `fullexecutors:` and `fullcxxinterop:` (the prefix follows `#if $Embedded`), which the shell smoke test expects alongside the Embedded ones. What works and what does not with C++ is the same in both modes. `fullhello` prints its arguments through `CommandLine`, runs the standard library's unspecialized generic code (sorting, a `Dictionary` and a `Set`, a generic stack) on its own types through their metadata, and with `fail` stops in `fatalError`, whose message the runtime prints before the program traps. At `-O`, Swift's `precondition` and the standard library's own checks, such as an array index out of range, trap without a message, as on other platforms; `fatalError` prints in every build.

`reflect` checks the runtime type information Embedded Swift leaves out: casts from `Any` to protocols, protocol compositions and parameterized existentials, through conditional conformances, of arrays element by element and of metatypes; `catch let error as E`; `Mirror` of structs, classes with their superclass, enums, tuples and `CustomReflectable` types, and `dump`; key paths as values (in arrays, written through, appended, through optional chains, as functions, compared and hashed); `weak` and `unowned` references; overridden non-`final` generic methods and `class var`s of generic classes; `required init` through a metatype; and `String(describing:)` and `String(reflecting:)` of any value. Each line of its output starts with `reflect:`; `dump`'s triangles show as `?` on the console, which prints only ASCII.

`fullcodable` checks `Codable` over the standard library's own protocols, with conformances the compiler synthesizes. Its `Milestone` (a raw-value enum, an enum with associated values and an optional payload, an optional, a `[String: UInt8]`) encodes to the same JSON text as in Embedded `hello`. Decoding errors print upstream's descriptions with their paths (`steps[0].boot.seconds`) and the type expected, for `dataCorrupted`, `keyNotFound`, `typeMismatch`, `valueNotFound` and an integer out of range. Then what Embedded Swift does in a form of its own or not at all: dictionary keys through `CodingKeyRepresentable` (an enum's, `Int`'s as strings, and `Double`'s as alternating keys and values), which `Dictionary` finds with a protocol cast; a `[any Encodable]` filtered from `[Any]` by a cast and encoded with each existential opened; a subclass that encodes its superclass through `superEncoder` and decodes it through `superDecoder`; a generic `Pair<Int128>` pretty-printed with sorted keys; and a `UInt128` beyond 64 bits, whose `invalidValue` description shows the value and its type. Each line of its output starts with `fullcodable:`.

`fullregex` checks upstream's `Regex`, which Embedded Swift cannot have: literals with named, numbered and optional captures, typed as tuples by the compiler; `firstMatch`, `wholeMatch`, `prefixMatch`, `matches`, `ranges`, `contains`, `starts(with:)`, `trimmingPrefix`, `split` and `replacing`, also with a closure; back-references, lookahead, reluctant and greedy repetition; `ignoresCase`, `anchorsMatchLineEndings` and Unicode-scalar semantics against the default grapheme clusters; `\p{Script=Greek}`; patterns made from a `String` while the program runs, as `AnyRegexOutput` with captures by name and number, or with an `Output` type checked at run time, and an invalid one rejected; and a `RegexBuilder` regex with `ChoiceOf`, `Optionally`, an anchor and a `TryCapture` transformed to `Int` and read through a `Reference`. Swift has no `~=` for regexes, so `case /.../:` does not compile. Each line of its output starts with `fullregex:`.

### Embedded or full Swift

Both modes build the same sources; `#if $Embedded` tells them apart where they differ. Embedded Swift is the default: a Release program is 0.15 to 0.4 MB stripped (`life`, `http`, `swiftbench`, `json`), with no runtime to start, and it has `FeatherSupport`, a `Codable` of its own, concurrency and C++ interoperability. Choose full Swift when a program needs what Embedded Swift leaves out (see "What Embedded Swift leaves out"): casts to protocols, `Mirror` and `dump`, key paths as values, `weak` and `unowned`, upstream's `Regex`, `Codable` with existentials or `CodingKeyRepresentable` found by a cast, generic methods called through existentials, parameter packs. It costs size: every full program carries the standard library and the runtime, 2.4 to 3.1 MB stripped in Release and 5.0 to 5.8 MB in Debug (x86-64 is the larger), against 0.14 to 0.41 MB and 0.29 to 0.83 MB for the same programs in Embedded Swift. Speed is close to Embedded Swift's, faster on some workloads and slower on others (`swiftbench` below), and memory is no concern: a full program uses 600 to 1,300 of a process's 16,384 pages.

### Swift against C++

`swiftbench [-n runs | -t seconds] [workload...]` (`src/user/swiftbench/`) and `cppbench` (`src/user/cppbench.cpp`) run the same five workloads, written step for step alike in each language:
- `life`: a 64x64 grid with a random quarter of its cells alive, for 20 generations. Swift uses `life`'s own `Grid`, and the C++ version is a port of it.
- `sieve`: the primes below 1 000 000, by the sieve of Eratosthenes.
- `quicksort`: 20 000 random 32-bit numbers, sorted by the same quicksort (median of three, Hoare partition) and a final insertion sort.
- `sort`: the same numbers, sorted by each language's library: Swift's `sort()`, and the C library's `qsort`.
- `json`: a generated document of 500 objects (87 373 bytes, with escapes, a `\u` escape and raw UTF-8), parsed and then walked. Swift uses `json`'s own parser, and the C++ version is a port of it.

A Swift program compiles in files from another program's directory through `FEATHEROS_SWIFT_SHARED_SOURCES_<name>` in `src/user/CMakeLists.txt`, so `swiftbench` gets `life/grid.swift`; `fullswiftbench` is the same sources built in full Swift (see "Full Swift"); `json`'s parser it takes from `FeatherSupport`. Neither program has the other language's library: the C++ port uses FeatherOS's `std::vector` (see "C++ Standard Library"). Both programs draw their numbers from SplitMix64 with the same seeds. Each workload prints a check value: the population, the prime count, a sum weighted by position of the sorted numbers, or the document's size plus its count of values and of string, key and number bytes. Equal check values show that both programs did the same work. Each workload runs for `-t` seconds (3 by default) or `-n` times, timed with `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, whose 10 ms ticks are why the runs last seconds. It then prints `life: check 691, 2 runs in 10 ms, 5000 us each`.

The benchmark builds in Release, so the programs, the C library and its allocator, which both languages call, are optimized: C++ at `-O2` and Swift at `-O`, full Swift sealed (see "Full Swift"). The shell smoke test runs each program with `-n` and checks the check values and the option errors.

`./build.sh --arch <arch> benchmark` builds in Release, boots QEMU, and runs `cppbench`, `swiftbench` and `fullswiftbench` in turn, two rounds of 1 s a workload (`BENCHMARK_ROUNDS`, `BENCHMARK_SECONDS`), which takes under a minute per architecture after a Release build. For each workload it prints the fastest time per run of any round and the ratio of each Swift time to C++'s, then each program's loaded bytes (its `PT_LOAD` segments). It fails if a check value differs, and saves the session to `build/<arch>-release/benchmark-output.txt`. Run the two architectures one after the other: two `build.sh` processes started together race for `deps/`'s git configuration. QEMU emulates both CPUs, so only the ratios mean anything, and those still move by about 10 % between runs; three rounds of 3 s gave the same ratios within that. The table below shows results on an Apple M3 Max with QEMU 11.1.2, Swift 6.4.2 and GCC 14.1 (M27 Part 6), Swift's times with their ratio to C++'s:

| Workload | x86-64 C++ | x86-64 Embedded | x86-64 full | AArch64 C++ | AArch64 Embedded | AArch64 full |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `life` | 922 µs | 4081 µs (4.43×) | 3968 µs (4.30×) | 594 µs | 2688 µs (4.53×) | 2421 µs (4.08×) |
| `sieve` | 9009 µs | 17 857 µs (1.98×) | 16 666 µs (1.85×) | 9615 µs | 16 557 µs (1.72×) | 14 285 µs (1.49×) |
| `quicksort` | 1512 µs | 2710 µs (1.79×) | 3533 µs (2.34×) | 1254 µs | 2053 µs (1.64×) | 2409 µs (1.92×) |
| `sort` | 14 705 µs | 9433 µs (0.64×) | 3703 µs (0.25×) | 9708 µs | 6802 µs (0.70×) | 2673 µs (0.28×) |
| `json` | 6756 µs | 12 948 µs (1.92×) | 14 925 µs (2.21×) | 5988 µs | 10 869 µs (1.82×) | 11 904 µs (1.99×) |
| Code bytes | 108 110 | 201 117 | 2 007 976 | 98 104 | 160 584 | 1 766 292 |
| Data bytes | 23 404 | 28 452 | 867 828 | 21 680 | 25 752 | 830 988 |

The table predates `cppbench`'s move to `std::vector` (M26 Part 3 step 2), which made its `json` 15 to 19 % slower (8196 µs on x86-64, 7518 µs on AArch64), Embedded Swift's `json` ratio about 1.55 on both, and its code about 4 % smaller.

Here is what the numbers say:
- Safe Swift takes 1.6 to 2.0 times as long as C++ on `sieve`, `quicksort` and `json`, and 4.4 to 4.5 times as long on `life`.
- On the three array workloads, the checks make most of the gap. Each array index is checked against the bounds, and each addition is checked for overflow; `life`'s inner loop does eight of each per cell. A trial build of `swiftbench` at `-Ounchecked` (x86-64, two rounds of 2 s, M21) brought the ratios down to 1.87 times on `life`, 1.16 on `sieve` and 1.05 on `quicksort`.
- `json` stayed at 1.79 times without the checks, so its cost is elsewhere. It most likely comes from the parser's values: an `indirect enum` boxes every value on the heap, with reference counting, while the C++ port keeps its values in place in its vectors.
- `sort` favours Swift: its standard library's sort is generic code specialized for `UInt32` and compiled with the program, against the C library's `qsort`, a heap sort that calls a comparison function for each step. Full Swift's `sort` is another 2.5 times faster than Embedded Swift's, which was not investigated.
- Full Swift runs the same source 3 to 13 % faster than Embedded Swift on `life` and `sieve`, and 9 to 31 % slower on `quicksort` and `json`.
- Embedded Swift's code is 1.6 to 1.9 times the size of C++'s: the parts of the standard library and runtime that the program compiles in, such as arrays, dictionaries, hashing, string interpolation and its sort. Full Swift's code is about 18 times C++'s, with 0.8 MB of data: the standard library and runtime with the type metadata and records that the seal keeps.

Interactive runs accept keys from the QEMU window on both architectures. x86-64 uses PS/2; AArch64 uses virtio-input on QEMU `virt`. The launching terminal's serial console also remains available. Program output appears on both serial and the framebuffer console.

Input reaches the console's terminal as one byte stream (`src/kernel/input/input.cpp`), in the form a VT100 terminal sends it. The terminal (`src/kernel/terminal/terminal.cpp`) holds the input queue, the mode, the size and the foreground group, and applies the line discipline: echo, canonical line editing and Ctrl+C. It draws through a device, which for the console is serial plus `term.cpp`. Serial bytes arrive as typed, with `\r\n` counted once. The keyboard drivers only turn scancodes into key codes (PS/2 set 1, including its `E0` keys, virtio-input codes, which match for the main keys, or, for a USB keyboard, the HID usages through `Usb::Hid::UsageToKeyCode`; all of them can be present at once, `ps2=off` leaves the PS/2 or virtio one out, and the Caps and Num Lock LEDs follow the global state on USB keyboards only). `src/kernel/input/keyboard.cpp` then maps them for both architectures, with the US layout, Shift, Caps Lock and Ctrl (Caps Lock and Num Lock are shared by every keyboard, and the keypad sends its digits with Num Lock on and the keys printed below them otherwise; Num Lock starts off):

| Key | Bytes |
| --- | --- |
| Enter, Backspace, Tab, Escape | `\r`, `0x7F`, `\t`, `ESC` |
| Ctrl+A to Ctrl+Z, Ctrl+[ \ ] | 1 to 26, 27 to 29 |
| Up, Down, Right, Left | `ESC [ A`, `B`, `C`, `D` |
| Home, End | `ESC [ H`, `ESC [ F` |
| Insert, Delete, Page Up, Page Down | `ESC [ 2 ~`, `3 ~`, `5 ~`, `6 ~` |
| Shift+Tab | `ESC [ Z` |

Ctrl+C (byte 3) goes to the console's interrupt handler first, in order with the bytes around it, and is queued only if the handler declines it. The console has terminal settings (`TerminalMode`, behind `tcgetattr` and `tcsetattr`), which start as canonical input with echo, signals, and Enter as `\n`:
- **Canonical:** `ReadLine` and console reads edit a line: the prompt and the typed line appear inline in the scrolling text with a block cursor, like a normal terminal. Printable ASCII is inserted, Backspace erases (also across a wrapped line), and Enter ends the line. Escape sequences are skipped, so the cursor keys do nothing there; programs that want them, like the shell, use raw input. Input is echoed to serial and the screen unless echo is off.
- **Raw** (canonical off): a read returns bytes as they arrive, without editing, once `minimum` bytes are there. `timeout`, in tenths of a second, limits the wait for the first byte when `minimum` is 0, and the wait between bytes otherwise; with both 0 a read returns at once. Echo, when on, prints what arrives.
- **Signals:** with them off, Ctrl+C arrives as byte 3 instead of sending `SIGINT`.

Only the foreground group may change the settings. The process that changed them owns them: when it exits, even after a fault or `SIGKILL`, the console returns to the defaults and the terminal to the main screen and normal video with the cursor shown, so a crashed full-screen program cannot leave the shell in raw mode.

The kernel runs four virtual consoles, `/dev/tty1` to `/dev/tty4` (`src/kernel/console.cpp`), each a `Terminals::Terminal` with its own `Term` screen: text, cursor, alternate screen and status bar. Alt+F1 to Alt+F4 (either Alt, with or without Ctrl; `Input::SubmitKey`) choose which one is drawn and gets the keyboard. A hidden console keeps updating its text without drawing, and is repainted whole when shown again. Serial input and output, kernel messages and init's own descriptors stay with the first console. Each console runs its own shell in its own session, from init's `console` services, so jobs, Ctrl+C and the foreground program shown in the status bar are per console.

The framebuffer's top row is a status bar. Its left side names the shown console and its foreground program, as `tty1  shell` (`kernel` before the shell starts). Its right side shows busy CPUs over online CPUs, a network dot, then the UTC date and time as `Mon Oct 19 9:36:05 PM`. The dot is green while the link is up and an address has a way off the local network (IPv4 with a gateway, or a global IPv6 address with a router; `Network::HasRoute`), and red otherwise, including without a network device. Nothing probes whether that path reaches the internet. The status text marks the dot with byte 1 (green) or 2 (red), which `Term` draws as a filled circle. A CPU counts as busy when the scheduler's timer ticks found it running a task other than its idle task for at least half of the last second. A high-priority kernel task redraws the bar once a second and when the foreground program changes. Its clock is the kernel's wall clock, and setting the clock redraws the bar at once.

Below the bar, the console understands a VT100 subset, so programs can draw full screens (`src/kernel/framebuffer/term.cpp`). Row 1 of `CSI row;column H` is the first row below the bar, and moves stop at the screen's edges.

| Sequence | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| `CSI n A`, `B`, `C`, `D` | Cursor up, down, right, left by `n` (default 1) |
| `CSI n G`, `CSI n d` | Cursor to column `n`, to row `n` |
| `CSI row;column H` or `f` | Cursor to a position (default 1;1) |
| `CSI n J` | Erase below the cursor (0), above it (1), or the whole text area (2) |
| `CSI n K` | Erase to the end of the line (0), to its start (1), or the whole line (2) |
| `CSI 7 m`, `CSI 27 m`, `CSI 0 m` | Reverse video on, off, off |
| `CSI s`, `CSI u`, `ESC 7`, `ESC 8` | Save and restore the cursor |
| `CSI ?25 l`, `CSI ?25 h` | Hide and show the cursor |
| `CSI ?1049 h`, `CSI ?1049 l` | Switch to a blank alternate screen, and back to the main screen as it was, with its cursor |
| `\r`, `\b` | Column 0, one column left |

Other sequences are read and ignored. Serial output carries the same bytes, so a terminal emulator on the serial port follows them too. `ioctl(TIOCGWINSZ)` reports the text area's size.

`CALC_TEST=ON` checks a calculator expression, an unknown command, and a blank line; `SHELL_TEST=ON` (the part `dmesg.py` checks `dmesg | head -n 2`, `cat /proc/kmsg | head -n 1`, `CPU 1 online` and the `PCI:` lines in `/proc/kmsg` and `dmesg -h`) checks init's startup line and its restart of the shell after `exit`, `filetest`, `fbtest` (and `fbtest pty` under `script -c`), `cd` and `pwd` from `/home` with relative paths, `/`, and `/system`, `ls /dev`, a file written to `/tmp`, `/proc/self/status` and `/proc/meminfo`, the FAT16 partition mounted read-only at `/mnt` (a long name read, writes refused with `EROFS`, `ls /mnt` failing once it is unmounted), the ext2 partition `vda6` at `/mnt` (mounting it as ext4 failing, `-r` showing `ro` in `/proc/mounts` and refusing a `write`, then `rw`, `ls` with the 73-character name whole, `ls -l` with modes, owners `1000` and group `100`, sizes, times and link targets, `cat` of the long-named file and through the fast and slow links, then `mkdir`, `write`, `mv`, `ln`, `ln -s`, `cat` through the new link, `ls -l` with two links, `rmdir .` failing with `Invalid argument`, a non-empty `rmdir` with `Directory not empty`, `mv` of `..` with `Device or resource busy`, then everything removed again and unmounted, after which the harness requires `e2fsck -fn` on the partition to exit 0), and an 80-character name in `/tmp`, the ext4 root's `/samples` (`mount -t ext2 vda1` failing, `ls -l` with the link target, `cat` of a file and through the link, `readlink`, `wc -c` of the 2 MiB extent-mapped file, the same writes and removals as on ext2, then `/shell.txt`) and the ext3 partition (`rw` in `/proc/mounts`, `cat` through its link, `wc -c` of `big.txt`, writes), after which the harness requires ext3 and ext2 unchanged and `e2fsck -fn` clean, and the root, once `sync` before the last part has committed it, the same on a copy replayed with `e2fsck -fy`, symbolic links in `/tmp` (`ln -s`, `cat` and `readlink` through one, `readlink -f`, `ls -l`, a loop that fails with `Too many levels of symbolic links`, a hard link refused, and `rm` removing the link but not its target), `mount` before and after `umount /tmp` (when `ls /tmp` fails) and after mounting it again with a file written before still there, and `umount /` and `umount /nonexistent` and an unknown type failing, `fptest 8`, `libctest` with a quoted argument and a console read, `cxxtest`, programs copied to `/bin` and `/tmp` with `cp` and run by `PATH`, by path and relative to the working directory (with `aslr` and `memtest exhaust` checking ASLR and the page limit for them, and `/proc/self/status` their name), a missing file, a text file and a directory run as commands, `env` with the variables from init, `--help`/`-h`, Ctrl+C on a CPU-bound `primes`, a `sleep`, `upper` and `calc` waiting for input, and the shell prompt, Ctrl+Z on a `sleep` that `jobs` lists as stopped, `bg` continues for the time it had left and `fg` waits for, on a pipeline that `fg 1` brings back, on `vi` (whose file is then saved and checked with `upper`), on `top` (then quit with `q`) and at a nested shell's prompt, each brought back with `fg`, a background `upper` that stops on its read and reads once `fg` gives it the console, a stopped background job that `kill %1` ends, plus `echo` arguments, foreground input in `upper`, the interactive `calc> ` prompt and its errors, `primes 4 100000` and `primes 16 20000` (the counts and one report per worker, through pipes), a nonzero exit status, the `uptime` format, and that `date` is within two minutes of the host clock while the session ran. `INPUT_TEST=ON` runs the keyboard test through QEMU's `sendkey`, which exercises PS/2 on x86-64 and virtio-input on AArch64: Ctrl+C at the prompt, typing with Backspace and Enter, keys typed after Alt+F2 that must not reach the first console's shell before Alt+F1, Left and Up editing a shell line, and then special keys in `keys` (`up`, `home`, `delete`, `esc`, `ctrl-a`, `shift-tab`). It then switches consoles, starting `sleep 60` on the second, `top` on the third and `vi` on the fourth, stops the second one's `sleep` with Ctrl+Z, continues it with `fg`, interrupts it with Ctrl+C, and returns to the first. `INPUT_TEST` also builds the kernel with a hook (`KernelTest::StartConsoleReports`) that writes `KERN/I: Consoles: tty2 shown; shell, sleep, shell, shell` to serial whenever the shown console or a console's foreground program changes, and each step waits for its report. The harness removes those lines before matching the shell's output, which they may split. The shell test also edits lines over serial (Home, Right, Up, Ctrl+U, Ctrl+W, Delete, a lone Escape, `history`), runs `clear`, edits a file in `vi` and checks it with `upper`, quits an interactive `top` with `q`, and runs `keys` with every special key and Ctrl+C as a byte. Last before Ctrl+C, it starts `sleep 61 &` and runs `script /tmp/session.txt`; inside, it edits a line, runs and brings back a job with `&`, `jobs` and `fg`, interrupts a `sleep`, stops one with Ctrl+Z and brings it back with `fg`, edits a file in `vi` and stops `vi` and `top` with Ctrl+Z on the way, sets the size with `stty rows 20 cols 50`, checks it with `stty size` there and in `script -c "stty size"`, then starts a nested `script` and resizes the outer pty from inside it with `stty -F /dev/pts/0`, which the nested `stty size` must see. After both shells exit, the console shell must still list its own `sleep 61 &` as job 1, and `grep` must find the edited line and the start and end lines in the file. `USB_KEYS_TEST=ON` (`--expect-usb-keys`, `usb_keys.py`, matrix cases 57 and 58) boots with `qemu-xhci` and one `usb-kbd` as the only keyboard (`-machine pc,i8042=off` on x86-64, no `virtio-keyboard-device` on AArch64, so the kernel logs `Keyboard device unavailable`) and types through QMP `send-key`: `echo usb works | upper`, Caps Lock around two letters (`ABcd`), Ctrl+C discarding a line, and Left and Up editing and recalling one; it also attaches a `usb-mouse`, runs `mousetest 9` and sends QMP `input-send-event` events without a `device` (they go to the one mouse the machine has): `rel` x 10 and y -5 (`mouse: rel x 10`, `mouse: rel y -5`, `mouse: syn`), a `wheel-up` button (`mouse: rel wheel 1`) and the left button down and up (`mouse: key 272 1`, `272 0`). `USB_FALLBACK_TEST=ON` (`--expect-usb-fallback`, matrix cases 59 and 60) repeats it with everything the defaults avoid: `qemu-xhci,msi=off,msix=off` (INTx on AArch64, polling on x86-64, checked in the controller line), a full-speed `usb-kbd,usb_version=1`, and `usbhid=report` (added by `build.sh` for this toggle, `KERNEL_USBHID=report` anywhere else) so the keyboard goes through the report parser; its mouse is full-speed too and runs the same steps. `KERNEL_PS2=off` adds `ps2=off`: the keyboard that is not on USB (PS/2 on x86-64, virtio-input on AArch64) stays uninitialized (`BootOptions::ParsePs2`, host-tested; the last `ps2=` wins, anything but `off` counts as absent). `USB_TEST=ON` (`--expect-usb`, `tools/smoke/cases/usb.py`, matrix cases 55 and 56) attaches `qemu-xhci` with a `usb-kbd`, a `usb-mouse` and a second `usb-kbd,usb_version=1` and checks the boot log: the `USB: xHCI ... MSI-X` line on both targets, no `polling`, and one `USB 1-N: <speed>-speed 0627:0001 "<product>" <driver>` line per device (a high-speed keyboard and mouse and a full-speed keyboard, `usb_version=1`; QEMU puts them on its USB 2 ports 5, 6 and 7), each logged once its device, configuration and product-string descriptors were read. The shell then runs `lsusb` (the three devices as `1-5 dev 2 high 0627:0001 class 03/01/01 hid-keyboard`, `hid-mouse` for the mouse, numbers 2, 3 and 4), and the harness hot-plugs a `usb-kbd` into QEMU's USB 2 port 4 (root port 8) through QMP `device_add` and pulls it with `device_del`, three times, each time requiring its `USB 1-8: high-speed ... hid-keyboard` line, typing `echo k2 N | upper` on it (QEMU gives key events to the keyboard registered last; the machine has no i8042 or virtio keyboard) and then requiring `USB 1-8: disconnected`; a second `lsusb` must list exactly what the first did (the number 5 was free again each time). `KERNEL_USB=off|poll` adds `usb=off` or `usb=poll` to the kernel command line in any run (like `KERNEL_ROOT`, not a test hook): `usb=off` leaves the controller alone and prints no `USB` line, `usb=poll` takes controller events from the timer instead of an interrupt (`USB: interrupts by polling`); the last `usb=` wins and any other value counts as absent (`BootOptions::ParseUsb`, host-tested). Without MSI-X and MSI the controller uses INTx on AArch64 and polling on x86-64, which `qemu-xhci,msi=off,msix=off` shows. `PCI_TEST=ON` (`--expect-pci`, `tools/smoke/cases/pci.py`, matrix cases 53 and 54) attaches QEMU's `edu` test device (`-device edu`; on AArch64 `edu,dma_mask=0xffffffff`, because `edu` clamps DMA addresses to 28 bits and virt's RAM starts at 1 GiB) and runs `KernelTest::RunPciTests` once the kernel is up: the boot log must list a `PCI:` line with `1234:11e8 class 00ff00`, `edu` must answer its identification and liveness registers, on AArch64 an INTx delivered through the `interrupt-map` route (`KERN/PCI: INTx delivered`; the handler must quiet the device, since a level line fires again at once), two MSIs raised through `edu`'s interrupt register (`KERN/PCI: MSI delivered with data 50` on x86-64, the vector, and `80` on AArch64, the GICv2m SPI; the second one only arrives if the first was ended correctly), a DMA round trip through `edu`'s buffer with two `Dma::Allocate` buffers below `edu`'s limit (256 MiB on x86-64, 4 GiB on AArch64), and a `Dma::Allocate` below the first frame that must fail. `edu` has no MSI-X, which the host tests `MSI-X: ...` in `tests/unit/kernel/pci/msi_tests.cpp` cover instead. Pass each with `./build.sh test --arch x86_64` or `--arch aarch64`. `X86_UEFI=ON` (any x86-64 smoke mode, e.g. `USB_TEST=ON X86_UEFI=ON ./build.sh --arch x86_64 test`) boots under OVMF instead of SeaBIOS: `build.sh` takes `QEMU_OVMF` or the first of Homebrew's `edk2-x86_64-code.fd`, `/usr/share/OVMF/OVMF_CODE(_4M).fd`, `/usr/share/edk2/ovmf/OVMF_CODE.fd` and `/usr/share/qemu/OVMF.fd`, passes it as `--firmware`, and `tools/smoke/qemu.py` attaches it as a read-only pflash (`-bios` does not accept the 3.6 MiB code image); without firmware the run prints `SKIP: no x86-64 UEFI firmware` and exits 0, which `tools/matrix.py` shows as `skip` for case 63 `x86_64_usb_uefi` (`USB_TEST` under OVMF). On x86-64 the kernel sets up the i8042 after the USB handoff (`arch/x86_64/i8042.cpp`, `keyboard.cpp`): flush the output buffer (at most 64 bytes), read the configuration byte (`0x20`), write it back (`0x60`) with IRQ 1 (bit 0) and translation (bit 6) on and the keyboard clock (bit 4) enabled, then send `0xAE`; a status port reading `0xFF` means no controller. The log has `ACPI: FADT 8042 flag set|clear|unknown` (the FADT's `IAPC_BOOT_ARCH` bit 1, read from FADT revision 3 on and never trusted) and `Keyboard: i8042 config 0xNN, was 0xNN, flushed N bytes` or `Keyboard: no i8042 controller`. `NODISK_TEST=ON` (`--expect-nodisk`, `tools/smoke/cases/no_disk.py`, matrix cases 64 and 65) leaves QEMU's virtio-blk device out (`Case.has_disk`) and expects the boot to log `Storage: no disk; running from the initramfs`, `VFS: / is tmpfs root, read-write` and `VFS: /etc is the initramfs's RAM copy: there is no disk`, no panic, `Init: starting /system/init from the initramfs`; the shell then starts in `/home`, runs `echo`, writes, reads and lists a file under `/home`, `mount` shows `root / tmpfs rw` and `etc /etc tmpfs rw`, `dmesg | head -n 1` prints the ring's first line (`Limine transferred control to the kernel`) and `grep -c "Storage: no disk" /proc/kmsg` finds one, and `sync` and `shutdown` end with `Shutdown: no disk to flush` and `Powering off` and QEMU exiting by itself. `USB_STORAGE_TEST=ON` (`--expect-usb-storage`, `tools/smoke/cases/usb_storage.py`, matrix cases 66 and 67; the kernel hook `test=usb-storage`, `KernelTest::StartUsbStorageTests`, chosen by `build.sh`) attaches four QEMU `usb-storage,removable=on` drives on the `qemu-xhci`, on root ports 1 to 4 so the storage worker probes them in that order and they become `sda` to `sdd`. The case builds their images per run in `build/<arch>/usb-disks/` with `tools/create_usb_disks.py` (mtools and e2fsprogs through `create_test_disk`; also runnable alone, `python3 tools/create_usb_disks.py <folder>`): `usb-fat16.img` is `sda`, 64 MiB with an MBR, `sda1` FAT16 of 32 MiB (type 0x06; `USBFILE.TXT` and the 1 MiB `BIG.BIN`) and `sda2` ext2 of 16 MiB (0x83), with the rest unused; `usb-floppy.img` is `sdb`, FAT16 on the whole 32 MiB (a superfloppy); `usb-protected.img`, a copy of `usb-fat16.img` that QEMU opens with `readonly=on` (the drive reports write protect), is `sdc`; `usb-dirty-copy.img`, a copy of `usb-dirty.img` (a 16 MiB superfloppy whose FAT[1] bit 15, the clean-shutdown bit, is cleared in every FAT copy), is `sdd`. The boot log must show the four `USB 1-N: super-speed 46f4:0001 "QEMU USB HARDDRIVE" usb-storage` lines, the `Storage:` probe line of each (`sdc` ending `removable, write-protected`) and the worker's volume lines (`sda1`, `sda2`, `sdb`, `sdc1`, `sdc2`, `sdd`). The hook task waits for the probed disk (`Storage::Acquire("sda")`) and its volumes and prints `KERN/USBSTORAGE:` lines: `/proc/partitions`, `sda1` and `sda2` mounted, read and unmounted, then the disk's name and sector count, a pattern written to and read back from the last sector, a read and a write past the end refused, 128 KiB written and read back in the drive's unused tail (several commands), the disk's counters and `tests passed`; no `reset recovery` line may appear. The shell then runs (typed after `tests passed`): `cat /proc/partitions` (every disk and volume line, `sdc` with `removable ro`); on `sda1` mounted at `/tmp/usb` a small file, a long VFAT name, a `cp` of the 1 MiB `BIG.BIN`, `mkdir`, `mv`, `rm`, then `umount`, a second `mount` and reads of what was written, `sync` and `umount`; a file written to the ext2 `sda2`; the superfloppy `sdb` written; `sdc1`, which logs `VFS: sdc1 is write-protected, mounted read-only`, shows `ro` in `/proc/mounts`, reads, refuses `write` with `Read-only file system` and refuses `mount -o remount,rw`; `sdd`, which logs `VFS: sdd was not cleanly unmounted (FAT16 dirty bits); mounted read-only, run fsck.fat` and behaves the same; then `sda1` and `sda2` mounted at `/tmp/usb` and `/tmp/usb2` with `write /tmp/usb/before-eject.txt` and `sleep 2 < /tmp/usb/small.txt &` holding a file of `sda1` open: `eject sda` gives `eject: sda: Device or resource busy` and both mounts stay (`grep sda /proc/mounts` lists both once), `wait` ends the sleep, `cd /tmp/usb2` makes `eject sda` busy again (a working directory), and after `cd /home` `eject sda` prints nothing and logs `Storage: sda: ejected, safe to unplug` once (`eject vda` gives `eject: vda: Operation not supported`, `eject sdz` `eject: sdz: no such disk`, `mount -t fat16 sda1 /tmp/usb` fails with `No such device or address`, `cat /proc/partitions` shows `sda` ending `rw ejected`, a second `eject sda` prints nothing, and `grep sda /proc/mounts` lists nothing new); and last `sdb` mounted again and `LAST.TXT` written right before `shutdown` with the volume still mounted (`Shutdown: disk flushed`, QEMU exiting by itself, no `Shutdown:` warning). Afterwards the harness checks the images with the helpers of `tools/smoke/disk_checks.py` (shared with `storage.py`, `power.py` and `root.py`): the FAT copies agree on `sda1` and `sdb`, mtools reads every file byte for byte (the copy equals the original, the removed ones are gone, `sda1` holds the `before-eject.txt` written just before the eject and `sdb` the `LAST.TXT` written before shutdown), FAT[1] still reads `0xFFFF`, `fsck.fat -n` passes on `sda1` and `sdb` when it is installed (the pass message says when it is not and the check is skipped), `sda2` is marked clean and passes `e2fsck -fn` with `debugfs` reading the written file, and the images of `sdc` and `sdd` are byte for byte what the harness wrote (nothing may write them, and the guest never sets FAT[1]'s bits). A removable disk's FAT16 volume with FAT[1] bit 15 or 14 clear mounts read-only, `remount,rw` gives `EROFS`, and the kernel never writes these bits in Part 2; the same volume on `vda` is unchanged (`Fat::StatusBitsClean` in the core, the decision in `CoreDriver::MediaAllowsWrites`, which also makes a write-protected disk's volumes mount read-only). `USB_STORAGE_HOTPLUG_TEST=ON` (`--expect-usb-storage-hotplug`, `tools/smoke/cases/usb_storage_hotplug.py`, matrix cases 68 and 69; no kernel hook) boots with the xHCI controller and no USB drive, and the harness plugs and pulls QEMU `usb-storage,removable=on` drives through QMP (`blockdev-add` under a fresh node name each time, `device_add`, `device_del`, waiting for the `DEVICE_DELETED` event, `blockdev-del`; the images are copies of `usb-fat16.img` in `build/<arch>/usb-hotplug-disks/`) while it types shell lines, each held until a counted log line or an `echo` marker appeared. Rounds 1 to 3 mount `sda1`, write, read back, `umount` and unplug, and `lsusb` and `/proc/partitions` must read as before the first plug. Round 4 pulls the drive 1 s after three `cp` of `/samples/extents.bin` started: `Storage: sda: gone`, `cp` ending with `Input/output error`, `cat` of a file on the mount giving `Input/output error`, `sda1 /tmp/usb fat16 ro 0 0` in `/proc/mounts`, `sync` printing `sync: Input/output error`, `umount` succeeding, the dead disk gone from `/proc/partitions`, and a replug that is `sda` again with the earlier file. Round 5 unplugs a mounted `sda`, plugs a second drive before the `umount` (`sdb`), unmounts the dead mount and plugs a third (`sda` again). Round 6 pulls a drive right after its `USB 1-1: super-speed ...` line (the probe or the volume scan is running). The log may hold `reset recovery after ... cancelled` warnings (a command was in flight) and no other warning; the number of enumerated drives must equal the disconnects. After shutdown mtools reads each image's files and `fsck.fat -n` passes on the first one when installed. A kernel regression shows as a `FAIL: round N ...` line naming the first step that went wrong, or as the run stopping at the time limit on the step that never came. `USB_STORAGE_LARGE_TEST=ON` (`--expect-usb-storage-large`, `tools/smoke/cases/usb_storage_large.py`, matrix cases 70 and 71; no kernel hook) boots with the xHCI controller and three QEMU `usb-storage,removable=on` drives built per run in `build/<arch>/usb-large-disks/` by `tools/create_usb_disks.py` (`python3 tools/create_usb_disks.py <folder> --large` makes them alone; `create_test_disk.write_gpt` takes a sector size and a disk size): `sda` on root port 1 is `usb-large.img`, a sparse 3 TiB drive (`BuildLarge`) with a GPT and one FAT16 partition of 32 MiB at 2.5 TiB (LBA 5368709120, built in a small file and copied into place; `LARGE.TXT`), so the probe needs READ CAPACITY(16) and the volume READ(16) and WRITE(16); `sdb` on root port 2 is `usb-4k.img` (`BuildFourK`), 64 MiB with `logical_block_size=4096,physical_block_size=4096` and a GPT in 4096-byte LBAs whose partitions are entries 5 and 6 (`sdb5` an ext4 made with `-b 4096`, `FOURK.TXT`, and `sdb6` a FAT16 volume the core refuses, because it needs 512-byte sectors: `Storage: sdb6: no supported filesystem`; a reader that took the sectors for 512 bytes would miss the entries, which is what makes the case catch it); `sdc` is `usb-hub.img` (the `usb-fat16.img` layout) behind QEMU's `usb-hub` on its USB 2 port 3, which is root port 7, so the hub is `USB 1-7: full-speed 0409:55aa "QEMU USB Hub" hub` and the drive `USB 1-7.1: full-speed` (QEMU's hub is full speed). The case fails when the boot log shows a speed, capacity (`3221225472 KiB`, `65536 KiB`) or sector size (`512-byte`, `4096-byte`) other than the one asked for, which an old QEMU that ignores a property would give. The shell mounts `sda1` (reads `LARGE.TXT`, writes `w.txt`, `sync`, `umount`, mounts again and reads), `sdb5` (reads, writes, copies the 2 MiB `/samples/extents.bin`, `sync`, `umount`), tries `mount -t fat16 sdb6` and mounts `sdc1` (reads `USBFILE.TXT`, writes `hub.txt`, copies the 1 MiB `BIG.BIN`); then `shutdown`. The harness checks: mtools at the 2.5 TiB offset reads `LARGE.TXT` and `w.txt` and the FAT copies agree, nothing was written below the partition and the image is still sparse (a READ(10) or WRITE(10) past 2^32 sectors would wrap its LBA into the low area), `sdb5` is clean and passes `e2fsck -fn` with `debugfs` reading the three files and `sdb6` is byte for byte unchanged, and mtools reads the files and the copy of `sdc1`. The case is skipped (`SKIP:` and exit 0, `SkipCase` in `tools/smoke/cases/base.py`) when the host cannot make a sparse 3 TiB file (the image is under 128 MiB on disk). `USB_STICK_TEST=ON X86_UEFI=ON` (`--expect-usb-stick`, `tools/smoke/cases/usb_stick.py`, matrix case 72 `x86_64_usb_stick_uefi`, x86-64 only, skipped without edk2 firmware) boots the image `tools/make_usb_stick.sh` builds (a copy per run beside the ISO, via `Case.BootMedia()`, which makes `qemu.py` skip `-cdrom`) from `usb-storage,bootindex=0` with no virtio disk, then the shell runs `cat /proc/partitions`, `mount -t fat16 sda2 /mnt`, `dmesg > /mnt/logs/dmesg-1.txt` and `eject sda`; the harness checks with mtools that the file holds `Limine transferred control`, that the FAT copies agree, `fsck.fat -n` when installed, and that the ESP's bytes, directory listing, kernel and initramfs are unchanged. `NVME_TEST=ON` (`--expect-nvme`, `tools/smoke/cases/nvme.py`, matrix cases 74 and 75, and 78 and 79 with `KERNEL_USB=off`; the kernel hook `test=nvme`, `KernelTest::StartNvmeTests`, chosen by `build.sh`) attaches QEMU's `nvme` controller (`-device nvme,serial=feather-nvme0,drive=nvme_disk`) over a per-run copy of `storage-controller.img` in `build/<arch>/storage-disks/`, built by `tools/create_storage_disks.py` (`python3 tools/create_storage_disks.py <folder>` makes it alone): an MBR disk of 82 MiB with `nvme0n1p1` FAT16 (32 MiB; `NVME.TXT` and the 1 MiB `BIG.BIN`), `nvme0n1p2` ext2, `nvme0n1p3` ext3 and `nvme0n1p4` ext4 (16 MiB each, the builders of `create_test_disk.py`) and 1 MiB free at the end. The boot log must show the controller line (`Storage: nvme0: 00:05.0 "QEMU NVMe Ctrl" fw ..., MSI-X, 1 I/O queue of 32, max transfer 128 KiB, volatile write cache`; the case refuses polling), `QEMU controller, writes allowed`, `nvme0n1: 83968 KiB, 512-byte sectors, rw` and the four volume lines. The hook mounts each volume read-only and reads a file, writes and reads the last sector, checks that transfers past the end are refused, moves 512 KiB in the free tail (several commands) and checks the disk's counters; the shell then does on each volume `mount -t <type> nvme0n1pN`, a write, a 1 MiB `cp` (from p1's `BIG.BIN`), `mkdir`, `mv`, `rm`, `sync`, `umount`, a second mount and `cat`, and last writes the ext4 volume right before `shutdown`, which must log `Storage: nvme0: shutdown complete`. The harness then checks the image with mtools (the FAT copies agree, every file byte for byte), `fsck.fat -n` when installed, and on p2 to p4 the clean flag, no journal recovery needed, `e2fsck -fn` and `debugfs cat`; the root must still be `vda1`. `AHCI_TEST=ON` (`--expect-ahci`, `tools/smoke/cases/ahci.py`, matrix cases 80 and 81; the kernel hook `test=ahci`, `KernelTest::StartAhciTests`, `KERN/AHCI:` lines) attaches `-device ich9-ahci,id=ahci` with an `ide-hd` on port 0 (`serial=feather-sata0`) over a per-run copy of the same `storage-controller.img` (in `build/<arch>/storage-disks/`) and an empty `ide-cd` on port 2. The boot log must show `Storage: ahci0: 00:05.0 6 ports, 32 command slots, MSI, 64-bit DMA` (the case refuses polling), `QEMU controller, writes allowed`, `Storage: AHCI 00:05.0 port 2: ATAPI device not supported`, `sda: AHCI 00:05.0 port 0, "QEMU HARDDISK" "feather-sata0", 83968 KiB, 512-byte sectors, write cache, rw` and the volume lines `sda1` to `sda4`. The hook and the shell run the same checks as `NVME_TEST` on `sda1` to `sda4` (the hook and the harness's checks are shared code: `Naming` in `nvme.py`, `ControllerTest` in `kernel_test.cpp`), and shutdown must log `Storage: sda: parked` (STANDBY IMMEDIATE). `NVME_NAMESPACES_TEST=ON` (`--expect-nvme-namespaces`, `tools/smoke/cases/nvme_namespaces.py`, matrix case 76 and 77; no kernel hook) attaches one `nvme,id=nvme0` controller with three `nvme-ns` namespaces over images from `BuildNamespaceDisks` in `tools/create_storage_disks.py`: nsid 1 `storage-ns4k.img` (a GPT disk in 4096-byte sectors from `create_usb_disks.BuildFourK`, `logical_block_size=4096`: ext4 as `nvme0n1p5`, FAT16 as `nvme0n1p6`), nsid 2 `storage-ns512.img` (a FAT16 superfloppy, mounted as `nvme0n2`) and nsid 3 `storage-nsms.img` (`ms=8`). The log must show both disks at their sector sizes, `Storage: nvme0n1p6: no supported filesystem` (the FAT16 core refuses 4096-byte sectors), `Storage: nvme0: namespace 3: LBA format with metadata not supported` and no `nvme0n3` (the case fails when QEMU ignored a property); the shell writes and copies on `nvme0n1p5` and writes on `nvme0n2`, and the host checks `e2fsck -fn`, `debugfs`, mtools, `fsck.fat -n` when installed, that the refused FAT16 partition and the metadata image are untouched. `STORAGE_POLICY_TEST=ON` with `KERNEL_STORAGE=ro` (`--expect-storage-policy`, `tools/smoke/cases/storage_policy.py`, matrix cases 82 and 83; the kernel hook `test=storage-policy`, `KernelTest::StartStoragePolicyTests`, `KERN/STORAGEPOLICY:` lines) boots an NVMe controller and an `ich9-ahci` (with the `ide-hd` and the `ide-cd` of `AHCI_TEST`), each over its own `storage-controller.img` copy, with `storage=ro`: both controller lines say `storage=ro, read-only`, `nvme0n1` and `sda` are `ro` in the boot log and in `/proc/partitions`, the hook finds each disk read-only by the policy, a read-write mount of each volume refuses `VFS::Create`, a `Disk::Write` is refused and the controller's `write_commands()` and `flush_commands()` counters stay 0 (`Namespace::Owner()` and the `Ahci::Port` driver reach them); in the shell each mount shows `ro` with the `VFS: <volume> is read-only by the storage policy` log line, `write` gives `Read-only file system` and `mount -o remount,rw` fails; on the host both images' SHA-256 are unchanged. `KERNEL_STORAGE=ro tools/check.sh --modes STORAGE_POLICY` runs it (the variable applies to every mode of that call). `DISK_BUS=virtio|nvme|nvme4k|ahci` (`--disk-bus`, `DiskArguments` in `tools/smoke/qemu.py`; any smoke run, `tools/matrix.py` clears it) decides how `featheros_disk` is attached: virtio-blk (the default), `-device nvme,serial=feather-root`, an `nvme-ns` of 4096-byte blocks (`nvme4k`) or an `ich9-ahci` with an `ide-hd` on port 0; a case can swap the image through `Case.DiskImage()` (`nvme4k` boots `storage-root-4k.img`, `BuildRootFourK` in `tools/create_storage_disks.py`: GPT in 4096-byte sectors, p1 a 127 MiB ext4 root with 4096-byte blocks, a 4 MiB journal, `/sbin/init` and the programs). `ROOT_DISK_TEST=ON` (`--expect-root-disk` with `--kernel-root` and `--kernel-storage` from `KERNEL_ROOT` and `KERNEL_STORAGE`, `tools/smoke/cases/root_disk.py`, matrix cases 86 to 99; no kernel hook) works out from the bus and the options which root the kernel must mount (the named partition, the named disk's lowest volume after `root=<name> is not a usable partition, using the default root`, `vda1` after `root=<name>: no disk <disk>, using the default root`, or the RAM root with no `vda` and no `root=`) and checks the `VFS: / is <name> <type>, <mode>` line, the `/etc` line, init (`/sbin/init` from the root on the first partition, else the initramfs's `/system/init`) and the shell. On a writable root the shell writes `/rootdisk.txt`, and after shutdown the host checks the root volume with `e2fsck -fn`, the journal state and `debugfs cat`; with `KERNEL_STORAGE=ro` (the root line says `read-only: the storage policy (storage=rw to write)` and `/etc` is the RAM copy) the write gives `Read-only file system` and the image's SHA-256 is unchanged; with the RAM root and a disk on NVMe the shell lists `/proc/partitions`, mounts `nvme0n1p1` at `/mnt` and unmounts it. The cases are `nvme_root` (`KERNEL_ROOT=nvme0n1p3`, the ext3 volume, no virtio disk), `ahci_root` (`sda1`), `nvme_4k_root` (`nvme0n1p1` of the 4K image), `nvme_root_readonly`, `nvme_no_default_root`, `missing_root_disk` (virtio only, `root=nvme0n1p3`) and `nvme_unusable_root` (`nvme0n1p9`). `GRAPHICS_TEST=ON` (`--expect-graphics`, `tools/smoke/cases/graphics.py`) is the only mode that reads the screen: it types `fbdemo 10`, and through QMP `screendump` (a PPM of 1024x768, read in Python; QEMU draws it with `-display none` on x86-64's VGA and AArch64's ramfb alike) checks, retrying each dump for up to 2 s, that (A) 1.5 s after `fbdemo: presented` the whole screen is exactly the pattern (the status bar's changing cells would show), (B) after `sendkey alt-f2` the second console's status bar is white at (1011,24) and the pattern is gone, (C) after `alt-f1` the whole screen is black (the acquired console is blanked when shown and nothing redraws it), (D) after typing `r` the second present is exactly the pattern again, and (E) after `fbdemo: done` and the prompt the first console's text is back (status bar white at (1011,24), padding black at (4,4)). Before `fbdemo` it runs `featherfall --selftest`, `featherfall --demo --seed 1` and `--seed 2` and requires `featherfall: selftest ok` and the two exact demo lines (the same on both architectures). It then types `fbdemo --keys 120` and sends keys through QMP `input-send-event` (one command per key transition, `SendQmpKeyEvent`), waiting up to 3 s for each expected `fbdemo: event` line (PS/2 on x86-64, virtio-input on AArch64): `a`, Shift+`a`, a key sent as three `down` events (press, two repeats, release), Ctrl+Z (queued, fbdemo goes on), `x` held while Alt+F2 switches away (releases of `x` and Alt, then `hidden`, the second console showing text, and no lines for the keys released there), Alt+F1 (`shown`, `presented`, no stray release, the pattern again), `p` followed by 100 `a` pairs (exactly 62 alternating key events once reading resumes, and `dropped` at least 1), and Ctrl+C (the Ctrl and `c` presses, `interrupted`, the text console back, and a following `echo ok` that runs without stray characters).
